## Part III. Sc-Fredholm theory and the implicit function theorem (HWZ Chapter 3, §§3.1–3.8)

Chapter 3 is the reason for the first two chapters: it defines the sections of strong bundles to which an implicit function theorem applies, the *sc-Fredholm sections*, and proves that theorem. The engine is a fixed point problem with finitely many parameters, $w = B(a, w)$, $a \in \widetilde C \subseteq \mathbb R^n$, $w \in W$, where $B(a, \cdot)$ contracts with an arbitrarily small constant near $0$ *on every level* $W_m$ of the scale. Its solution $w = \delta(a)$ is the *solution germ*; the germ implicit function theorem (Theorem 3.3.6 (preprint 3.33)) says that $\delta$ is as regular as $B$. Around it the chapter builds: germs and their calculus (§III.1), sections of strong bundles in charts (§III.2), fillers (§§III.3–III.4), basic germs and their linearization (§III.5), sc-Fredholm germs and sections with the stability theorem 3.15 (§III.6); the germ implicit function theorem itself (§§III.7–III.11); local regularity and compactness, Theorems 3.5.1 and 3.1.13 (preprint 3.40 and 3.12) (§§III.12–III.13); the finite-dimensional geometry of good position and the inverse and implicit function theorems in partial quadrants, which the formalization proved first and the rest uses (§§III.14–III.17); the basic class and local solvability (§§III.18–III.20); the linearization of an sc-Fredholm section at a zero, good position, and the graph description of the solution set of Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54) (§§III.21–III.23); the tangent germ property, its invariance, and the manifold structure of solution sets (§III.24); and Lemmas 3.7.2–3.7.4 (preprint 3.59–3.61) of the basic germ criterion (§III.25). Sections III.27–III.39 were added after the part was first assembled, for the Lean written since: tameness under sc-diffeomorphisms (§III.27), Corollary 3.4.3 (preprint 3.39) (§III.28), good complements whose projection maps the quadrant into itself and local solvability with such a complement (§III.29), the index in Proposition 3.6.5 (preprint 3.52) (§III.30), the linearization at a zero in charts and its chart independence (§III.31), Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) at the nearby zeros (§§III.32–III.33), Theorem 3.6.8 (2) (preprint 3.54) (§§III.34–III.36), the implicit function theorems 3.54, 3.23, 3.19, 3.24, 3.56, 3.57 assembled (§III.37), the basic germ criterion, Theorem 3.7.1 (preprint 3.58) (§III.38), and what this addendum taught us (§III.39). Sections III.40–III.46 were added in a second addendum: Theorem 3.1.21 (preprint 3.18) (§III.40); Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) with the text's hypotheses, by an implicit function theorem at the nearby zero (§§III.41–III.43); the dimension of the solution set as the Fredholm index and its local constancy (§III.44); the kernel as tangent space, good position at interior zeros, and the filler condition of §III.33 as a theorem (§III.45); and what the second addendum taught us (§III.46). Section III.47 repairs a tameness hypothesis, and §III.48 records the switch of Definition 3.1.11 (preprint 3.11) to the text's germ reading; the passages above that use the filling or the sc⁺-section have been rewritten for it.

**What is proved, and what is not.** The germ implicit function theorem is proved *with the text's hypotheses*: no hypothesis had to be added to Theorems 3.3.2–3.3.7 (preprint 3.29–3.34) and Theorem 3.35 (preprint; not in the 2021 edition). What the formalization adds is precision about *where* the solution germ is regular. A germ is formalized as a fixed sequence of sets, and the proof shows that $\delta$ is sc$^k$ on the germ shifted by $s_k = 2^k - 1$ levels; "if $f$ is sc-smooth so is its solution germ" therefore holds for every $k$ on a germ depending on $k$ (§III.11). Elsewhere hypotheses had to be added: the good complement must be *closed* in Lemma 3.6.9 (preprint 3.55) and in Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25); and Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) needs two regularity conditions on its data, each of which the text's proof asserts and each of which fails in an example (§III.24) — for solution sets both are theorems (§§III.29, III.35–III.36). These are the only ones. Definition 3.1.11 (preprint 3.11) is formalized as the text writes it, with the filling and the sc⁺-section sc-smooth *germs* whose levels may shrink, and every theorem built on it holds in that reading with the text's conclusions (§§III.6, III.48); the first formalization's whole-chart reading was narrower. Definition 6.2.1 (preprint 6.1) (the index of the germ at a smooth point) and Proposition 6.2.2 (preprint 6.2) (its constancy along sc-smooth paths), from Chapter 6 of the text, are proved with the text's hypotheses — tame germ data, no bump functions, the locally defined sc⁺-sections of Definition 6.2.1 (preprint 6.1) — for paths on $\mathbb R$, by the text's device run on the restriction of the bundle to a chart domain; the first proof, with global sections, assumed bump functions (§III.49). The implicit function theorems of §§III.23–III.45 were first stated with a tameness hypothesis that no strong bundle over a nonempty base satisfies, and were vacuous; they now assume germ data and good position in tame charts, as the text does (§III.47). Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) was first proved only under condition (3) of Definition 3.1.4 (preprint 3.4) at the nearby zeros (§III.33); it is now proved with the text's hypotheses, by an argument that is not the text's (§§III.41–III.43), and the condition is a theorem (§III.45). The local solvability theorems 3.43 and 3.47 are proved in full, their clause (1) ("the solution map is an sc-smooth germ") in the level-wise sense with $k$-dependent neighbourhoods (§III.20). Of Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54) the graph description of the solution set near a zero in good position is §III.23. Its conclusion (2), the manifold structure, and the global Theorems 3.6.10, 3.6.11 (preprint 3.56, 3.57) and Corollary 3.1.27 (preprint 3.24) are proved with the text's hypotheses (§§III.34–III.37); its conclusion (1), good position at the nearby zeros, first under the added filler condition (§III.33), then with the text's hypotheses (§III.43), and with it the local Theorems 3.6.8, 3.1.26 and 3.1.22 (preprint 3.54, 3.23 and 3.19) (§§III.37, III.43), with the dimension of the solution set equal to the Fredholm index (§III.44). Theorem 3.1.21 (preprint 3.18) is §III.40. The basic germ criterion (Theorem 3.7.1 (preprint 3.58)) is proved in the coordinates of its proof (§III.38), with Lemmas 3.7.2–3.7.4 (preprint 3.59–3.61) (§III.25).

**Seven structural choices.** (1) Germs are fixed sequences of sets, and the calculus the text uses on them silently is proved (§III.1). (2) Contraction germs carry explicit contraction regions instead of the text's balls, because the regions of the iterated germ $f^{(1)}$ must be unbounded in the tangent directions (§III.7). (3) "A strong bundle chart" in the definitions of §3.1 is a chart *compatible* with the atlas (§III.2). (4) Tameness, which the text assumes throughout, is used only in Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54), and only for the chart of the germ data at the zero: through Proposition 2.5.3 (preprint 2.35), $(1 - Dr(0))E \subseteq E_0$, and through the tameness of the model of the chart $\psi \circ \varphi$ (§III.27). The theorems assume germ data and good position in tame charts, as Definitions 3.6.3, 3.6.4 and 3.6.6 (preprint 3.50, 3.51 and 3.53) do; the first formalization assumed every compatible chart tame, which cannot be met (§III.47). (5) The coordinate change of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) is linear, which makes the transport of solution sets in §3.5 linear algebra. (6) Classical regularity in the appendix is Mathlib's $C^k$ within a set, and the iterations of the text's Lemmas 3.8.20 and 3.8.26 (preprint 3.76 and 3.82) are replaced by bootstrapping a derivative formula. (7) In Definition 3.1.11 (preprint 3.11) the filling $g$ and the sc⁺-section $s$ are germs on germs of neighbourhoods of their own, and the basic germ is shrunk to the germ $G''$ of the points that $\psi^{-1}$ sends into both, so that the nearby zeros produced by the implicit function theorem are points where $g$ and $s$ are sc¹ (§§III.6, III.48).

Throughout, $P$ and $Q$ are finite-dimensional normed spaces with the constant sc-structure (the text's $\mathbb R^n$ and $\mathbb R^N$), $\widetilde C \subseteq P$ a set containing $0$, convex, with nonempty interior (the text's $[0, \infty)^k \oplus \mathbb R^{n-k}$), $W$ an sc-Banach space, $E = P \oplus W$ with levels $P \times W_m$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ScBanach.paramProd⟧. Because all levels of $P$ coincide, the condition "$\widetilde C \times W$ is quadrant-like" is just "$0 \in \widetilde C$, convex, nonempty interior" ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · isQuadrantLike_const_iff⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · IsQuadrantLike.paramProd⟧.

### III.1 Germs and their calculus (Definitions 3.1.1, 3.1.2 (preprint 3.1, 3.2))

**Definition 3.1.1 (preprint 3.1).** An *sc-germ of neighbourhoods* around $0 \in C$ is a family of sets $U_m \subseteq E_m$, each of the form $O \cap C_m$ with $O$ open in $E_m$, containing $0$, and nested along the inclusions: $\iota U_{m+1} \subseteq U_m$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScNbhdGerm⟧. Its *tangent germ* has levels $U_{m+1} \times E_m$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScNbhdGerm.tangent⟧. The text's example, a relatively open $U \ni 0$ with $U_m = U \cap E_m$, is `ofSet` ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScNbhdGerm.ofSet⟧. The germ calculus needs one property of $C$: every level $C_m$ is convex with nonempty interior and $0 \in C$ ("quadrant-like") ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsQuadrantLike⟧. Partial quadrants have it ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsPartialQuadrant.isQuadrantLike⟧, and it passes to $TC$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsQuadrantLike.tangentSet⟧. It makes every $U_m$ a set of unique differentiability ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScNbhdGerm.uniqueDiffWithinAt⟧, the intersection of an open and a convex set ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScNbhdGerm.relOpenConvex⟧.

A germ is a *fixed* sequence of sets. The text says "the size of the sets $U_m$ does not matter", i.e. it reads germs up to shrinking; the formalization cannot, and must say on which sequence each statement holds. One trivial remark becomes important in §III.9: the re-indexed sequence $(U_{m+1})_m$ is in general not a germ, since $U_{m+1}$ is relatively open in $C_{m+1}$ and not in $C_m$; it is one when all levels coincide, i.e. for germs in the finite-dimensional parameter space $P$.

**Definition 3.1.2 (preprint 3.2).** $f$ is an *sc⁰-germ* on $G = (U_m)$ if $f(U_m) \subseteq F_m$ and the level map $f_m : U_m \to F_m$ is continuous for every $m$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc0Germ⟧. It is an *sc¹-germ with derivative family $D$* if it is sc⁰, differentiable at every $x \in U_1$ within $U_1$ in the sense of Definition 1.1.15 (preprint 1.14) (increments in $E_1$, remainder measured in $F_0$) with derivative $D(x)$, and its tangent map $(x, h) \mapsto (f(x), D(x)h)$ is an sc⁰-germ on the tangent germ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc1GermWith⟧; an sc¹-germ if some such $D$ exists ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc1Germ⟧, in which case a derivative family is chosen ⟦Chapter3/Germ · scDerivGerm⟧ and gives the tangent germ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · tangentMapGerm⟧. On quadrant-like $C$ the derivative is unique on $U_1$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc1GermWith.eq_of_uniqueDiffWithinAt⟧, so the choice does not matter there ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc1Germ.tangentMapGerm_eqOn⟧. Then sc^k is defined by recursion — sc⁰ for $k = 0$, and sc¹ with sc^k tangent germ for $k + 1$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSckGerm⟧ — and sc-smooth means sc^k for all $k$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsScSmoothGerm⟧. These are the notions of Part I, §I.11, with $U \cap E_m$ replaced by $U_m$, and the bridge is exact: an sc^k map on a relatively open $U \ni 0$ is an sc^k-germ on `ofSet U` ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSck.isSckGerm⟧ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsScSmooth.isScSmoothGerm⟧.

**The calculus of germs.** The text restricts, composes and pairs germs without comment. All of it is proved, with the proofs of Part I, §§I.11–I.12. (1) sc^k-germs depend only on the values on $U_0$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSckGerm.congr⟧ and restrict to smaller germs ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSckGerm.mono⟧ — by induction on $k$, using that the tangent germs of the smaller germ and of the larger one agree on the smaller tangent germ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc1Germ.tangentMapGerm_mono⟧. (2) *The chain rule.* A composition $g \circ f$ makes sense level by level only if $f$ maps $U_m$ into the domain $V_m$ of $g$ for every $m$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScNbhdGerm.MapsToGerm⟧; then sc⁰-germs compose ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc0Germ.comp⟧, the derivative of $g \circ f$ at $x \in U_1$ is $Dg(f(x)) \circ Df(x)$ — proved as in §I.12 with the mean value inequality along segments in $V_1$ and the uniform smallness of $Dg(y) - Dg(y_0)$ on compact sets ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc1GermWith.hasFDerivWithinAt_comp⟧ — so $g \circ f$ is sc¹ with $T(g \circ f) = Tg \circ Tf$ on the tangent germ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc1GermWith.comp⟧ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSc1Germ.comp⟧, and by induction on $k$ it is sc^k ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSckGerm.comp⟧ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsScSmoothGerm.comp⟧. (3) An sc-operator $T$ is an sc¹-germ with derivative $T$ itself ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScOperator.isSc1GermWith⟧, its tangent map is the sc-operator $(T, T)$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScOperator.tangentOp⟧, and so it is an sc-smooth germ on every germ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScOperator.isSckGerm⟧ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · ScOperator.isScSmoothGerm⟧; composing with one on the outside preserves sc^k ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSckGerm.operator_comp⟧. (4) The pairing $(f, g)$ of two sc^k-germs is an sc^k-germ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · pairMap⟧ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · IsSckGerm.pair⟧, through the reshuffle $TF \oplus TG \cong T(F \oplus G)$ ⟦Chapter3/Germ · tangentProdComm⟧. (5) Where the text says "near $0$" it shrinks germs: for an sc⁰-germ $f$ with $f(0) = 0$ and a germ $G'$ the sets $\{x \in U_m : f_m(x) \in U'_m\}$ form the largest sub-germ on which $f$ maps into $G'$ ⟦Chapter3/GermFilling · ScNbhdGerm.preimage⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermFilling · ScNbhdGerm.preimage_mapsToGerm⟧.

**Propositions 1.2.1–1.2.3 (preprint 1.16–1.18) for germs.** The text applies these to germs ("recalling Proposition 1.17" in Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9); Lemma 3.3.4 (preprint 3.31); Corollary 3.3.7 (preprint 3.34)) but states them for relatively open sets. For an sc¹-germ with derivative family $D$ and $x \in U_{m+1}$, $D(x)$ restricts to a bounded level operator $E_m \to F_m$ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSc1GermWith.exists_levelDeriv⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSc1GermWith.toBase_levelDeriv⟧, the level-$m$ tangent map is $(x, h) \mapsto (f_{m+1}(x), D_m(x)h)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSc1GermWith.levelMap_tangentMapWith⟧, jointly continuous ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSc1GermWith.continuousOn_levelDeriv⟧ and continuous in $x$ in the operator norm (the compact-open lemma of §I.13) ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSc1GermWith.continuousOn_levelDeriv_clm⟧; and $f : U_{m+1} \to F_m$ is differentiable within $U_{m+1}$ with derivative $D_m(x)$, by the integral representation $f(x + k) - f(x) = \int_0^1 D(x + \tau k)k\,d\tau$ in $F_m$ (Proposition 1.2.1 (preprint 1.16)) ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSc1GermWith.hasFDerivWithinAt_level⟧. The shifted sequence $(U_{m+1})_m$ is a germ in $E^1$ around $0 \in C^1$ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · ScNbhdGerm.shift⟧, and an sc^k-germ on $G$ is an sc^k-germ $G^1 \to F^1$ (Proposition 1.2.2 (preprint 1.17)) ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSc0Germ.shift⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSc1GermWith.shift⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSckGerm.shift⟧. An sc^k-germ is $C^k$ from $U_{m+k}$ to $F_m$ (Proposition 1.2.3 (preprint 1.18)), by the proof of §I.16 with $U_m$ for $U \cap E_m$ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSckGerm.contDiffOn_levels'⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSckGerm.contDiffOn_levels⟧. sc^k depends on a germ only through its sets ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSckGerm.of_U_eq⟧.

### III.2 Sections of strong bundles in charts (Definition 3.1.3 (preprint 3.3))

Let $P : Y \to X$ be a strong bundle over an M-polyfold (Part II, §II.19). A strong bundle chart $(\Phi, P^{-1}(V), K, U \triangleleft F)$ has a model $K = R(U \triangleleft F)$ for a strong bundle retraction $R$. Its base and fibre parts are read off: $r(u) = (R(u, 0))_1$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.r⟧, $\rho(u)h = (R(u, h))_2$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.ρ⟧, and $R(u, h) = (r(u), \rho(u)h)$ because the base component of a strong bundle map does not depend on the fibre variable ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.apply_eq⟧. Comparing components of $R \circ R = R$ gives $r \circ r = r$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.r_r⟧ and $\rho(r(u))\rho(u) = \rho(u)$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.ρ_r_ρ⟧; for $x \in O = r(U)$, $r(x) = x$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.r_eq_self⟧ and $\rho(x)$ is a projection ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.ρ_ρ⟧; $R$ fixes the points of $K$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.apply_eq_self_of_mem_K⟧, so $\rho(x)h = h$ for $(x, h) \in K$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.ρ_eq_self_of_mem_K⟧. $\rho(u)$ is linear by Definition 2.6.1 (preprint 2.47) and continuous because $R$ is sc⁰ on level $0$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.ρ_continuous⟧; it is a bounded operator ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.ρCLM⟧ — the text's "$\rho(u) : F \to F$ is a bounded linear operator", without comment.

*Principal parts and the section algebra.* For a section $f$ (a map with $P \circ f = \mathrm{id}$), $\Phi \circ f \circ \varphi^{-1}(z) = (z, \mathbf f(z))$; $\mathbf f$ is its *principal part* ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.principal⟧ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.apply_section⟧. The fibrewise sum $f + s$ is defined with the vector space structure of the fibres $P^{-1}(x)$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · sectionAdd⟧ and is a section ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsSection.sectionAdd⟧. Since $\Phi$ is linear on the fibres ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.snd_add⟧ and maps the zero of $P^{-1}(x)$ to $(\varphi(x), 0)$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.snd_fiberZero⟧, the principal part of $f + s$ is $\mathbf f + \mathbf s$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.principal_sectionAdd⟧ and $f(x) = 0$ iff $\mathbf f(\varphi(x)) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.isZeroAt_iff⟧ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsZeroAt⟧. The fibres of $K$ over $O$ are linear: $(u, a), (u, b) \in K$ give $(u, a + b) \in K$, because membership is $\rho(u)a = a$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.add_mem_K⟧. *The sum rule*, used silently in "$f + s$ is an sc-smooth section", is pairing (Part I) followed by the addition sc-operator $F \oplus F \to F$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · ScBanach.addOp⟧ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsScSmooth.add⟧ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsScSmooth.sub⟧ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsScSmooth.neg⟧; a map sc-smooth into $F^1$ is sc-smooth into $F$ (compose with the inclusion) ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsScSmooth.toBase_shift⟧.

*Which charts.* "A strong bundle chart" in Definitions 3.1.3, 3.1.6 and 3.1.11 (preprint 3.3, 3.6 and 3.11) must mean a chart *compatible* with the atlas of $P$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsChart⟧ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.isChart_of_mem⟧: a chart of the atlas need not be centred at the point in question, and the notions must not depend on the atlas within its class. A compatible chart is a chart of the M-polyfold $Y[0]$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsChart.toMChart⟧. The price is that representatives of sections must be sc-smooth in every compatible chart. For an sc-smooth section, $z \mapsto (z, \mathbf f(z))$ is sc-smooth $O \to K$ by Definition 2.3.8 (preprint 2.14) as it stands ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScSmoothSection.isScSmoothRetractMap⟧. For an sc⁺-section, sc-smooth into $Y[1]$, the principal part takes values in $F_1$ in every compatible chart ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScPlusSection.principal_mem_range⟧, and $z \mapsto (z, \mathbf s_1(z))$ is sc-smooth $O \to K[1]$: in a chart $\Phi'$ of the atlas this is the definition, and in the given chart it is the composition with the change of chart $(\Phi \circ \Phi'^{-1})[1]$ on $K[1]$, glued over the atlas by locality ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScPlusSection.isScSmoothRetractMap_one⟧. This is precisely the compatibility on $[1]$ of Definition 2.6.4 (preprint 2.50), used here for the first time.

**Definition 3.1.3 (preprint 3.3).** $(f, x_0)$ is an *sc-smooth section germ* if $x_0$ is smooth and in some compatible strong bundle chart with $\varphi(x_0) = 0$ the map $\mathbf f \circ r$ is an sc-smooth germ on some sc-germ of neighbourhoods $G$ of $0$ with $G_0 \subseteq U$, whose levels may shrink with $m$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScSmoothSectionGerm⟧ — the text's germ reading, as for Definition 3.1.11 (preprint 3.11) (§III.48); until the fourth addendum the germ was the chart's $U \cap E_m$, which is stricter (§III.48). The text's "continuous section on a neighbourhood $V$" is the section on the chart domain; near $x_0$ continuity is a consequence. An sc-smooth section is an sc-smooth section germ at every smooth point with a centred chart: its representative composed with $r$ is sc-smooth on the relatively open $U$, and §III.1's bridge applies on the germ $U \cap E_m$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScSmoothSection.isScSmoothSectionGerm⟧. No theorem assumes the definition.

### III.3 Fillers (Definitions 3.1.4, 3.1.6 (preprint 3.4, 3.6); Lemmas 3.1.19, 3.1.20 (preprint 3.16, 3.17); Remark 3.6.2 (preprint 3.49))

**Definition 3.1.4 (preprint 3.4).** For the principal parts $f, g : E_0 \to F_0$ (with $O = r(U)$, $K = R(U \triangleleft F)$, $0 \in U$), $g$ *fills* $f$ if (1) $f = g$ on $O$ near $0$; (2) a point $y \in U$ near $0$ with $g(y) = \rho(r(y))g(y)$ lies in $O$; (3) the map $y \mapsto [1 - \rho(r(y))]g(y)$ is sc¹ on $U$, and its derivative at $0$ restricts to a topological linear isomorphism $\ker Dr(0) \to \ker\rho(0)$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · IsFillerOf⟧. The text's condition (3) presupposes the linearization; requiring the map to be sc¹ loses nothing, since it is sc-smooth when $g$ and $R$ are. The sc-smoothness of $g$ itself is added in Definition 3.1.6 (preprint 3.6). **Definition 3.1.6 (preprint 3.6)**: a *filled version* of $\mathbf f$ is a germ $(g, 0)$, and it is formalized as one, a *filling germ* ⟦Chapter3/GermFilling · IsFillingGerm⟧: $g$ is an sc-smooth germ (§III.1) on a germ $G$ with $G_0 \subseteq U$, whose levels may shrink with $m$; (1) and (2) hold near $0$; and in (3) the fill defect $k(y) = [1 - \rho(r(y))]g(y)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermFilling · fillDefect⟧ is an sc¹-germ on $G$ with a derivative family $Dk$ whose value $Dk(0)$ — unique (§III.1) — restricts to a topological isomorphism $\ker Dr(0) \to \ker\rho(0)$. The first formalization took a filler sc-smooth on the whole $U$ of the chart ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsFilledVersion⟧, on the ground that a chart can always be restricted to $U \cap W$. That is the special case $G = $ `ofSet U` ⟦Chapter3/GermFillingChart · IsFilledVersion.isFillingGerm⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermFillingChart · IsFillingGerm.isFilledVersion⟧, and it is narrower: restricting a chart on level $0$ never makes the levels shrink, and there are sc-smooth germs with shrinking levels that are sc-smooth on no neighbourhood of $0$ ⟦Chapter3/GermVsChart · exists_isScSmoothGerm_not_isScSmooth⟧ (§III.48). The lemmas below about fillers on $U$ are the whole-chart ones; their germ forms, which the canonical notions use, are cited next to them.

**Lemma 3.1.19 (preprint 3.16).** For a section $s$ of $K \to O$ (that is, $(x, s(x)) \in K$ for $x \in O$), the extension $\tilde s = s \circ r$ satisfies $\tilde s = s$ on $O$ (as $r(x) = x$ there), and $R(r(u), s(r(u))) = (r(u), s(r(u)))$, i.e. $\rho(r(u))\tilde s(u) = \tilde s(u)$, because $s(r(u))$ is a point of $K$ and $R$ fixes $K$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · lemma_3_1_19⟧. The text's computation uses $s(x) \in K$ without saying so; it is the hypothesis that $s$ is a section. **Lemma 3.1.20 (preprint 3.17).** If $g$ fills $f$ and $s$ is a section of $K \to O$, then $g + s \circ r$ fills $f + s$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · lemma_3_1_20⟧: (1) on $O$, $g + s \circ r = f + s$; (2) by Lemma 3.1.19 (preprint 3.16), $(g + \tilde s)(y) = \rho(r(y))(g + \tilde s)(y)$ is equivalent to $g(y) = \rho(r(y))g(y)$; (3) by the same identity $[1 - \rho(r(y))](g + \tilde s)(y) = [1 - \rho(r(y))]g(y)$ on $U$, so the two maps of (3) coincide on $U$ and have the same derivative at $0$ (derivatives on $U_1$ are unique). *For a filling germ* $g$ on $G$ and $s$ sc-smooth on $U$ the proof is the same: the identity of (3) holds on $U$, hence on $G_0$, so the derivative family $Dk$ of the fill defect of $g$ is one of $g + s \circ r$, a filling germ on the same $G$ ⟦Chapter3/GermCalculus · IsFillingGerm.add_section⟧; for $s$ itself a germ on $G_s$ the filling lives on $G \cap r^{-1}(G_s)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermCalculus · IsFillingGerm.add_section_germ⟧. The regularity statements of both lemmas — $\tilde s$ sc⁺, $g + \tilde s$ sc-smooth — are proved where they are used, in Theorem 3.1.18 (preprint 3.15) (§III.6). Condition (1) only involves $f$ on $O$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsFillerOf.congr_left⟧.

**Remark 3.6.2 (preprint 3.49).** On a smaller neighbourhood $W$ of $0$ condition (1) holds on all of $O \cap W$ and (2) for all $y \in U \cap W$: intersect the two neighbourhoods given by (1) and (2) ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · IsFillerOf.remark_3_6_2⟧.

### III.4 The linearization of a filler (Proposition 3.1.5 (preprint 3.5))

The text derives, "by linearization at $y = 0$", the matrix of $Dg(0)$ in the splittings $E = Dr(0)E \oplus \ker Dr(0)$, $F = \rho(0)F \oplus \ker\rho(0)$, and from it Proposition 3.1.5 (preprint 3.5). The formal proof has two halves: the derivative identities, and the linear algebra.

*The derivative identities.* $r$ is sc-smooth on $U$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · StrongRetr.r_isScSmooth⟧ with $r(0) = 0$ since $0 \in O$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · StrongRetr.r_zero⟧, and the chain rule applied to $r \circ r = r$ makes $Dr(0)$ idempotent ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · StrongRetr.scDeriv_r_idem⟧; $\rho(0)$ is idempotent ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · StrongRetr.ρCLM_idem⟧. The fibre part $(u, k) \mapsto \rho(u)k$ is sc¹ on $U \oplus F$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · StrongRetr.fiber_isSc1⟧, and its derivative at $(0, 0)$ is $(h, k) \mapsto \rho(0)k$: in the fibre direction because $k \mapsto \rho(0)k$ is linear, in the base direction because $\rho(u)0 = 0$; both directional derivatives are computed on the dense level $1$ and extended by continuity ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · StrongRetr.scDeriv_fiber_zero⟧. By the chain rule for $(u, k) \mapsto \rho(u)k$ composed with $y \mapsto (r(y), g(y))$ at the point $(r(0), g(0)) = (0, 0)$ — this is where $g(0) = 0$ enters — the derivative of $y \mapsto [1 - \rho(r(y))]g(y)$ at $0$ is $(1 - \rho(0))Dg(0)$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · IsFillerOf.scDeriv_sub_eq⟧ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · scDeriv_comp_apply'⟧. Next, $[1 - \rho(r(y))]g(r(y)) = 0$ near $0$, from $g = f$ on $O$ and $\rho(x)f(x) = f(x)$ on $O$ ($f$ a section of $K$) and the continuity of $r$; linearizing gives $(1 - \rho(0))Dg(0)Dr(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · IsFillerOf.deriv_comp_r_eq_zero⟧, i.e. $\rho(0)Dg(0)Dr(0) = Dg(0)Dr(0)$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · IsFillerOf.ρ_deriv_comp_r⟧. Condition (3) becomes: $(1 - \rho(0))Dg(0)$ restricts to a topological isomorphism $\ker Dr(0) \to \ker\rho(0)$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · IsFillerOf.exists_kerEquiv⟧. Finally $f \circ r = g \circ r$ near $0$, so $D(f \circ r)(0) = Dg(0)Dr(0)$ — the text's "$Tg(0) = Tf(0)$ on $T_0O$" ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · IsFillerOf.scDeriv_section_comp_r⟧.

*The linear algebra.* Let $p$, $q$ be bounded idempotents on Banach spaces $E$, $F$ and $L : E \to F$ bounded with $(1 - q)Lp = 0$ and $(1 - q)L : \ker p \to \ker q$ a topological isomorphism $C$. Then $A := L|_{\mathrm{range}\,p} : \mathrm{range}\,p \to \mathrm{range}\,q$ is well defined ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict⟧. Two facts carry everything: if $(1 - q)Lw = 0$ then $w \in \mathrm{range}\,p$ (injectivity of $C$, applied to the $\ker p$-component of $w$) ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict_mem_range_of⟧; and $(1 - q)Lz = y$ is solvable in $\ker p$ for $y \in \ker q$ (surjectivity of $C$) ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict_exists_of⟧. From them: $A$ is onto iff $L$ is ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict_surjective_iff⟧; the inclusion $\ker A \to \ker L$ is a bijection — the text's $\ker Dg(0) = \ker Tf(0) \oplus \{0\}$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict_kerEquiv⟧; $\mathrm{range}\,A = \mathrm{range}\,L \cap \mathrm{range}\,q$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict_mem_range_iff⟧ and $\mathrm{range}\,q/\mathrm{range}\,A \cong F/\mathrm{range}\,L$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict_cokerEquiv⟧. So the indices are equal as $\dim\ker - \dim\mathrm{coker}$ with no finiteness assumption ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fredholmIndex_fillerRestrict⟧. Closedness of the ranges transfers both ways: $\mathrm{range}\,A$ is closed in $\mathrm{range}\,q$ when $\mathrm{range}\,L$ is ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict_isClosed_range_of⟧, and conversely $\mathrm{range}\,L$ is the preimage of $\mathrm{range}\,A$ under the continuous $M(y) = y - LC^{-1}(1 - q)y$ ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · isClosed_range_of_fillerRestrict⟧ — this is where "topological" in (3) is used. Hence $A$ is Fredholm iff $L$ is ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · fillerRestrict_isFredholm_iff⟧, and the three statements together ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearAlgebra · proposition_3_1_5_preprint⟧. The text's proof factorizes $\begin{pmatrix}A & B\\ 0 & C\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}1 & BC^{-1}\\ 0 & 1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A & 0\\ 0 & C\end{pmatrix}$ and uses the additivity of the index; the isomorphisms of kernels and cokernels avoid both.

**Proposition 3.1.5 (preprint 3.5).** For a filler $g$ of the section $f$ of $K \to O$, $g$ sc-smooth with $g(0) = 0$: with $p = Dr(0)$, $q = \rho(0)$, $L = Dg(0)$ the hypotheses of the linear algebra hold by the identities above, and $Tf(0) := Dg(0)|_{Dr(0)E} : Dr(0)E \to \rho(0)F$ is onto iff $Dg(0)$ is, Fredholm iff $Dg(0)$ is, with equal index ⟦Chapter3/FillerLinearization · IsFillerOf.proposition_3_1_5⟧. (The text writes the target of the lower right entry as "$(1 - D\rho(0))F$"; it is $(1 - \rho(0))F$.) *For a filling germ* (§III.3) the proof is the same with any derivative family $Dg$ of $g$ on any germ inside $U$: the fill defect is an sc-smooth germ on the germ of $g$, by the chain rule with the whole-chart outer map $R$ (§III.48) ⟦Chapter3/GermCalculus · fillDefect_isScSmoothGerm⟧, and the value at $0$ of its derivative family is $(1 - \rho(0))Dg(0)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermFillerLinearization · fillDefect_deriv_zero_eq_rho⟧, which replaces `scDeriv_sub_eq`; so $Tf(0) = Dg(0)|_{Dr(0)E}$ is onto, Fredholm, of the index of $Dg(0)$ iff $Dg(0)$ is ⟦Chapter3/GermFillerLinearization · IsFillingGerm.proposition_3_1_5⟧. $Dg(0)$ does not depend on the family or the germ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · IsSc1GermWith.deriv_zero_eq⟧.

### III.5 Basic germs, Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9) and Proposition 3.1.10 (preprint 3.10)

**Definition 3.1.7 (preprint 3.7) (and 3.37).** $f : P \times W_0 \to Q \times W_0$ is a *basic germ* on a germ $G$ around $0$ in $\widetilde C \oplus W$, with contraction map $B$, if $f$ is an sc-smooth germ with $f(0) = 0$ whose $W$-component is $w - B(a, w)$ on $U_0$, and $B$ is an sc⁰-contraction germ in the sense of Definition 3.3.1 (preprint 3.28) (§III.7) ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsBasicGerm⟧. ($B = w - f_W$ is then itself an sc-smooth germ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · IsBasicGerm.B_isScSmoothGerm⟧.)

**Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9).** The text: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ and $m$, $|D_2B(a, w)\zeta|_m \le \varepsilon|\zeta|_m$ when $(a, w) \in E_{m+1}$ is close to $0$ in $E_m$; in particular $D_2B(0, 0) = 0$. Its proof: for $\zeta \in W_{m+1}$ the difference quotient of $t \mapsto B(a, w + t\zeta)$ is bounded by the contraction estimate, and the derivative is the limit (this needs $B : U_{m+1} \to W_m$ differentiable, Proposition 1.2.1 (preprint 1.16) for germs — "recalling Proposition 1.17" in the text); then density of $W_{m+1}$ in $W_m$. Only sc¹ is used. The formalization proves the three forms the chapter needs. (a) On level $m$ in the working form of §III.7, with the constant $\tfrac12$: at $(a, w) \in U_{m+1}$ with $a \in N(m+1) \cap \widetilde C$, $w \in O(m+1)$ and $\iota w \in O(m)$, $\|D_2B(a, w)\|_{L(W_m)} \le \tfrac12$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.norm_levelDeriv_snd_le⟧; a linear map of norm $\le \tfrac12$ then has no nonzero fixed point ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.eq_zero_of_norm_le_half⟧. (b) $D_2B(0, 0) = 0$, from the difference quotients along $t \mapsto (0, t\zeta)$, bounded by $\varepsilon|\zeta|$ for every $\varepsilon$, then density ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · IsSc0ContractionGerm.deriv_zero_snd⟧. (c) On level $0$ with any $\varepsilon$: $|D_2B(x)\zeta|_0 \le \varepsilon|\zeta|_0$ for $x \in U_1$ small *on level $1$* ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · IsSc0ContractionGerm.exists_norm_deriv_snd_le⟧ — the curve $t \mapsto x + t(0, \zeta)$, $\zeta \in W_1$, must stay in $U_1$, where $B$ is differentiable.

**Proposition 3.1.10 (preprint 3.10).** Let $\widetilde C$ be a partial quadrant, $h$ a basic germ, $s$ an sc¹-germ into $(Q \oplus W)^1$ (an sc⁺-germ) with derivative family $Ds$, and $f = h + \iota s$ sc¹ with derivative family $Df$. Then $Df(0)$ is an sc-operator, sc-Fredholm of index $\dim P - \dim Q$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · proposition_3_1_10⟧. *Proof.* The derivative of an sc¹-germ at the smooth point $0$ is an sc-operator, its level-$m$ map being the fibre component of the level-$m$ tangent map at $(0, \cdot)$ (the remark after Proposition 1.2.1 (preprint 1.16)) ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · IsSc1GermWith.isScOperator_deriv_zero⟧. The operator (32) of the text, $T_0(\delta a, \delta w) = (0, \delta w)$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · ScBanach.projWOp⟧, is sc-Fredholm with kernel $P \oplus 0$ and cokernel $Q \oplus 0$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · ScBanach.projWSplitting⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · ScBanach.isScFredholm_projWOp⟧, of index $\dim P - \dim Q$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · ScBanach.scIndex_projWOp⟧. The text says "$Df(0)$ is an sc⁺-perturbation of (32)"; the formal proof builds the sc⁺-operator $S = Df(0) - T_0$ level by level ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · proposition_3_1_10_scPlus⟧: its $Q$-component is sc⁺ because $Q$ has all levels equal; $\mathrm{pr}_W Dh(0)(\delta a, 0) = -D_1B(0)\delta a = -DB(0)(\delta a, 0)$ has values in $W_\infty$ ($(\delta a, 0)$ is smooth and $DB(0)$ an sc-operator) and its level maps $E_m \to W_{m+1}$ factor through $DB(0)_{m+1}$; $\iota Ds(0)$ is sc⁺ because $Ds(0)$ is an sc-operator into $(Q \oplus W)^1$; and $\mathrm{pr}_W Dh(0)(0, \delta w) = \delta w$ because $D_2B(0) = 0$ (Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9) (b)). The identities $Df(0) = Dh(0) + \iota Ds(0)$ and $\mathrm{pr}_W Dh(0) = \mathrm{pr}_W - DB(0)$ are derivative identities within $U_1$, extended to $E_0$ because two bounded operators agreeing on the dense $E_1$ are equal ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · ContinuousLinearMap.ext_of_comp_toBase_one⟧. Proposition 1.1.11 (preprint 1.10) with its index statement concludes ⟦Chapter1/ScFredholm · proposition_1_1_11_preprint⟧ ⟦Chapter1/FredholmIndex · proposition_1_1_11_index⟧. For a basic germ alone ($s = 0$) ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · ScOperator.zero⟧ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · proposition_3_1_10_basic⟧, with its level-$0$ consequence: $Dh(0)$ is Fredholm of index $\dim P - \dim Q$ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · proposition_3_1_10_basic_level_zero⟧.

The second statement of Proposition 3.1.10 (preprint 3.10) ($Df(a, w)$ Fredholm on every level $m$ for $(a, w)$ small) is proved on level $0$ for the germs of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38), at points of $U_1$ small on level $1$ (§III.19); on levels $m \ge 1$ it is not formalized.

### III.6 Sc-Fredholm germs and sections; Theorem 3.1.18 (preprint 3.15) (Definitions 3.1.11, 3.1.16, 3.6.3, 3.6.4 (preprint 3.11, 3.14, 3.50, 3.51))

**Definition 3.1.11 (preprint 3.11).** A *strong bundle isomorphism* $\Psi : U \triangleleft F \to U' \triangleleft F'$ is a pair of strong bundle maps (Definition 2.6.1 (preprint 2.47)) inverse to each other on $U \oplus F$ and $U' \oplus F'$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsStrongBundleIso⟧; its base map is $\psi$. A principal part $k : U \to F$ is *conjugated to a basic germ* if there are a strong bundle isomorphism with $\psi(0) = 0$ onto $U'$ relatively open in $\widetilde C \oplus W$ ($\widetilde C$ a partial quadrant of a finite-dimensional $P$), a basic germ $h$ on a germ $G$ with $G_0 \subseteq U'$, and $(\Psi(u, k(u)))_2 = h(\psi(u))$ for $u \in U$ near $0$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsConjugateToBasicGerm⟧; this depends only on $k$ on $U$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · IsConjugateToBasicGerm.congr⟧. $(f, x_0)$ is an *sc-Fredholm germ* if in a compatible chart centred at $x_0$ the principal part has a filling germ $g$ (§III.3), and there is a local sc⁺-section $s$ — an sc-smooth germ into $F^1$ on a germ $G_s$ with $(G_s)_0 \subseteq U$ ⟦Chapter3/GermFilling · IsScPlusGerm⟧ — with $\iota s(0) = g(0)$ such that $g - \iota s$ is conjugated to a basic germ ⟦Chapter3/GermFillingChart · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmGerm⟧. This is the text's reading: $g$ and $s$ are germs, on germs of neighbourhoods of their own whose levels may shrink with the level, while $\Psi$ is an isomorphism of the whole local bundles and the conjugation identity is asked near $0$ only, so it depends only on the germ of $g - \iota s$ ⟦Chapter3/GermFillingChart · IsConjugateToBasicGerm.congr_nhds⟧. The first formalization asked $g$ and $s$ to be sc-smooth on $U \cap E_m$ for every $m$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmGermWholeChart⟧. That notion implies the germ reading ⟦Chapter3/GermFillingChart · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmGermWholeChart.isScFredholmGerm⟧ and is exactly the germ reading with $g$ and $s$ sc-smooth on $U$ ⟦Chapter3/GermFillingChart · StrongBundle.isScFredholmGerm_iff_germG_wholeChart⟧; whether every germ datum can be traded for a whole-chart one is open (§III.48). Definitions 3.1.16, 3.6.3, 3.6.4, 3.6.6 (preprint 3.14, 3.50, 3.51, 3.53) and every theorem of this part built on them are stated for the germ reading; the whole-chart notions and theorems are kept under the names `…WholeChart`, `…_wholeChart`. The text's $C' = T\psi(0)C$, used in the proof of Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54), is not part of the data; it is a theorem (§III.23).

**Definition 3.1.16 (preprint 3.14).** $f$ is an *sc-Fredholm section* if it is sc-smooth, regularizing ($x \in X_m$ and $f(x) \in Y_{m,m+1}$ imply $x \in X_{m+1}$) and an sc-Fredholm germ at every smooth point ⟦Chapter3/TameGermsG · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmSection⟧. The text assumes a tame bundle; nothing in Definitions 3.1.3–3.1.16 (preprint 3.3–3.14) or in Theorem 3.1.18 (preprint 3.15) uses tameness, so it is not assumed. (Definitions 3.6.3, 3.6.4 (preprint 3.50, 3.51) restate 3.11, 3.14.) A zero of a regularizing section is a smooth point: the zero section lies in $Y_{m,m+1}$ over $X_m$ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · StrongBundle.fiberZero_mem_doubleLevel⟧, so a zero on level $m$ is on level $m + 1$, and by induction on every level ⟦Chapter3/TameGermsG · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmSection.mem_smoothSet_of_isZeroAt⟧.

**Theorem 3.1.18 (preprint 3.15).** If $f$ is an sc-Fredholm section and $s$ an sc⁺-section, then $f + s$ is an sc-Fredholm section ⟦Chapter3/FredholmSectionsG · theorem_3_1_18_preprint⟧. *Proof.* The text says "$f + s$ is an sc-smooth section which is also regularizing" and checks the germ property; each clause is a proof.

*Sc-smooth.* In a chart the representative of $f + s$ is $z \mapsto (z, \mathbf f(z) + \mathbf s(z))$. The map $z \mapsto (z, \mathbf f(z))$ is sc-smooth $O \to K$, $z \mapsto (z, \mathbf s_1(z))$ is sc-smooth $O \to K[1]$ (§III.2), and composing the latter with $K[1] \subseteq K$ and adding fibre components (sum rule) gives an sc-smooth map with values in $K$, since its fibres are linear ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScSmoothSection.add_isScSmoothRetractMap⟧; continuity of $f + s$ on each chart domain is read off the same representative ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.continuousOn_sectionAdd⟧ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScPlusSection.continuousOn_source⟧. So $f + s$ is sc-smooth ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScSmoothSection.sectionAdd⟧.

*Regularizing.* For $x \in X_m$, the representative of $s$ maps $O_m$ into $K[1]_m$, so $\mathbf s(\varphi(x)) \in F_{m+1}$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScPlusSection.snd_mem_range⟧. If $(f + s)(x) \in Y_{m,m+1}$ then $\mathbf f(\varphi(x)) = (\mathbf f + \mathbf s)(\varphi(x)) - \mathbf s(\varphi(x)) \in F_{m+1}$, i.e. $f(x) \in Y_{m,m+1}$ ($\Phi$ is linear on fibres ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.snd_sub⟧), and $x \in X_{m+1}$ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmSectionsG · StrongBundle.regularizing_sectionAdd⟧.

*The germ property at a smooth point $x$* ⟦Chapter3/FredholmSectionsG · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmGerm.sectionAdd⟧. Take the chart, filling germ $g$ on $G_g$, sc⁺-germ $t_0$ on $G_{t_0}$ with $\iota t_0(0) = g(0)$ and conjugation $\Psi$ for $(f, x)$. Let $t = \mathbf s$ be the principal part of $s$ in this chart; it is a section of $K \to O$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScPlusSection.mem_K⟧ with lift $t_1$ to $F_1$, $\iota t_1 = t$ on $O$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScPlusSection.toBase_principalLift⟧. Then: $\tilde t = t \circ r$ is sc-smooth on the whole $U$, and $t_1 \circ r$ is sc-smooth into $F^1$ on $U$ (sc-smoothness on the retract $O$, Definition 2.1.14 (preprint 2.7)) — $s$ is a section of the bundle, not a germ; $g + \tilde t$ is a filling germ of $\mathbf f + \mathbf s$ on the same $G_g$ (Lemma 3.1.20 (preprint 3.17) for germs, §III.3 ⟦Chapter3/GermCalculus · IsFillingGerm.add_section⟧, with the filler condition (1) transported along $\mathbf f + \mathbf s$ on $O$ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmSectionsG · IsFillingGerm.congr⟧); $t_1 \circ r + t_0$ is an sc⁺-germ on the same $G_{t_0}$, a germ plus a whole-chart map (§III.48), with value $(g + \tilde t)(0)$ at $0$ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmSectionsG · StrongBundle.sectionAdd_chartData_germ⟧; and $(g + \tilde t) - \iota(t_1 \circ r + t_0) = g - \iota t_0$ *on $U$* (where $\iota t_1(r(u)) = t(r(u))$ because $r(u) \in O$), which is conjugated to a basic germ by the same $\Psi$. This last identity holds on $U$ only, which is why the conjugation condition is stated for $u \in U$. The germs of the data do not change, so nothing of $g$ or $t_0$ away from $G_g$, $G_{t_0}$ is used; the whole-chart proof ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmGermWholeChart.sectionAdd⟧ is the same with the sum rule on $U$ in place of the sum of a germ and a whole-chart map. $\square$

### III.7 Contraction germs: Definition 3.3.1 (preprint 3.28) and its working form

**Definition 3.3.1 (preprint 3.28).** For a germ $G$ around $0$ in $\widetilde C \oplus W$, $B : P \times W_0 \to W_0$ is an *sc⁰-contraction germ* (i.e. $f(a, w) = w - B(a, w)$ is one ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · contractionMap⟧) if $B$ is an sc⁰-germ on $G$, $B(0, 0) = 0$, and for every $\varepsilon > 0$ and $m$ there is $r > 0$ such that every $(a, w)$ with $a \in \widetilde C$, $|a| < r$, $|w|_m < r$ lies in $U_m$ and $|B_m(a, w) - B_m(a, w')|_m \le \varepsilon|w - w'|_m$ for such points ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · IsSc0ContractionGerm⟧. (The text: "for all $(a, w)$, $(a, w')$ on level $m$ sufficiently close to $(0, 0)$"; that these points are in the domain $U_m$ is presupposed.) $B_m$ is the level-$m$ map ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · levelB⟧, with $B_m(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · levelB_zero⟧.

*The working form.* The induction of §III.10 builds from $f$ a contraction germ $f^{(1)}$ whose fibre is $TW = W^1 \oplus W$ and whose map is affine in the second factor; its natural contraction region is unbounded there, which balls cannot express. So the proofs work with explicit regions: a *contraction germ in working form* consists of $B$ and $G$ as above with open neighbourhoods $N(0) \supseteq N(1) \supseteq \cdots$ of $0$ in $P$, open $O(m) \ni 0$ and closed $K(m) \subseteq O(m)$ in $W_m$ with $\iota K(m+1) \subseteq K(m)$, such that for $a \in N(m) \cap \widetilde C$: $\{a\} \times O(m) \subseteq U_m$, $B_m(a, \cdot)$ maps $K(m)$ into $K(m)$ and is a $\tfrac12$-contraction on $O(m)$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm⟧.

*Lemma: every sc⁰-contraction germ has a working form* ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.toContractionGerm⟧. Apply Definition 3.3.1 (preprint 3.28) with $\varepsilon = \tfrac12$ to get radii $r_m$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.rad⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.rad_spec⟧. Make them decrease fast enough for the inclusions: $r'_0 = r_0$, $r'_{m+1} = \min(r_{m+1}, r'_m/(\|\iota_m\| + 1))$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.rad'⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.norm_incl_le_of_le⟧. Put $O(m) = \{|w|_m < r'_m\}$, $K(m) = \{|w|_m \le r'_m/2\}$; then $|\iota w|_m \le \|\iota_m\| r'_{m+1}/2 \le r'_m/2$ for $w \in K(m+1)$. Since $B_m$ is continuous at $0$ with $B_m(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.levelB_zero_eq⟧, there is $\rho^0_m > 0$ with $|B_m(x)|_m < r'_m/4$ for $x \in U_m$, $|x|_m < \rho^0_m$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.exists_cont_rad⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.contRad⟧. Let $\rho_m$ decrease with $\rho_m \le \min(\rho^0_m, r'_m)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.prad⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · IsSc0ContractionGerm.prad_anti⟧ and $N(m) = \{|a| < \rho_m\}$. For $a \in N(m) \cap \widetilde C$ and $w \in K(m)$, $|B_m(a, w)|_m \le |B_m(a, w) - B_m(a, 0)|_m + |B_m(a, 0)|_m \le \tfrac12 \cdot \tfrac{r'_m}{2} + \tfrac{r'_m}{4} = \tfrac{r'_m}{2}$.

### III.8 Existence of the solution germ: the parametrized fixed point theorem and Theorem 3.3.2 (preprint 3.29)

**The parametrized Banach fixed point theorem.** The text invokes "the parametrized version of Banach's fixed point theorem" in Theorems 3.3.2 and 3.5.1 (preprint 3.29 and 3.40) without stating it; Mathlib has the theorem without the parameter. Let $X$ be a complete metric space, $K \subseteq X$ closed and nonempty, and $g_l : K \to K$ ($l \in \Lambda'$, a subset of a topological space) $\tfrac12$-contractions such that $l \mapsto g_l(x)$ is continuous for every $x \in K$. Then the fixed point $\phi(l) \in K$ is unique in $K$ and continuous in $l$: from $d(\phi(l), \phi(l_0)) \le \tfrac12 d(\phi(l), \phi(l_0)) + d(g_l\phi(l_0), g_{l_0}\phi(l_0))$ one gets $d(\phi(l), \phi(l_0)) \le 2d(g_l(\phi(l_0)), g_{l_0}(\phi(l_0)))$, which tends to $0$ by continuity at the single point $\phi(l_0)$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · exists_fixedPoint_continuousOn⟧.

**Theorem 3.3.2 (preprint 3.29).** In the working form, for $a \in N(m) \cap \widetilde C$ the map $B_m(a, \cdot) : K(m) \to K(m)$ is a $\tfrac12$-contraction of a closed subset of $W_m$, with a fixed point $\delta_m(a) \in K(m)$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.exists_fixed⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.fixLvl⟧, which is the only fixed point in the larger set $O(m)$ (two fixed points in $O(m)$ satisfy $|w - w'|_m \le \tfrac12|w - w'|_m$) ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.fixed_unique⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.eq_fixLvl⟧. For $a \in N(m+1) \cap \widetilde C$, $\iota\delta_{m+1}(a) \in K(m) \subseteq O(m)$ is a fixed point of $B_m(a, \cdot)$ because $B$ commutes with the inclusions; hence $\iota\delta_{m+1}(a) = \delta_m(a)$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.incl_fixLvl⟧. So $\delta := \delta_0$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.sol⟧ maps $V_m := N(m) \cap \widetilde C$ into $W_m$ with level-$m$ map $\delta_m$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.levelMap_sol⟧. Each $\delta_m$ is continuous on $V_m$ by the parametrized fixed point theorem ($g_a = B_m(a, \cdot)$ on $K(m)$, continuous in $a$ because $B_m$ is) ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.continuousOn_fixLvl⟧. Write $\mathrm{germ}(s) = (N(m+s) \cap \widetilde C)_m$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.germ⟧ — legitimately a germ because $P$ is finite-dimensional — and call $\delta$ a *solution germ* on a germ $V$ if it is an sc⁰-germ there with $\delta(0) = 0$, $(a, \delta_m(a)) \in U_m$ on $V_m$ and $B(a, \delta(a)) = \delta(a)$ on $V_0$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.IsSolutionGerm⟧. Then: $\delta$ is a solution germ on $\mathrm{germ}(0)$, and every $w \in O(0)$ with $B(a, w) = w$, $a \in N(0) \cap \widetilde C$, equals $\delta(a)$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · theorem_3_3_2⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.eq_sol⟧. The property passes to every $\mathrm{germ}(s)$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.isSolutionGerm_germ⟧. In the text's form, from Definition 3.3.1 (preprint 3.28) with balls: the working form of §III.7 gives the solution germ, unique among solutions $w$ with $|w|_0 < r'_0$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · theorem_3_3_2_of_isSc0ContractionGerm⟧. The text's "uniquely determined" leaves this region implicit (Remark 3.3.8 (preprint 3.36) names it, $|w|_0 \le \tau_0$).

### III.9 The solution germ is sc¹: Theorem 3.3.3 (preprint 3.30)

The text cites [21, Theorem 2.3], "the hard part of the regularity theory", and gives no proof. The formal proof shows that $\delta$ is sc¹ on the germ shifted by one level, $V_m = N(m+1) \cap \widetilde C$, and the shift is forced by the argument.

*The operators.* Let $D$ be the derivative family of $B$. For $a \in N(m+1) \cap \widetilde C$ the point $(a, \delta_{m+1}(a))$ lies in $U_{m+1}$, and the level-$m$ operator of §III.1 there is $L_m(a) := DB(a, \delta_{m+1}(a)) : P \times W_m \to W_m$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.Lop⟧, with fibre part $T_m(a) = D_2B(a, \delta_{m+1}(a))$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.Top⟧ and $L_m(a)(h, \zeta) = L_m(a)(h, 0) + T_m(a)\zeta$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.Lop_apply⟧. By Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9) (a), $\|T_m(a)\| \le \tfrac12$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.norm_Top_le⟧, so $1 - T_m(a)$ is invertible (Neumann series) ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.isUnit_one_sub_Top⟧, and the candidate derivative is $A_m(a) := (1 - T_m(a))^{-1}L_m(a)(\cdot, 0) \in L(P, W_m)$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.Aop⟧. It satisfies the *key identity* $L_m(a)(h, A_m(a)h) = A_m(a)h$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.Lop_Aop⟧, and is the unique solution $\zeta$ of $L_m(a)(h, \zeta) = \zeta$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.eq_Aop⟧. Because the level operators commute with the inclusions ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.toBase_Lop⟧, so do the $A_m$: $\iota A_m(a)h = A_0(a)h$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.toBase_Aop⟧.

*Differentiability at $a_0 \in N(2) \cap \widetilde C$* ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.hasFDerivWithinAt_sol⟧. Let $x_1 = (a_0, \delta_1(a_0)) \in U_1$, $A_1 = A_1(a_0)$, $A_0 = A_0(a_0)$. For $a$ near $a_0$ put $h = a - a_0$ and $k = (h, A_1h) \in E_1$, so $|k|_1 \le (1 + \|A_1\|)|h|$. *This is where $a_0 \in N(2)$ is needed*: $A_1$ exists only because $(a_0, \delta_2(a_0)) \in U_2$, and without it the increment $A_0h$ need not lie in $W_1$, so the sc¹-differentiability of $B$ at $x_1$, which controls only increments in $E_1$, could not be used. For $h$ small, $x_1 + k \in U_1$ and $y := \delta(a_0) + A_0h \in O(0)$. By the sc¹ property, $B(a, y) = B(a_0, \delta(a_0)) + D(x_1)\iota k + \mathrm{rem} = \delta(a_0) + L_0(a_0)(h, A_0h) + \mathrm{rem} = y + \mathrm{rem}$ with $|\mathrm{rem}|_0 = o(|h|)$, using the key identity on level $0$. So $y$ is an approximate fixed point of $B(a, \cdot)$, and the $\tfrac12$-contraction on $O(0)$ gives $|\delta(a) - y|_0 \le \tfrac12|\delta(a) - y|_0 + |\mathrm{rem}|_0$, i.e. $|\delta(a) - \delta(a_0) - A_0h|_0 \le 2|\mathrm{rem}|_0 = o(|h|)$.

*The tangent map is sc⁰.* On level $m$ of the tangent of $\mathrm{germ}(1)$, i.e. on $(N(m+2) \cap \widetilde C) \times P$, the tangent map is $(a, h) \mapsto (\delta_{m+1}(a), A_m(a)h)$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.tangent_toBase_sol⟧. Its first component is continuous (§III.8); the second because $(x, v) \mapsto D_m(x)v$ is jointly continuous and $\zeta(a) = A_m(a)h$ satisfies $\zeta(a) = L_m(a)(h, 0) + T_m(a)\zeta(a)$, whence $|\zeta(a) - \zeta(a')|_m \le 2|L_m(a)(h, 0) - L_m(a')(h, 0)|_m + 2|(T_m(a) - T_m(a'))\zeta(a')|_m$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.continuousOn_Aop⟧. With the derivative family $a \mapsto A_0(a)$ on $\mathrm{germ}(1)$ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.solDeriv⟧, $\delta$ is an sc¹-germ there ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · ContractionGerm.isSc1GermWith_sol⟧ — **Theorem 3.3.3 (preprint 3.30)**, on the shifted germ ⟦Chapter3/ContractionGerm · theorem_3_3_3⟧. At parameters $a_0 \in N(1) \setminus N(2)$ the argument does not apply, and whether $\delta$ is differentiable there is neither needed nor addressed.

### III.10 The contraction germ $f^{(1)}$: Lemma 3.3.4 (preprint 3.31)

**Lemma 3.3.4 (preprint 3.31).** The text: if $f$ is of class sc^k, $k \ge 1$, and $\delta$ of class sc^j, then $f^{(1)}(v, b, u, w) = (u - B(v, u), w - DB(v, \delta(v))(b, w))$ is an sc⁰-contraction germ of class sc^{min(k−1, j)} whose solution germ is $T\delta$ — "as one verifies by comparing $Tf$ with $f^{(1)}$", and "from the uniqueness". The formal version: if $B$ is sc^{k+1} with derivative family $D$ and $\delta$ is sc^j on $\mathrm{germ}(s)$, then $f^{(1)}$ is a contraction germ in working form of class sc^{min(k, j)} with solution germ $T\delta$ on $(N(2+s) \cap \widetilde C) \times P$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.lemma_3_3_4⟧. The parameters are $(v, b) \in \widetilde C \times P \subseteq P \times P$ (quadrant-like ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.isQuadrantLike_next⟧), the unknowns $(u, w) \in TW$, with levels $W_{m+1} \times W_m$.

*Step 1: the germ and the regions.* The germ of $f^{(1)}$ is $U^{(1)}_m = \{((v, b), (u, w)) : (v, u) \in U_{m+1},\ v \in N(m+1+s)\}$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.nextG⟧, and the regions are $N^{(1)}(m) = N(m+2+s) \times P$, $O^{(1)}(m) = O(m+1) \times W_m$, $K^{(1)}(m) = K(m+1) \times W_m$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.next⟧. They are unbounded in $b$ and $w$, necessarily: the solution must be $(\delta(v), D\delta(v)b)$ for all $b \in P$.

*Step 2: the map.* With the reshuffling sc-operator $\sigma((v, b), (u, w)) = ((v, u), (b, w))$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · tangentShuffle⟧ and $\Gamma((v, b), (u, w)) = ((v, \delta(v)), (b, w))$, where $v \mapsto \delta(v)$ is regarded as the *shifted* germ into $W^1$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.nextΓ⟧, formula (38) is $B^{(1)} = (\mathrm{pr}_1 \circ TB \circ \sigma, \mathrm{pr}_2 \circ TB \circ \Gamma)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.nextB⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.nextB_apply⟧. On level $m$, for $v \in N(m+1) \cap \widetilde C$, $B^{(1)}_m((v, b), (u, w)) = (B_{m+1}(v, u), L_m(v)(b, w))$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.levelB_next⟧: the point $(v, \delta_{m+1}(v))$ lies in $U_{m+1}$, which is where the parameter region $N(m+1+\cdot)$ comes from.

*Step 3: contraction and invariance.* For $(v, b) \in N^{(1)}(m)$ and $(u, w), (u', w') \in O^{(1)}(m)$, the two components contract by $\tfrac12$: $B_{m+1}(v, \cdot)$ on $O(m+1)$, and $T_m(v)$ with $\|T_m(v)\| \le \tfrac12$ (Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9) (a)); $K(m+1) \times W_m$ is mapped into itself; $B^{(1)}(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.nextB_zero⟧.

*Step 4: the class of $B^{(1)}$.* $TB$ is an sc^k-germ on the tangent germ; $\sigma$, the projections $(v, b, u, w) \mapsto v$ into $P^1$ and $\mapsto (b, w)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · projV⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · projBW⟧ and the identification $P^1 \oplus W^1 = (P \oplus W)^1$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · shiftParamOp⟧ are sc-operators; $\Gamma$ is the pairing of $(b, w)$ with $(v, \delta(v))$, which is sc^j because $\delta$ is and so is its shift into $W^1$ (Proposition 1.2.2 (preprint 1.17) for germs) ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.nextΓ_isSckGerm⟧; and $\Gamma$ maps $U^{(1)}_m$ into $U_{m+1} \times E_m$ level by level ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.levelMap_nextΓ⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.nextΓ_mapsToGerm⟧, as does $\sigma$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.tangentShuffle_mapsToGerm⟧. The chain rule and the pairing rule of §III.1 give $B^{(1)}$ sc^j ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.nextB_isSckGerm⟧.

*Step 5: the solution germ is $T\delta$.* For $v \in N(2+s) \cap \widetilde C$ and $b \in P$, the point $(\delta_1(v), A_0(v)b) \in O(1) \times W_0$ is a fixed point of $B^{(1)}(v, b, \cdot)$ — the first component by $B_1(v, \delta_1(v)) = \delta_1(v)$, the second by the key identity of §III.9 — so by the uniqueness of Theorem 3.3.2 (preprint 3.29) *in $O^{(1)}(0)$* it is the solution germ of $f^{(1)}$ at $(v, b)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.next_sol⟧, and since $A_0(v) = D\delta(v)$ (Theorem 3.3.3 (preprint 3.30)) this is $T\delta(v, b)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.next_sol_eq_tangentMapWith⟧. The text's "from the uniqueness" is true because the regions of Step 1 contain $T\delta(v, b)$ for every $b$.

### III.11 The induction: Lemma 3.3.5 (preprint 3.32), Theorem 3.3.6 (preprint 3.33), Corollary 3.3.7 (preprint 3.34), Theorem 3.35 (preprint)

The text proves Lemma 3.3.5 (preprint 3.32) by iterating $f \mapsto f^{(1)}$ and Theorem 3.3.6 (preprint 3.33) by contradiction (if $\delta$ were sc^j but not sc^{j+1}, $f^{(j)}$ would be sc¹, so $T^j\delta$ would be sc¹ by Theorem 3.3.3 (preprint 3.30)). The formal proof is the same argument as one induction on $j$, with the statement quantified over *all* contraction germs, since $f^{(1)}$ has parameter space $P \times P$ and fibre $TW$ and the induction hypothesis must apply to it. It also keeps track of the germ.

*Claim ($\mathrm S_j$): for every contraction germ in working form, over any finite-dimensional parameter space, of class sc^k with $k \ge j$, the solution germ is an sc^j-germ on $\mathrm{germ}(s)$ for some $s$* ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.sol_isSckGerm⟧. For $j = 0$ take $s = 0$ (Theorem 3.3.2 (preprint 3.29)). *Step $j \to j + 1$* ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ContractionGerm.isSckGerm_succ_of_next⟧: let $B$ be sc^k, $k = k' + 1 \ge j + 1$. (1) By ($\mathrm S_j$) for $f$, $\delta$ is sc^j on $\mathrm{germ}(s)$. (2) By Lemma 3.3.4 (preprint 3.31) with this $s$, $f^{(1)}$ is a contraction germ of class sc^{min(k', j)} = sc^j. (3) By ($\mathrm S_j$) for $f^{(1)}$, its solution germ is sc^j on its $\mathrm{germ}(s')$, whose level $m$ is $(N(m + s' + 2 + s) \cap \widetilde C) \times P$. (4) By Theorem 3.3.3 (preprint 3.30) $\delta$ is sc¹ on $\mathrm{germ}(1)$, hence on $\mathrm{germ}(s + s' + 1)$ (restriction), whose tangent germ has level $m$ equal to the set in (3). (5) On that set the solution germ of $f^{(1)}$ is $T\delta$ (Lemma 3.3.4 (preprint 3.31), Step 5); the identification of $T\widetilde C = \widetilde C \times P$ inside $TP$ with $\widetilde C \times P$ inside $P \times P$ is the identity on every level, an sc-isomorphism ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · constTangentOp'⟧ through which the chain rule transports the germ. (6) So $\delta$ is sc¹ with sc^j tangent germ: sc^{j+1} on $\mathrm{germ}(s + s' + 1)$. Since the proof does the same for every contraction germ, $s$ and $s'$ are the same function of $j$, so $s_0 = 0$, $s_{j+1} = 2s_j + 1$: $s_j = 2^j - 1$.

**Theorem 3.3.6 (preprint 3.33)** follows from ($\mathrm S_k$): there is $s$ such that $\delta$ is a solution germ and an sc^k-germ on $\mathrm{germ}(s)$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · theorem_3_3_6⟧; if $B$ is sc-smooth this holds for every $k$ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · theorem_3_3_6_smooth⟧; and from Definition 3.3.1 (preprint 3.28) with balls through the working form ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · theorem_3_3_6_of_isSc0ContractionGerm⟧. Lemma 3.3.5 (preprint 3.32) is contained in the induction: its step from $j$ to $j + 1$ is items (2)–(3), without the iterated germs $f^{(j)}$. **Corollary 3.3.7 (preprint 3.34)**: if $B$ is sc-smooth, for $m$ and $k$ take $s$ for $k$; by Proposition 1.2.3 (preprint 1.18) for germs, $\delta$ maps $V_{m,k} := N(m+k+s) \cap \widetilde C$ into $W_m$ by a $C^k$ map ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · corollary_3_3_7⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermLevels · IsSckGerm.contDiffOn_levels⟧, the inclusions of the constant structure being the identity ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · ScBanach.const_inclK⟧. **Theorem 3.35 (preprint)**: for $\widetilde C$ a partial quadrant (which is quadrant-like), the same ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · theorem_3_35_preprint⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermIFT · theorem_3_35_smooth_preprint⟧.

**What the shrinking means.** Definition 3.1.2 (preprint 3.2) calls $\delta$ sc-smooth on a germ if it is sc^k *on that germ* for every $k$. The proof gives, for each $k$, the germ $\mathrm{germ}(2^k - 1)$; these shrink with $k$, their intersection is in general not a neighbourhood of $0$, and no diagonal choice gives one germ, because being sc^k on a germ involves every level of it. So Theorem 3.3.6 (preprint 3.33) and the first sentence of Theorem 3.35 (preprint) hold for germs read up to shrinking, and the last sentences of Corollary 3.3.7 (preprint 3.34) and Theorem 3.35 (preprint) hold in the sense of Corollary 3.3.7 (preprint 3.34) — for every $k$ on a germ depending on $k$ — and are proved neither by the text nor here as `IsScSmoothGerm` on one germ. In the text's reading of germs the two senses coincide. Whether the shrinking is a property of contraction germs or only of the method is not decided: in the toy model of the exposition (Chapter3.md §3) the solution germ is sc-smooth on the original germ.

### III.12 Local regularity and compactness: Theorem 3.5.1 (preprint 3.40)

**Theorem 3.5.1 (preprint 3.40)** (text): for $f = h + s$ on a relatively open $U \ni 0$ in $C$, $h$ a basic germ, $s$ an sc⁺-germ with $s(0) = 0$, and $S = \{f = 0\}$, there are relatively open neighbourhoods $U \supseteq O(0) \supseteq O(1) \supseteq \cdots$ of $0$ on level $0$ with $\mathrm{cl}(S \cap O(m)) \subseteq C_m$. Of $f$ only the $W$-component matters: $S$ lies in the set of solutions of $w = B(a, w) - \mathbf s(a, w)$, $\mathbf s = P_W \circ s$. The formal statement uses only that $B$ is an sc⁰-contraction germ (not sc-smooth), that $\mathbf s$ is an sc⁰-germ into $W^1$ with $\mathbf s(0) = 0$ (the sc⁺ property level by level), and that $\widetilde C$ is closed (true for partial quadrants; the theorem is proved for convex $\widetilde C$, and closedness makes the solution set closed in $E_0$, where the text takes closures in $C_0$). It is *stronger* than the text's in the way Theorem 3.1.13 (preprint 3.12) needs: for any set $S$ of solutions in $U_0$ there are nested open $O(i) \ni 0$ in $E_0$ and compact $K_i \subseteq E_i$ with $\mathrm{cl}_{E_0}(S \cap O(i)) = \iota_i(K_i)$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · theorem_3_5_1_compactness⟧; in particular $\mathrm{cl}(S \cap O(i)) \subseteq E_i$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · theorem_3_5_1_regularity⟧.

*Step 0: the level-$m$ contractions with a parameter.* Let $r_m$ be the radii of Definition 3.3.1 (preprint 3.28) for $\varepsilon = \tfrac12$ and $c_m = \min(r_m, r_0/(\|\iota_m\| + 1))$, $\iota_m : W_m \to W_0$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.crad⟧, so that $|\iota_my|_0 < r_0$ whenever $|y|_m \le c_m/2$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.norm_toBase_lt_rad⟧ (the text's $\tau'_1 < \tau_0/c_1$). Points with $a \in \widetilde C$, $|a|, |w|_m < r_m$ lie in $U_m$ and satisfy the contraction estimate ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.mem_G_of_lt⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.contr_of_lt⟧. Choose $\rho_m \in (0, c_m]$ with $|B_m(a, 0)|_m \le c_m/8$ for $|a| < \rho_m$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.exists_cprad⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.cprad⟧. On $\Sigma_m = \{(a, z) : a \in \widetilde C, |a| < \rho_m, |z|_m \le c_m/8\}$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.Sigma⟧ the maps $w \mapsto B_m(a, w) - z$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.gmap⟧ send the closed ball $\bar B_m(c_m/2)$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.cBall⟧ into itself ($\tfrac12 \cdot \tfrac{c_m}{2} + \tfrac{c_m}{8} + \tfrac{c_m}{8} = \tfrac{c_m}{2}$) ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.gmap_mapsTo⟧, are $\tfrac12$-contractions ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.gmap_contr⟧ and depend continuously on $(a, z)$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.gmap_continuousOn⟧. The parametrized fixed point theorem gives a continuous $\delta_m : \Sigma_m \to \bar B_m(c_m/2)$ with $\delta_m(a, z) = B_m(a, \delta_m(a, z)) - z$, unique in the ball ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.exists_delta⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.delta⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.delta_fixed⟧.

*Step 1: the lifts — the induction the text leaves to the reader.* Let $\mathrm{Sol}(\tau)$ be the solutions $x = (a, w)$ with $a \in \widetilde C$, $|a| \le \tau$, $|w|_0 \le \tau$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · solSet⟧. *Claim:* for every $m$ there are $\tau_m > 0$ and a map $L_m : E_0 \to E_m$ such that for every $x \in \mathrm{Sol}(\tau_m)$: $\iota_mL_mx = x$, $L_mx \in U_m$, $|(L_mx)_W|_m \le c_m/2$, and $L_m$ is continuous on $\mathrm{Sol}(\tau_m)$ *for the level-$0$ topology of the source* ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.exists_lift⟧. For $m = 0$, $L_0 = \mathrm{id}$. Given $L_m$: by continuity of $\mathbf s_m : U_m \to W_{m+1}$ at $0$ (where $\mathbf s_m(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · levelMap_s_zero⟧) there is $\eta$ with $|\mathbf s_m(y)|_{m+1} < c_{m+1}/8$ for $|y|_m < \eta$; by continuity of $L_m$ at $0$ there is $\theta$ with $|L_mx|_m < \eta$ for $x \in \mathrm{Sol}(\tau_m)$, $|x|_0 < \theta$. Put $\tau_{m+1} = \min(\tau_m, \rho_{m+1}/2, \theta/2)$ and, for $x = (a, w) \in \mathrm{Sol}(\tau_{m+1})$, $z = \mathbf s_m(L_mx)$, so $(a, z) \in \Sigma_{m+1}$, and $L_{m+1}x := (a, \delta_{m+1}(a, z))$. *It lifts $x$:* $w' = \iota_{m+1}\delta_{m+1}(a, z)$ solves $w' = B(a, w') - \mathbf s(x)$ (apply $\iota_{m+1}$, which commutes with $B$, and use $\iota_mL_mx = x$); so does $w$; both are smaller than $r_0$ on level $0$ by the choice of $c_{m+1}$, so the level-$0$ contraction gives $w' = w$ — the text's (43), on every level. *Continuity:* $x \mapsto (a, \mathbf s_m(L_mx))$ is continuous from the level-$0$ topology into $\Sigma_{m+1}$, and $\delta_{m+1}$ is continuous. The induction step needs the continuity of $L_m$: the bound $|\mathbf s_m(L_mx)|_{m+1} \le c_{m+1}/8$ requires $L_mx$ small on level $m$, whereas $x$ is small only on level $0$. In the text's level-$1$ step $L_0 = \mathrm{id}$ and this does not show; existence, bound and continuity of the lifts have to be proved together.

*Step 2: compactness.* $\mathrm{Sol}(\tau)$ is closed in $E_0$ ($\widetilde C$ closed, $B$ and $\mathbf s$ continuous on level $0$) ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.isClosed_solSet⟧. For $x \in \mathrm{Sol}(\tau_{m+1})$ the $W$-component is $\iota_{m+1}$ of a vector of norm $\le c_{m+1}/2$, and $\iota_{m+1} : W_{m+1} \to W_0$ is compact ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · ScBanach.toBase_succ_isCompactOperator⟧; with $|a| \le \tau_{m+1}$ in the finite-dimensional $P$, a sequence of lifts has a subsequence converging in $E_{m+1}$ (parameters in a compact ball, $W_0$-parts in a compact image, and the continuity of the lift for the level-$0$ topology) ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · IsSc0ContractionGerm.isCompact_lift_image⟧.

*Step 3: the neighbourhoods.* $O(i)$ are the balls of radius $\varrho_i$, $\varrho_0 = \tau_1$, $\varrho_{i+1} = \min(\varrho_i, \tau_{i+2})$ — one level ahead of the text's $O(m)$, because compactness in $E_i$ comes from boundedness in $E_{i+1}$. Then $\mathrm{cl}(S \cap O(i)) \subseteq \mathrm{Sol}(\tau_{i+1})$, every point of it is $\iota_i$ of the image under $E_{i+1} \to E_i$ of its lift $L_{i+1}x$, and $K_i$ is the compact set of these images intersected with the closed preimage of $\mathrm{cl}(S \cap O(i))$.

For a basic germ plus an sc⁺-germ ($S$ the zero set of $h + \iota s$; its $Q$-component only shrinks $S$) this is the **local Theorem 3.1.13 (preprint 3.12)** ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · theorem_3_1_13_local⟧, and **Corollary 3.1.15 (preprint 3.13) in local form**: solutions $x_k \to 0$ in $E_0$ lie in $\mathrm{Sol}(\tau_m)$ for $k$ large, and $L_mx_k \to L_m0 = 0$ in $E_m$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalCompactness · corollary_3_1_15_local⟧.

### III.13 Theorem 3.1.13 (preprint 3.12) and Corollary 3.1.15 (preprint 3.13) for sections

**Theorem 3.1.13 (preprint 3.12).** Let $f$ be a section of a strong bundle with $(f, x_0)$ an sc-Fredholm germ and $f(x_0) = 0$. Then there are nested open $O(i) \ni x_0$ in $X$ such that $\mathrm{cl}_X\{x \in O(i) \mid f(x) = 0\}$ is the image of a compact subset of $X_i$ (with the level topology of Part II, §II.6) under the inclusion $X_i \to X$ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactnessG · theorem_3_1_13_preprint⟧. Of "$f$ an sc-smooth section of a tame strong bundle" only $P \circ f = \mathrm{id}$ is used; the regularity enters through the germ data, and of the filling germ $g$ only its continuity on level $0$ near $0$. The text calls the theorem "an immediate consequence of Theorem 3.40"; the proof makes explicit six steps.

(i) *Normalizations.* $f(x_0) = 0$ gives $\mathbf f(0) = 0$, so $g(0) = 0$ (filler (1)) and $s(0) = 0$ ($\iota s(0) = g(0)$). (ii) *The zeros correspond.* $f(x) = 0 \iff \mathbf f(\varphi(x)) = 0$ (§III.2) $\iff g(\varphi(x)) = 0$ (filler (1)) near $x_0$; zeros of $g$ in $U$ near $0$ lie in $O$ (filler (2)); and $g(u) = 0 \iff (h + t)(\psi(u)) = 0$, $t(\psi(u)) := (\Psi(u, s(u)))_2$, because $\Psi(u, k) = (\psi(u), \Gamma(u, k))$ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · IsStrongBundleMap.apply_eq⟧ with $\Gamma(u, \cdot)$ linear ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · IsStrongBundleMap.snd_sub⟧ and injective ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · IsStrongBundleIso.snd_eq_zero_iff⟧. (iii) *$t$ is an sc⁺-germ with $t(0) = 0$*: the base map of a strong bundle map is sc-smooth ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · IsStrongBundleMap.base_isScSmooth⟧, $\psi'(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · IsStrongBundleIso.base_zero'⟧, and the fibre component of $\Psi[1]$ lifts that of $\Psi$ to $F'_1$ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · IsStrongBundleMap.toBase_shiftFiber_snd⟧. Theorem 3.5.1 (preprint 3.40) needs $t$ only as an sc⁰-germ into $F'^1$ on the germ of the basic germ. With $s$ a germ on $G_s$, $t$ is one on the germ of the points of the germ of $h$ that $\psi^{-1}$ maps into $G_s$ level by level ⟦Chapter3/GermFilling · ScNbhdGerm.preimage⟧ (germ composition with the whole-chart $\psi^{-1}$ and $\Psi[1]$, §III.48), and the basic germ restricts to that germ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermShrink · IsBasicGerm.mono⟧ (a form of the shrinking of §III.48). (iv) *Local compactness for a subset.* The local theorem for an arbitrary subset of the solution set of $h + \iota t$ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · theorem_3_1_13_local_subset⟧ is applied on the restricted basic germ to $\psi\varphi$ of the zeros near $x_0$; its $O'(i)$ are intersected with a small ball, so that $E_0$-closures stay inside $\psi(U)$ and the region of (ii) (the closure of a subset of the solution set is still compact). (v) *Back to $X$.* The $E_0$-closure consists of zeros of $h + \iota t$, a limit of zeros in the domain of continuity being a zero ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · eq_of_mem_closure_of_continuousOn⟧, hence of zeros of $g$ in $U$, which lie in $O$. Here $g$ must be continuous at the limits $\psi^{-1}(u')$: the level-$0$ neighbourhood of the filler and conjugation identities is chosen inside the open set $O_g$ with $(G_g)_0 = O_g \cap C$, so the limits lie in $(G_g)_0$, where the sc⁰-germ $g$ is continuous. The whole-chart theorem ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · theorem_3_1_13_wholeChart⟧ took $t$ sc-smooth on all of $U'$ and $g$ continuous on $U$; nothing of either away from a neighbourhood of $0$ was used. An $X$-closure of a subset of the chart domain could a priori leave it; compactness in $E_0$ shows it does not. (vi) *Levels.* A compact subset of $E_i$ is carried to a compact subset of $X_i$ because $\psi^{-1}$ is sc⁰ and the inverse chart $O_i \to X_i$ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · MPolyfold.levelChartSymm⟧ is continuous for the level topology of $X_i$, the transition maps being sc⁰ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · MPolyfold.continuousOn_levelChartSymm⟧ — the paragraph after Definition 2.3.8 (preprint 2.14), for every level.

**Corollary 3.1.15 (preprint 3.13).** If $f(x_k) = 0$ and $x_k \to x_0$, fix $m$ and take $O(m)$ and the compact $K_m \subseteq X_m$; for $k$ large $x_k \in O(m)$, so $x_k = \iota y_k$ with $y_k \in K_m$, and $x_0 = \iota y_0$. $X_m$ is not known to be first countable here (its metrizability, Theorem 2.3.10 (preprint 2.16), is not used), so subsequences are avoided: a sequence in a compact set whose images under a continuous injection converge, converges ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · tendsto_of_isCompact_of_tendsto_map⟧. So $y_k \to y_0$ in $X_m$ ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactnessG · corollary_3_1_15_preprint⟧. The text states the corollary for an arbitrary limit $x$ while the hypothesis concerns the germ at $x_0$; for an sc-Fredholm section and any limit $x$ of zeros, $x$ is a zero (the principal part is continuous in a chart ⟦Chapter3/SectionCompactness · StrongBundle.IsScSmoothSection.isZeroAt_of_tendsto⟧), hence a smooth point (regularizing), hence the germ property holds at $x$ and the first part applies ⟦Chapter3/Chapter5BridgeG · corollary_3_1_15_section⟧.

### III.14 Good position (Definition 3.1.24 (preprint 3.21), Lemma 3.8.8 (preprint 3.65), Proposition 3.1.25 (preprint 3.22), Lemma 3.5.9 (preprint 3.45), Proposition 3.8.12 (preprint 3.69))

**Definition 3.1.24 (preprint 3.21).** $P$ is a *good complement* of the finite-dimensional $N$ with respect to $C$ if $E_0 = N \oplus P$ algebraically and for some $\varepsilon > 0$, $|p|_0 \le \varepsilon|n|_0$ implies ($n \in C \iff n + p \in C$) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · IsGoodComplement⟧; $N$ is *in good position* if $N \cap C$ has nonempty interior in $N$ and $N$ has a good complement ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · IsInGoodPosition⟧. The text asks for an sc-complement (or, for non-smooth $N$, a topological one); none of Proposition 3.1.25 (preprint 3.22), Lemmas 3.5.9, 3.8.8 (preprint 3.45, 3.65) or Proposition 3.8.12 (preprint 3.69) uses more than the algebraic splitting, so only that is required. Where closedness is needed (Lemma 3.6.9 (preprint 3.55), Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25)) it is added there.

*Set-up in coordinates.* After a presentation $E \cong \mathbb R^n \oplus W$, $C = \{\ell_i \ge 0\}$ for continuous functionals $\ell_1, \dots, \ell_n$ jointly onto $\mathbb R^n$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · coordFun⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · coordFun_surj⟧. Inside $N$, $Q := N \cap C$ is the cone $\{\ell_i \ge 0\}$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · quadCone⟧, closed ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · quadCone_isClosed⟧. Let $\Sigma$ be the indices $i$ with $\ell_i(a) = 0$ for some nonzero $a \in Q$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · vanIdx⟧; by definition, for $i \notin \Sigma$ and $x \in Q \setminus 0$, $\ell_i(x) > 0$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · pos_of_not_mem_vanIdx⟧. Let $N \cap W = \{x \in N : \ell_i(x) = 0\ \forall i\}$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · nullSpace⟧ and $Q_\Sigma = (\ell_i)_{i \in \Sigma} : N \to \mathbb R^\Sigma$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · sigmaCoord⟧.

**Lemma 3.8.8 (preprint 3.65).** A good complement satisfies $\ell_i(p) = 0$ for $p \in P$, $i \in \Sigma$: take $a \in Q \setminus 0$ with $\ell_i(a) = 0$, scale $p$ so that $|p| \le \varepsilon|a|$; $a \in C$ gives $a + p \in C$, so $\ell_i(p) = \ell_i(a + p) \ge 0$; the same for $-p$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · lemma_3_8_8⟧. *Consequences.* $Q_\Sigma$ is onto: for $k \in \mathbb R^\Sigma$ pick $v$ with $\ell_i(v) = k_i$ and split $v = n + p$; then $Q_\Sigma(n) = k$ (the text's (55), preprint p. 125) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · sigmaCoord_surjective⟧. If an interior point $y$ of $Q$ has $\ell_i(y) = 0$, then $\ell_i = 0$ on $N$ ($y \pm tu \in Q$) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · restrFun_eq_zero_of_interior⟧.

**Proposition 3.1.25 (preprint 3.22), the normal form.** There are $k$, a subspace $N_0$ and a linear isomorphism $e : N \to \mathbb R^k \times N_0$ with $Q = e^{-1}([0, \infty)^k \times N_0)$, $k = \dim N - \dim(N \cap W)$, the $\mathbb R^k$-component of $e$ vanishing exactly on $N \cap W$, and the vanishing coordinates of $e(x)$ as many as the vanishing $\ell_i(x)$ for $x \in Q \setminus N \cap W$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · QuadrantNormalForm⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · exists_quadrantNormalForm⟧. Three cases. *Case A, $Q = N$*: every $\ell_i$ is $\ge 0$ on a vector space, hence $0$; $k = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · quadrantNormalForm_of_eq_univ⟧ (a case the text's case distinction does not single out). *Case B, $Q \ne N$, $\Sigma = \emptyset$*: an interior point $y$ is nonzero with all $\ell_i(y) > 0$; for $u \in N$ the set $\{t : u + ty \in Q\}$ is closed, contains all large $t$ and is bounded below; at its minimum $t^*$ some $\ell_i(u + t^*y) = 0$, so $u + t^*y = 0$: $N$ is the line $\mathbb Ry$ and $Q$ a half-line ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · quadrantNormalForm_of_vanIdx_empty⟧ (the text's Lemma 3.8.11 (preprint 3.68), second case). *Case C, $\Sigma \ne \emptyset$*: first $Q = \{x \in N : \ell_i(x) \ge 0 \text{ for } i \in \Sigma\}$ — move from such an $x$ towards an interior point $y$ along $x + sy$; at the first parameter $s^*$ with $x + s^*y \in Q$, if $s^* > 0$ the point $z = x + s^*y$ is nonzero (else $\ell_i(x) < 0$ for $i \in \Sigma$), so it has $\ell_i(z) > 0$ for all $i$ ($\ell_i(z) \ge s^*\ell_i(y) > 0$ in $\Sigma$, by definition of $\Sigma$ outside), and finitely many strict inequalities survive a small step back, contradicting minimality ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · mem_quadCone_of_vanIdx⟧. This replaces the text's extreme rays (Lemmas 3.8.1–3.8.3 (preprint 3.62–3.64), cited from [21], and 3.66–3.68). Then a linear right inverse $g$ of $Q_\Sigma$ gives $e(x) = (Q_\Sigma x, x - gQ_\Sigma x) \in \mathbb R^\Sigma \times \ker Q_\Sigma$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · splitEquiv⟧, and $\ker Q_\Sigma = N \cap W$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · quadrantNormalForm_of_vanIdx⟧. For a partial quadrant in good position this applies in a presentation ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · IsInGoodPosition.quadrantNormalForm⟧, and choosing a basis of $N_0$ makes $(N, N \cap C)$ isomorphic to $(\mathbb R^{\dim N}, [0, \infty)^k \oplus \mathbb R^{\dim N - k})$ through an sc-isomorphism of constant structures ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · scIsoOfLinearEquiv⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · preimage_eq_of_normalForm⟧: **$N \cap C$ is a partial quadrant in $N$** ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · proposition_3_1_25⟧.

**Lemma 3.5.9 (preprint 3.45) (linear form).** A continuous linear isomorphism $S : E_0 \to E'_0$ with $x \in C \iff Sx \in C'$ carries a good complement $P$ of $N$ to one of $S(N)$, with $\varepsilon' = \varepsilon/(\|S\|\|S^{-1}\| + 1)$: for $|Sp| \le \varepsilon'|Sn|$, $|p| \le \|S^{-1}\|\varepsilon'\|S\||n| \le \varepsilon|n|$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · IsGoodComplement.map⟧; and the interior condition is transported by the homeomorphism $S|_N$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · IsInGoodPosition.map⟧. The text states it for $T\varphi(0)$; since the coordinate change of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) is linear, this is the form §§III.18–III.23 need.
