**Proposition 3.8.12 (preprint 3.69).** In the model $\mathbb R^n \oplus W$, for $x \in N \cap C$: $d_{N \cap C}(x) = d_C(x)$ if $x \notin N \cap W$, and $d_{N \cap C}(x) + \dim(N \cap W) = \dim N$ if $x \in N \cap W$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · proposition_3_8_12⟧ — read off the normal form ($d_{N \cap C}$ does not depend on the presentation, Lemma 1.3.4 (preprint 1.24)).

### III.15 The degeneracy index along a graph: Lemma 3.8.13 (preprint 3.70)

**Lemma 3.8.13 (preprint 3.70).** In the model, for $N$ in good position with good complement $Y$ and $\tau : N \to E$ with values in $Y$, $C^1$ on $N \cap C$, $\tau(0) = 0$, $D\tau(0) = 0$: there is $\rho > 0$ such that for $v \in N \cap C$, $|v| < \rho$, $v + \tau(v) \in C$, and $d_{N \cap C}(v) = d_C(v + \tau(v))$ if $v \notin N \cap W$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGraph · lemma_3_8_13⟧. The text distinguishes $\dim N = 1$ (a contradiction argument with $\tau(v) = o(|v|)$) and $\dim N \ge 2$ (the sign of a directional derivative). The formal proof unifies them.

*Ray decomposition.* From the normal form, $u_i = e^{-1}(\mathbf e_i, 0)$ are finitely many nonzero points of $Q$, and every $v \in Q \setminus N \cap W$ has some $i$ and $s > 0$ with $v - su_i \in Q$ and $s|u_i| \le c|v|$ (take $s = \lambda_i > 0$ for $e(v) = (\lambda, n_0)$) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGraph · exists_rayDecomp⟧ — the text's basis $e^j$ of $\widetilde N$. *Smallness.* $D\tau(0) = 0$ and continuity of $D\tau$ on the convex $Q$ make $\tau$ $\eta$-Lipschitz on $Q \cap B(\rho)$ for every $\eta$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGraph · exists_lipschitz_small⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGraph · quadCone_convex⟧. *The margin.* Choose $\eta \le \varepsilon$ with $\eta\|\ell_j\||u_i| < \ell_j(u_i)$ for all $i$ and $j \notin \Sigma$ — a positive lower bound of finitely many positive numbers ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGraph · exists_pos_le_of_forall_pos⟧. *Conclusion* ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGraph · lemma_3_8_13_core⟧. For $|v| < \rho/(1 + c)$: $|\tau(v)| \le \varepsilon|v|$, so $v + \tau(v) \in C$ (good complement). For $j \in \Sigma$, $\ell_j(\tau(v)) = 0$ (Lemma 3.8.8 (preprint 3.65)). For $v \notin N \cap W$, $w = v - su_i \in Q$, the segment $[w, v]$ lies in $Q \cap B(\rho)$ — which is why the radius is $\rho/(1 + c)$, the text's "close to $0$" — and for $j \notin \Sigma$,
$$\ell_j(v + \tau(v)) = \ell_j(w + \tau(w)) + s\ell_j(u_i) + \ell_j(\tau(v) - \tau(w)) \ge 0 + s\ell_j(u_i) - \|\ell_j\|\eta s|u_i| > 0.$$
So $v$ and $v + \tau(v)$ have the same vanishing coordinates, and Proposition 3.8.12 (1) (preprint 3.69) gives the lemma in the model, with the coordinate functionals ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGraph · coordCLM⟧. (The level $Y_1$ of the text's $\tau$ plays no role.)

### III.16 The quadrant inverse function theorem (Theorem 3.8.15 (preprint 3.72), Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73), Lemmas 3.8.18, 3.8.21 (preprint 3.74, 3.77), Corollary 3.8.19 (preprint 3.75))

*Interior points.* For the model quadrant, $x$ is interior iff all its half-line coordinates are positive ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · interior_quadrant⟧, so $d_C(x) = 0$ exactly at interior points ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · degIndex_eq_zero_iff⟧. A linear isomorphism $S$ with $S(C) = C$ preserves $d_C$ (Lemma 1.3.4 (preprint 1.24) for the presentation $T \circ S$) ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · ScIso.ofCLE⟧ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · degIndex_map_eq⟧. A linear map carrying a set with nonempty interior onto itself is onto ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · surjective_of_image_eq⟧ — the text's "$\mathbb R^n = C - C$".

**Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73).** The hypotheses (`InvData`): $U$ relatively open and convex in the partial quadrant $C$ of a finite-dimensional $V$; $f : U \to C$ of class $C^j$, $j \ge 1$; $\|Df(x) - 1\| \le \tfrac12$; and $x$ interior iff $f(x)$ interior — the only consequence of $d_C(x) = d_C(f(x))$ the proof uses ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData⟧. The text's $0 \in U$, $f(0) = 0$ are not needed. Conclusion: $f(U)$ is relatively open in $C$, $f$ is injective on $U$, with an inverse of class $C^j$ on $f(U)$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · proposition_3_8_17⟧.

**Lemma 3.8.18 (preprint 3.74).** $x \mapsto x - f(x)$ is a $\tfrac12$-contraction on the convex $U$ (mean value inequality) ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.norm_sub_sub_le⟧, which gives (70): $\tfrac12|x - x'| \le |f(x) - f(x')| \le \tfrac32|x - x'|$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.half_norm_le⟧ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.norm_sub_le⟧, and injectivity ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.injOn⟧. *Openness*: if $B(x', 3r) \cap C \subseteq U$ then $B(f(x'), r) \cap C \subseteq f(U)$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.ball_inter_subset_image⟧. The set $A = f(\bar B(x', 2r) \cap C)$ is compact ($\bar B(x', 2r) \cap C \subseteq U$ is compact in finite dimensions). Interior points first: for an interior $y_1 \in B(y', r)$ choose an interior $x_0 \in U$ with $f(x_0) \in B(y', r)$ — the text takes any interior $x_0 \in B(x', 3r) \cap C$, which does not suffice — and follow the segment $y_t$ from $f(x_0)$ to $y_1$, which stays interior. $\{t : y_t \in A\}$ is closed and contains $0$; at its maximum $t^* < 1$, $y_{t^*} = f(x^*)$ with $x^*$ interior and $|x^* - x'| < 2r$, and at the interior point $x^*$, $Df(x^*)$ is a unit ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.isUnit_deriv⟧ and $U$ a neighbourhood ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.U_mem_nhds⟧, so the classical inverse function theorem maps neighbourhoods onto neighbourhoods ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.map_nhds_eq⟧, pushing $t^*$ further — a contradiction. The compactness of $A$ replaces the text's Cauchy sequence and also gives the remaining points by density. So $f(U)$ is relatively open ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.image_relOpen⟧. **Corollary 3.8.19 (preprint 3.75)**: $f$ and its inverse ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.inv⟧ (2-Lipschitz by (70) ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.norm_inv_sub_le⟧) are continuous and inverse to each other ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · corollary_3_8_19⟧.

**Lemma 3.8.21 (preprint 3.77).** The inverse $g$ is differentiable within $f(U)$ with $Dg(y) = [Df(g(y))]^{-1}$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.hasFDerivWithinAt_inv⟧: with $x_0 = g(y_0)$, $x_0 + \delta = g(y_0 + h)$, the quotient $|g(y_0 + h) - g(y_0) - Df(x_0)^{-1}h|/|h|$ is $|\delta|/|h|$ (bounded by (70)) times the remainder quotient of $f$ at $x_0$. The lemma's extra hypotheses (relatively open image, homeomorphism, invertible derivative) are consequences of those of Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73). *Regularity (the text: Lemma 3.8.20 (preprint 3.76) on the iterated tangent maps $T^kf$).* $x \mapsto [Df(x)]^{-1}$ is $C^{j-1}$ (inversion is smooth on units) ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.contDiffOn_inverse_deriv⟧, so $Dg = [Df]^{-1} \circ g$ and $g \in C^i$, $i < j$, give $g \in C^{i+1}$: $g$ is $C^j$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.contDiffOn_inv⟧. Lemma 3.8.20 (preprint 3.76) is not needed.

**Theorem 3.8.15 (preprint 3.72).** $U$ a relatively open neighbourhood of $0$ in $C$, $f$ of class $C^j$ with $f(U) \subseteq C$, $f(0) = 0$, $Df(0)C = C$, $d_C(x) = d_C(f(x))$ on $U$: then $f$ is a $C^j$-diffeomorphism between relatively open neighbourhoods $U'$, $V'$ of $0$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · theorem_3_8_15⟧. *Proof.* $Df(0)$ is onto, hence invertible, with $Df(0)^{-1}C = C$; $\tilde f = Df(0)^{-1}f$ maps into $C$, $D\tilde f(0) = 1$, and preserves $d_C$ ($d_C \circ Df(0)^{-1} = d_C$), hence interior points. On $U' = B(0, \rho) \cap C$ with $\|D\tilde f - 1\| \le \tfrac12$, Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73) applies, and $V' = Df(0)\tilde f(U')$ is relatively open since $Df(0)$ is a linear homeomorphism preserving $C$. The text's hypothesis (2) is used only through interior points; some such hypothesis is needed ($f(x, y) = (x, y + x^2)$ on $[0, \infty)^2$ satisfies (1) but $f(C)$ is not a neighbourhood of $0$ in $C$).

### III.17 The implicit function theorem in partial quadrants (Theorem 3.8.22 (preprint 3.78), Lemmas 3.8.23–3.8.26 (preprint 3.79–3.82))

**Theorem 3.8.22 (preprint 3.78).** $U$ a relatively open neighbourhood of $0$ in the partial quadrant $C$ of a finite-dimensional $V$, $f : U \to F$ of class $C^j$, $j \ge 1$, with $f(0) = 0$, $Df(0)$ onto, $K = \ker Df(0)$ in good position with good complement $Y$. Then there are a relatively open $V' \ni 0$ in $K \cap C$, $\tau : K \to Y$ of class $C^j$ on $V'$ and $\varepsilon, \sigma > 0$ with (1) $\tau(0) = 0$, $D\tau(0) = 0$, and $k + \tau(k) \in U$, **$f(k + \tau(k)) = 0$** for $k \in V'$; (2) $|\tau(k)| \le \sigma$ for $|k| \le \varepsilon$; (3) if $k \in V'$, $|k| \le \varepsilon$, $y \in Y$, $|y| \le \sigma$, **$k + y \in U$** and $f(k + y) = 0$, then $y = \tau(k)$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · theorem_3_8_22⟧. The two bold clauses are not in the text; the first is the content of the theorem (its proof has $\tau(k) = B(k, \tau(k))$), the second is needed for $f(k + y)$ to make sense.

*Normalization (71)–(72).* With $L = (Df(0)|_Y)^{-1}$, in coordinates $x = k + y \in K \oplus Y$, $Lf(k + y) = y - B(k, y)$ with $B(k, y) := y - Lf(k + y)$, $B(0, 0) = 0$, $DB(0, 0) = 0$. The data after normalization: on $K \times Y$ a convex set $C_p$ with nonempty interior, a relatively open $U_p \ni 0$, $B$ of class $C^j$ on $U_p$ with $B(0) = 0$, $DB(0) = 0$, the good-position estimate and the interior condition ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · ContractionData⟧. Only convexity and interior are used; the quadrant enters through good position.

*Lemmas 3.8.23, 3.8.24 (preprint 3.79, 3.80): the constants* ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii⟧ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · ContractionData.exists_radii⟧. With $\varepsilon$ the good-position constant and $c_0 = \varepsilon/(2(1 + \varepsilon)) \le \tfrac12$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.c₀⟧, continuity of $DB$ at $0$ gives $a, b$ with $\varepsilon a \le b$, the region $R = \{|k| \le a, |y| \le b\} \cap C_p \subseteq U_p$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.R⟧ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.R_sub_U⟧ and $\|DB\| \le c_0$ on $R$. $R$ is convex ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.R_convex⟧, so the mean value inequality gives $|B(q) - B(p)| \le c_0|q - p|$ on $R$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.norm_sub_le⟧ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.norm_B_sub_le_snd⟧ and $|B(p)| \le c_0|p|$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.norm_B_le⟧. For $k \in V_0 = \{k \in K \cap C : |k| < a\}$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.V₀⟧ and $|y| \le \varepsilon|k|$: good position puts $(k, y) \in C_p$, $|y| \le \varepsilon a \le b$, so $(k, y) \in R$ and $|B(k, y)| \le c_0(1 + \varepsilon)|k| = \tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}|k|$. So $B(k, \cdot)$ maps $X_k = \bar B_Y(\varepsilon|k|)$ into itself ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.mapsTo_ball⟧ and has a fixed point $\tau(k)$ there ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.exists_fixed⟧ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.τ⟧; any fixed point $y$ with $(k, y) \in R$ equals $\tau(k)$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.eq_τ⟧. (In Lemma 3.8.24 (preprint 3.80) the maximum is over $B((1 + \varepsilon)a) \cap C$, where $DB$ is defined.)

*Lemma 3.8.25 (preprint 3.81): continuity.* $|\tau(k)| \le \tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}|k|$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.norm_τ_le⟧. If $|k_0| \le 2|k|$, then $(k, \tau(k_0)) \in R$ and $|\tau(k) - \tau(k_0)| \le \tfrac12|\tau(k) - \tau(k_0)| + c_0|k - k_0|$, so $|\tau(k) - \tau(k_0)| \le \varepsilon|k - k_0|$ — the text's estimate, whose argument needs $(k, \tau(k_0))$ in the domain. If $|k_0| > 2|k|$, then $|k - k_0| \ge \tfrac12|k_0|$ and $|\tau(k) - \tau(k_0)| \le \tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}(|k| + |k_0|) \le \tfrac{3\varepsilon}{2}|k - k_0|$. In all cases the constant is $1 + 2\varepsilon$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.norm_τ_sub_le⟧, and $\tau$ is continuous ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.continuousOn_τ⟧. Since $X_k$ depends on $k$, the parametrized fixed point theorem of §III.8 does not apply, and continuity is proved by hand, as in the text.

*Lemma 3.8.26 (preprint 3.82): regularity.* With $D_1B = DB \circ \mathrm{inl}$, $D_2B = DB \circ \mathrm{inr}$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.D₁⟧ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.D₂⟧, $\|D_2B\| \le \tfrac12$ on $R$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.norm_D₂_le⟧, $M = (1 - D_2B)^{-1}$ (Neumann) ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.M⟧ and $\Phi = MD_1B$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.Φ⟧: from the fixed-point identities and the little-o composition of $B$ with the Lipschitz $k' \mapsto (k', \tau(k'))$, $\tau$ is differentiable within $V_0$ with $D\tau(k) = \Phi(k, \tau(k))$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.hasFDerivWithinAt_τ⟧ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.fderivWithin_τ⟧. $\Phi$ is $C^{j-1}$ on $R$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.contDiffOn_Φ⟧, so $\tau \in C^i$, $i < j$, gives $D\tau = \Phi \circ (\mathrm{id}, \tau) \in C^i$ and $\tau \in C^{i+1}$: $\tau$ is $C^j$ on all of $V_0$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · Radii.contDiffOn_τ⟧. The text iterates $f \mapsto f^{(1)}$ (the finite-dimensional model of Lemma 3.3.4 (preprint 3.31)) and concludes "on a possibly smaller neighbourhood"; in finite dimensions there are no levels to shift and no shrinking is needed. *Assembly* ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantIFT · ContractionData.exists_solution⟧: $V' = V_0$, $\sigma = b$, $\varepsilon$ below $a$; (1) $Lf(k + \tau(k)) = \tau(k) - B(k, \tau(k)) = 0$ and $L$ is injective; (3) $f(k + y) = 0$ means $B(k, y) = y$ with $(k, y) \in R$.

### III.18 Weak stability of basic germs: Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38)

**Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38)** (text): for a basic germ $f$ and the principal part $s$ of an sc⁺-section with $s(0) = 0$ there is a strong bundle isomorphism $\Phi$ covering an sc-diffeomorphism $\varphi$ such that $(\Phi_*(f + s))^1$, with the levels raised by one, is a basic germ, and $n - N = n' - N'$. The text's proof: with $A = P_W \circ D_2s(0)$, $1 + A$ is sc-Fredholm of index $0$, with sc-splittings $W = C \oplus X = R \oplus Z$, $C = \ker(1 + A)$, $R = \mathrm{range}(1 + A)$; $P_W \circ s(a, w) = Aw + S(a, w)$ with $S$ a small contraction in $w$ on every level $m \ge 1$; and with $L = (1 + A)|_X : X \to R$ the equation becomes $x - L^{-1}P_2\bar B(a, c + x) = 0$ in the new parameters $(a, c)$ and the new fibre variable $x$. The formal proof follows it with two observations: the coordinate change is *linear*, and the index raise is a *change of sc-structure*.

*The linear part of $s$.* For an sc¹-germ $s$ into $W^1$, $A = D_2s(0)$ is an sc⁺-operator whose level-$m$ map is the level operator of Proposition 1.2.1 (preprint 1.16) at $0 \in U_{m+1}$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · scPlusOfDeriv⟧. The remainder $S(a, w) = s(a, w) - Aw$ on level $m + 1$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · scPlusRemainder⟧ is, for $a$ and $w$ small depending on $m$ and $\varepsilon$, an $\varepsilon$-contraction in $w$: $D_2S(0) = 0$ on level $m + 1$, the derivative of $s : U_{m+1} \to W_{m+1}$ is continuous in the operator norm (§III.1), and the mean value inequality on a ball for fixed $a$ concludes ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · scPlusRemainder_contraction⟧. The partial derivative of $S$ in $a$ does not vanish and plays no role. The text gives this one sentence ("$S$ is … an arbitrary small contraction on every level $m \ge 1$").

*The data and the splitting.* The data of the theorem are the germ $G$, the $W$-component $s$ of the sc⁺-germ with $s(0) = 0$, and an sc-Fredholm splitting $W = C \oplus X = R \oplus Z$ of $T = 1 + A$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData⟧. Such data exist: the identity is sc-Fredholm of index $0$ (the trivial splitting) ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · isScFredholm_id⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · scIndex_id⟧, so $1 + A$ is sc-Fredholm by Proposition 1.1.11 (preprint 1.10) ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.exists_of_scPlus⟧. With the projections $P_1 : W_m \to X_m$ along $C_m$, $P_2 : W_m \to R_m$ along $Z_m$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.projX⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.projR⟧, $L = T|_X : X_m \to R_m$ is bijective ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.Lm_bijective⟧, a topological isomorphism by the open mapping theorem ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.Lequiv⟧, and $L^{-1}P_2$ is an sc-operator $W \to X$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.LinvPOp⟧. The finite-dimensional $C$, $Z \subseteq W_\infty$ lift to every level ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · ScBanach.finLift⟧.

*The coordinate changes.* On the index-raised spaces, the new source is $E' = (P \oplus C) \oplus X^1$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.E'⟧ and the new target $F' = (Q \oplus Z) \oplus X^1$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.F'⟧ — the text's $\tau : Z \to C$, making the new finite-dimensional target $\mathbb R^N \oplus C$, is not applied. The base change $\psi(a, w) = ((a, (1 - P_1)w), P_1w)$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.split⟧ and its inverse $\psi^{-1}((a, c), x) = (a, c + x)$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.glue⟧ are sc-operators inverse to each other ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.glue_split⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.split_glue⟧; so are the fibre change $\Psi(q, w) = ((q, (1 - P_2)w), L^{-1}P_2w)$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.fiber⟧ and $\Psi^{-1}((q, z), x) = (q, z + (1 + A)x)$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.fiberInv⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.fiber_fiberInv⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.fiberInv_fiber⟧, and $\Psi$ vanishes only at $0$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.fiber_eq_zero_iff⟧. No general strong bundle isomorphism is needed.

*The push-forward.* The perturbed germ is $f + \iota s$ with the inclusion $W^1 \to Q \oplus W$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · ScBanach.inrOp⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.fsum⟧, sc-smooth by the pairing and operator rules ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.fsum_isScSmoothGerm⟧. Its germ of neighbourhoods on $E'$ is $\psi(U_{m+1})$, the preimage of $U_{m+1}$ under $\psi^{-1}$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushGerm⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.glue_mapsToGerm⟧, and the push-forward is $(\Phi_*(f + s))^1 = \Psi \circ (f + \iota s)^1 \circ \psi^{-1}$ with the level-shifted operators ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · ScOperator.shiftOp⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushMap⟧. It is sc-smooth: $(f + \iota s)^1$ is sc-smooth on the shifted germ (Proposition 1.2.2 (preprint 1.17) for germs) and $\Psi$, $\psi^{-1}$ are sc-operators ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushMap_isScSmoothGerm⟧. With $\bar B = B - S$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.Bbar⟧, the $W$-component of $(f + \iota s)^1$ at a level-$1$ point is $(1 + A)w - \bar B(a, w)$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.snd_levelMap_fsum⟧, so the $X$-component of the push-forward is $x - \hat B((a, c), x)$ with $\hat B((a, c), x) = L^{-1}P_2\bar B(a, c + x)$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushB⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.snd_pushMap⟧ — the text's "desired formula". $\hat B$ is an sc⁰-contraction germ on the index-raised structure: on level $m$ of $E'$, i.e. level $m + 1$ of $W$, $B$ contracts by Definition 3.1.7 (preprint 3.7) and $S$ by the remainder estimate, and $L^{-1}P_2$ is bounded ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.levelMap_pushB⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushB_isSc0ContractionGerm⟧. So **the push-forward is a basic germ with contraction map $\hat B$** ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · theorem_3_4_2⟧. Its zeros correspond to those of $f + \iota s$ under $\psi$, since $\Psi$ is a linear isomorphism ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushMap_eq_zero_iff⟧. *The index*: $\mathrm{ind}(1 + A) = \mathrm{ind}(1) = 0$ (Proposition 1.1.11 (preprint 1.10) with its index), i.e. $\dim C = \dim Z$, so $n' - N' = (n + \dim C) - (N + \dim Z) = n - N$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.finrank_Cs_eq_finrank_Zs⟧.

*The same on level $0$.* For §§III.19–III.20 the coordinate changes are also needed before the index raise: $\psi$, $\psi^{-1}$, $\Psi$, $\Psi^{-1}$ on every level ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.split₀⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.glue₀⟧ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.fiber₀⟧, the germ $\psi(U_m)$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushGerm₀⟧, and $g = \Phi_*(f + s) = \Psi \circ (f + \iota s) \circ \psi^{-1}$ on level $0$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushMap₀⟧, sc-smooth ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushMap₀_isScSmoothGerm⟧, whose level-$1$ map is the index-raised push-forward ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · StabilityData.pushMap_eq_levelMap_pushMap₀⟧.

### III.19 The linearization at nearby solutions: Corollary 3.5.3 (preprint 3.41)

**Corollary 3.5.3 (preprint 3.41).** For the data of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) with $f := h$ basic and $D(h + \iota s)(0)$ surjective, there is $r > 0$ such that every zero $x$ of $h + \iota s$ in $U_0$ with $|x|_0 < r$ is $\iota(x_1)$ with $x_1 \in U_1$, and $D(h + \iota s)(x_1) : E_0 \to F_0$ is a surjective Fredholm operator of index $\dim P - \dim Q$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.corollary_3_5_3⟧. The text's sc⁺-map $s$ has an $\mathbb R^N$-component; it can be absorbed into the basic germ (as in §III.23), so the data carry only the $W$-component.

*The derivative on $U_1$.* For $x \in U_1$, $D(h + \iota s)(x) = Dh(x) + \iota Ds(x)$ — uniqueness of the derivative within $U_1$, then density of $E_1$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.Dfs'_eq⟧ — and $\mathrm{pr}_W Dh(x) = \mathrm{pr}_W - DB(x)$, from $\mathrm{pr}_W h = \mathrm{pr}_W - B$ on $U_0$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.snd_Df_eq⟧; $D_2B(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.DB_zero_snd⟧. *The decomposition.* $D(h + \iota s)(x) = D(h + \iota s)(0) + S(x) + K(x)$ with $S(x)(\alpha, \zeta) = (0, -D_2B(x)\zeta)$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.Sop⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.Dfs'_eq_add⟧ of norm $\le \varepsilon$ for $x \in U_1$ small on level $1$, by Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9) (c) ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.exists_norm_Sop_le⟧, and $K(x)$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.Kop⟧ compact: the sum of finite-rank operators through $Q$ and through $P$ and of $\iota(Ds(x) - Ds(0))$ through the compact inclusion $W_1 \to W_0$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.Kop_isCompactOperator⟧.

*Fredholm with index $n - N$ at every small $x \in U_1$.* $D(h + \iota s)(0)$ is Fredholm of index $n - N$: $Dh(0)$ is (Proposition 3.1.10 (preprint 3.10) for the basic germ, §III.5) and $\iota Ds(0)$ is compact ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.isFredholm_Dfs⟧. A small perturbation of a Fredholm operator is Fredholm: if $v$ is a quasi-inverse of $u$ and $\|v\|\|s\| < 1$, then $(1 + vs)^{-1}v$ is a quasi-inverse of $u + s$ (Neumann series) ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · isFredholm_add_of_norm_lt⟧, with the same index ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · isFredholm_fredholmIndex_add_of_norm_lt⟧; a compact perturbation does not change the index ⟦Chapter1/FredholmIndex · fredholmIndex_add_isCompactOperator⟧. So $D(h + \iota s)(x)$ is Fredholm of index $n - N$ for *every* $x \in U_1$ small on level $1$, not only at solutions ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.exists_isFredholm_Dfs'⟧.

*Surjectivity: the compactness argument.* Let $N$ be a closed complement of $\ker D(h + \iota s)(0)$. Then $\ker D(h + \iota s)(x) \cap N = 0$ for $x \in U_1$ small on level $1$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.exists_ker_Dfs'_inf_eq_bot⟧. Otherwise there are $x_k \to 0$ in $E_1$ and unit vectors $(\alpha_k, \zeta_k) \in N$ in the kernels; the $W$-component of the equation reads $\zeta_k = D_1B(x_k)\alpha_k + D_2B(x_k)\zeta_k - \iota Ds(x_k)(\alpha_k, \zeta_k)$. $\alpha_k$ has a convergent subsequence ($P$ finite-dimensional); $Ds(x_k)(\alpha_k, \zeta_k)$ is bounded in $W_1$ (the derivatives near $0$ are uniformly bounded ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · IsSc1GermWith.exists_norm_deriv_le⟧), so its image in $W_0$ has a convergent subsequence; $D_1B(x_k)\alpha_k \to D_1B(0)\alpha$ by the joint continuity of the tangent map ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · IsSc1GermWith.tendsto_deriv⟧; and $\|D_2B(x_k)\| \to 0$. So $\zeta_k$ converges, $(\alpha_k, \zeta_k) \to (\alpha, z_0) \in N$ of norm $1$ in $\ker D(h + \iota s)(0)$ — a contradiction. (The text uses the Neumann series $\zeta_k = \sum(D_2B(x_k))^l z_k$; here $|\zeta_k - z_k| \le \varepsilon_k \to 0$ suffices.) Then $\dim\ker D(h + \iota s)(x) \le \dim\ker D(h + \iota s)(0) = n - N = \mathrm{ind}$, and an operator of finite index with $\dim\ker \le \mathrm{ind}$ is onto ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · HasFiniteIndex.surjective_of_finrank_ker_le⟧ — instead of the text's "$Df(x)|_N$ has index $0$ and is injective". *Zeros are on level $1$ and small there*: by the lifts of Theorem 3.5.1 (preprint 3.40), continuous at $0$ (§III.12); the text's detour through "bounded on level $2$, hence convergent on level $1$" is not needed.

### III.20 Local solvability: Lemma 3.5.10 (preprint 3.46), Theorem 3.5.6 (preprint 3.43), Lemmas 3.5.5, 3.5.8 (preprint 3.42, 3.44), Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47)

**Lemma 3.5.10 (preprint 3.46)** (Appendix 3.8.3 (preprint 3.8.2)). If $K' = \{(\alpha, L\alpha) : \alpha \in \widetilde K\}$, the image of the graph map ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · graphMap⟧ (with $(\alpha, L\alpha) \in \widetilde C \oplus W$ iff $\alpha \in \widetilde C$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · graphMap_mem_prod_iff⟧), has a good complement $Y'$ with respect to $\widetilde C \oplus W$, then $\widetilde K$ has a good complement $Z$ with respect to $\widetilde C$ such that $Z \times W$ is a good complement of $K'$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · lemma_3_5_10⟧. *Proof.* $Z' := \mathrm{pr}_P(Y')$ satisfies $P = \widetilde K + Z'$; choose $Z \subseteq Z'$ with $P = \widetilde K \oplus Z$ and a linear right inverse $\rho : Z' \to Y'$ of the projection (bounded, finite dimensions); for $|z| \le \varepsilon'|\alpha|$, $\varepsilon' = \varepsilon/(\|\rho\| + 1)$, $y = \rho(z) = (z, w) \in Y'$ has $|y| \le \varepsilon|(\alpha, L\alpha)|$, so $(\alpha, L\alpha) \in C'$ iff $(\alpha, L\alpha) + y \in C'$, i.e. $\alpha \in \widetilde C$ iff $\alpha + z \in \widetilde C$. The text's intermediate complement $Y_0$ is not needed. Good position of $K'$ gives good position of $\widetilde K$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · lemma_3_5_10_isInGoodPosition⟧.

**Projecting solutions into the quadrant.** A gap in the text's proof of Theorem 3.5.6 (3) (preprint 3.43): a solution $a = k + z$ ($k \in \widetilde K$, $z \in Z$) of $H(a) = 0$ is matched with the solution of Theorem 3.8.22 (preprint 3.78) by its uniqueness clause, which needs $k \in \widetilde C$ — not verified in the text. It holds because $z$ is small compared with $k$: from $H(a) = 0 = H(0)$ and $DH(0)k = 0$, $|DH(0)z| = o(|a|)$; $DH(0)$ is injective on $Z$, hence bounded below there ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · exists_norm_le_of_injOn⟧; so $|z| \le \varepsilon|k|$ near $0$, and the good-complement property gives $k \in \widetilde C$ from $k + z = a \in \widetilde C$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · exists_proj_mem_of_eq_zero⟧.

**The data of Theorem 3.5.6 (preprint 3.43).** The text states it for $g = \Phi_*(f + s)$; the formal statement is for any sc-smooth germ $g$ on $G$ with $g(0) = 0$ whose index-raised germ $g^1$ on the shifted germ is a basic germ with contraction map $B$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData⟧. Its solution germ $\delta$ (of the working form of $B$) takes values in $W_1$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.δ⟧, with $\mathrm{pr}_W g(a, \delta(a)) = 0$ on level $0$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.snd_g_sol⟧ and uniqueness in the contraction region ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.eq_δ⟧. Put $H(a) = \mathrm{pr}_Q\,g(a, \delta(a))$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.H⟧. *Regularity of $H$* (the text: "$H$ restricted to $U'_i$ is of class $C^{i-1}$"): for every $j \ge 1$ there is a relatively open $V \ni 0$ in $\widetilde C$ on which $H$ is $C^j$ — by Corollary 3.3.7 (preprint 3.34) for $g^1$, $\delta$ lifts to a $C^j$ map into $W_{2j}$, and $g : E_{2j} \to F_j$ is $C^j$ by Proposition 1.2.3 (preprint 1.18) for germs ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.H_contDiffOn⟧. A single $C^1$ neighbourhood $V_1$ carries the derivative data ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Deriv⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.exists_deriv⟧: $D\delta(0) : P \to W_1$, $L = \iota D\delta(0)$, and $DH(0) = \mathrm{pr}_Q Dg(0)(\alpha, L\alpha)$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Deriv.DH₀⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Deriv.hasFDerivWithinAt_H⟧, by the chain rule for $a \mapsto g(a, \delta(a))$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Deriv.hasFDerivWithinAt_comp⟧.

**Lemma 3.5.8 (preprint 3.44).** "Recalling from Lemma 3.9 that $D_2B(0) = 0$": Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9) gives this for $g^1$, on level $1$; the kernel of $Dg(0)$ is computed on level $0$. So first the $W$-component of $Dg(0)(0, \zeta)$ is $\zeta$ for $\zeta \in W_1$, by Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9) (b) for $g^1$, and then for all $\zeta \in W_0$ by density ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Deriv.snd_Dg_inr⟧. Differentiating $\mathrm{pr}_W g(a, \delta(a)) = 0$ at $0$ gives $\mathrm{pr}_W Dg(0)(\alpha, L\alpha) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Deriv.snd_Dg_graph⟧. Hence $Dg(0)(\alpha, \zeta) = 0$ iff $\zeta = L\alpha$ and $DH(0)\alpha = 0$: $\ker Dg(0) = \{(\alpha, D\delta(0)\alpha) : \alpha \in \ker DH(0)\}$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Deriv.ker_Dg_eq⟧, and $DH(0)$ is onto when $Dg(0)$ is ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Deriv.DH₀_surjective⟧.

**Theorem 3.5.6 (preprint 3.43)** ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · theorem_3_5_6⟧. Hypotheses: $\widetilde C$ a partial quadrant, $Dg(0)$ onto, $K' = \ker Dg(0)$ in good position to $\widetilde C \oplus W$, and *the regularity of Lemma 3.5.5 (preprint 3.42) as an explicit hypothesis*: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $r > 0$ such that every zero $x$ of $g$ in $U_0$ with $|x|_0 < r$ has $W$-component $\iota(w_1)$, $|w_1|_1 < \varepsilon$. *The choices* ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.exists_sol⟧: $\widetilde K = \ker DH(0)$ with $K'$ its graph (Lemma 3.5.8 (preprint 3.44)); the good complement $Z$ of $\widetilde K$ with $Z \oplus W$ good for $K'$ (Lemma 3.5.10 (preprint 3.46)); and the solution $\sigma : V' \to Z$ of $H(k + \sigma(k)) = 0$ with its uniqueness constants, from Theorem 3.8.22 (preprint 3.78) applied to $H$ (§III.17). Then $\tau(y) = (\sigma(v), \delta(v + \sigma(v)) - Lv)$ for $y = (v, Lv)$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.τ⟧ on $V = \pi^{-1}(V')$, relatively open in $K' \cap C'$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.V⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.V_relOpen⟧, with values in $Z \oplus W$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.τ_mem_Y⟧, so that $y + \tau(y) = (a, \delta(a))$ with $a = v + \sigma(v)$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.coe_add_τ⟧. (3), first half: $y + \tau(y)$ lies in $U_0$ and is a zero, since $\mathrm{pr}_W g(a, \delta(a)) = 0$ and $\mathrm{pr}_Q g(a, \delta(a)) = H(a) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.sol_mem_and_eq_zero⟧. Regularity: $\tau$ is $C^1$ on $V$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.τ_contDiffOn⟧ with $W$-component on level $1$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.snd_τ_mem_range⟧, and $D\tau(0)\eta = -D\delta(0)\pi(\eta) + D\delta(0)\pi(\eta) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.hasFDerivWithinAt_τ⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.fderivWithin_τ⟧ — (2). (3), second half ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.exists_eq_add_τ⟧: a zero $x = (a, w)$ near $0$ has, by the regularity hypothesis, $w$ on level $1$ and small there, so $w = \delta(a)$ by the uniqueness of the solution germ on level $1$, so $H(a) = 0$; write $a = k + z$ with the projection along $Z$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.projK⟧; then $k \in \widetilde K \cap \widetilde C$ (the gap above) and $z = \sigma(k)$ by the uniqueness clause of Theorem 3.8.22 (preprint 3.78); so $x = y + \tau(y)$ for $y = (k, Lk)$. *Clause (1), "$\tau$ is an sc-smooth germ"*, is proved level-wise: for every $k \ge 1$ there are a relatively open $V_k \subseteq V$ containing $0$ and a $C^k$ map $\tau_k : V_k \to E_k = P \oplus W_k$ with $\iota_k\tau_k = \tau$ on $V_k$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · ShiftedBasicData.Sol.exists_contDiffOn_level⟧ — the reading of Corollary 3.3.7 (preprint 3.34) and Definition 3.2.1 (ii) (preprint 3.25). The proof applies Theorem 3.8.22 (preprint 3.78) to $H$ at regularity $k$ on the neighbourhood of its regularity lemma, and identifies the $C^k$ solution $\sigma_k$ with $\sigma$ near $0$ through the uniqueness clause (3) of Theorem 3.8.22 (preprint 3.78) — which needs $\sigma$ continuous at $0$ with $\sigma(0) = 0$, so that $|\sigma(y)|$ is below the uniqueness constant for $y$ small; the text says nothing about why the solutions produced for the different $k$ coincide. The $W$-component $\iota\delta(a) - L\pi(y)$, $a = \pi y + \sigma(\pi y)$, lifts to $W_k$ through the $C^k$ lift of $\delta$ into $W_{2k}$, with $L$ the derivative of that lift at $0$ composed with the inclusion (uniqueness of the derivative of $\iota\delta$ at $0$). The full statement is ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · theorem_3_5_6_sc_smooth⟧; the statement without (1) is kept for Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54).

**Lemma 3.5.5 (preprint 3.42).** For the push-forward $g = \Phi_*(f + s)$ of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) — packaged as the data of Theorem 3.5.6 (preprint 3.43), $g$ on level $0$ with index-raised basic germ $(\Phi_*(f + s))^1$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · StabilityData.toShiftedBasicData⟧ — the regularity hypothesis holds: a zero $x'$ of $g$ in $\psi(U_0)$ with $|x'|_0$ small has $\psi^{-1}(x')$ a zero of $f + \iota s$, which by the lifts of Theorem 3.5.1 (preprint 3.40), continuous at $0$, lies on level $1$ and is small there; $\psi$ is linear on level $1$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · StabilityData.lemma_3_5_5⟧.

**Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47)** ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · theorem_3_5_11_sc_smooth⟧ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · theorem_3_5_11⟧. For a basic germ $f$ and the data of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) with $D(f + \iota s)(0)$ surjective and kernel $K$ in good position to $C = \widetilde C \oplus W$: a good complement $Y$, $V$ relatively open in $K \cap C$, $\sigma : V \to Y$ of class $C^1$ with values on level $1$, $\sigma(0) = 0$, $D\sigma(0) = 0$, the graph consisting of zeros of $f + \iota s$, an open $U'' \ni 0$ in which every zero lies on the graph, and for every $k$ a $C^k$ lift of $\sigma$ into $E_k$ near $0$. *Proof.* Theorem 3.5.6 (preprint 3.43) for $g = \Phi_*(f + s)$, with Lemma 3.5.5 (preprint 3.42) for its regularity hypothesis. Since the coordinate change is linear, $Dg(0) = \Psi \circ D(f + \iota s)(0) \circ \psi^{-1}$ (chain rule for sc-operators) ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · StabilityData.scDerivGerm_pushMap₀⟧, so surjectivity and the kernels correspond, $\ker Dg(0) = \psi(K)$; good position of $\psi(K)$ follows by Lemma 3.5.9 (preprint 3.45) (finite dimensionality of $K$ from Lemma 3.5.8 (preprint 3.44)); and the conclusion is transported back by $\psi^{-1}$: $Y = \psi^{-1}(Y')$, $V = \psi^{-1}(V')$, $\sigma = \psi^{-1}\tau\psi$. The text transports the solution set back with the nonlinear $\varphi$ and justifies the good position with the degeneracy index (Lemma 3.8.13 (preprint 3.70), Theorem 3.8.15 (preprint 3.72)); with $\psi$ linear neither is needed. The transport uses that $\widetilde C \oplus C$ is a partial quadrant for finite-dimensional $C$ ⟦Chapter3/LocalSolvability · IsPartialQuadrant.const_prod_univ⟧.

### III.21 The linearization at a zero (Proposition 3.6.5 (preprint 3.52)) and good position of a germ (Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53))

**Proposition 3.6.5 (preprint 3.52)** (text): if $f$ is an sc-Fredholm section of a tame strong bundle and $f(x) = 0$, then $Tf(x) : T_xX \to Y_x$ is an sc-Fredholm operator. Formalized in the chart of Definition 3.1.11 (preprint 3.11) centred at a zero $x_0$, with the level-$0$ Fredholm property ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearizationG · proposition_3_6_5_preprint⟧ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearizationG · proposition_3_6_5_local⟧: $Dg(0) : E_0 \to F_0$ and $Tf(x_0) = Dg(0)|_{T_0O} : T_0O \to K_0$ are Fredholm with $\mathrm{ind}\,Tf(x_0) = \mathrm{ind}\,Dg(0)$. Here $Dg(0)$ is the value at $0$ of a derivative family $Dg$ of the filling germ on a germ $G$ with $G_0 \subseteq U$, the same for every family and every germ (§III.4); the statement exposes $G$ and $Dg$, where the whole-chart statement ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · proposition_3_6_5_wholeChart⟧ has the derivative on $U$, a junk value for a germ. (The sc-Fredholm property of $Tf(x)$ would need $T_0O = Dr(0)E$ and $K_0 = \rho(0)F$ as sc-Banach spaces, not set up. The statements quantify the index as "$n - N$ for some natural numbers $n$, $N$", which every integer satisfies; the identification with $\dim P - \dim Q$ of the conjugating basic germ is made inside the proof, by `proposition_3_1_10_basic_level_zero`, and not exported.)

*Step (a): the derivative of the conjugation* ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearizationG · IsConjugateToBasicGerm.scDeriv_isFredholm⟧. Let $k = g - \iota s$, an sc¹-germ on $G_g \cap G_s$ (§III.48) with $k(0) = 0$ ($s(0) = g(0)$ in Definition 3.1.11 (preprint 3.11), $g(0) = \mathbf f(0) = 0$ by the filler) and $(\Psi(u, k(u)))_2 = h(\psi(u))$ near $0$. Linearizing needs: the chain rule for the germ $h \circ \psi$ on a germ small enough that $\psi$ maps it into the germ of $h$ (§III.1, `ScNbhdGerm.preimage`); the derivative of $u \mapsto \Gamma(u)k(u)$ at $0$, where $\Gamma(u) = (\Psi(u, \cdot))_2$ is a bounded operator ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · IsStrongBundleMap.fiberCLM⟧ and $D(\mathrm{pr}_{F'} \circ \Psi)(0, 0)(h, k) = \Gamma(0)k$ (as in §III.4) ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · IsStrongBundleMap.scDeriv_fiber_zero⟧, so the derivative is $\Gamma(0)Dk(0)$ because $k(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · IsStrongBundleMap.scDeriv_fiber_comp⟧ — for the germ $k$ by the chain rule with the whole-chart outer map $\mathrm{pr}_{F'} \circ \Psi$, the two derivative families agreeing at $0$ on $G_k \cap \psi^{-1}(G)$ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearizationG · IsStrongBundleIso.fiberCLM_comp_deriv_zero_eq_germ⟧, so no shrinking of the basic germ is needed at $0$; and the invertibility of $D\psi(0)$ and $\Gamma(0)$ (inverses $D\psi'(0)$, $\Gamma'(0)$, chain rule for $\psi' \circ \psi = \mathrm{id}$). Then $\Gamma(0)Dk(0) = Dh(0)D\psi(0)$, so $Dk(0) = \Gamma(0)^{-1}Dh(0)D\psi(0)$ is Fredholm with the index of $Dh(0)$: Fredholm operators and their index are stable under composition with isomorphisms on both sides ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · isFredholm_equiv_comp_equiv⟧, and $Dh(0)$ is Fredholm by Proposition 3.1.10 (preprint 3.10) for the basic germ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearization · proposition_3_1_10_basic_level_zero⟧. *Step (b)*: $Dg(0) = Dk(0) + \iota Ds(0)$ — for germs this is algebra at $0$: $g - \iota s$ has the derivative family $Dg - \iota Ds$ on $G_g \cap G_s$ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearizationG · IsSc1GermWith.sub_toBase_shift⟧ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearizationG · IsSc1GermWith.deriv_zero_eq_add_scPlus⟧ (on $U$ it was the uniqueness of the derivative within $U_1$ and density) — a compact perturbation since $\iota Ds(0)$ is the level-$0$ map of an sc⁺-operator ($s$ is an sc¹-germ into $F^1$ and $0$ is smooth); the index is unchanged (Part I, §I.9). *Step (c)*: Proposition 3.1.5 (preprint 3.5) (§III.4) transfers both to $Tf(x_0)$; it needs $g(0) = 0$.

**Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53)** (text): $(f, x)$ with $f(x) = 0$ is in good position if (1) $f'(x) : T_xX \to Y_x$ is onto and (2) if $d_X(x) \ge 1$, $\ker f'(x)$ is in good position to the partial cone $C_xX$ in $T_xX$. In the chart: Definition 3.1.24 (preprint 3.21) *inside a subspace* $T \subseteq E_0$ — $N \subseteq T$, interior of $N \cap C$ in $N$ nonempty, and a good complement $P \subseteq T$ of $N$ in $T$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · IsInGoodPositionIn⟧ (for $T = E_0$ it is Definition 3.1.24 (preprint 3.21) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · isInGoodPositionIn_top_iff⟧); and the germ is in good position if $Tf(0) = Dg(0)|_{T_0O}$ is onto and $\ker Dg(0)$ is in good position to $T_0O \cap C$ inside $T_0O = Dr(0)E$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · IsGoodPositionAt⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · IsGoodPositionAt.surjective_fillerRestrict⟧. Here $\ker Dg(0) \subseteq T_0O$, because $(1 - \rho(0))Dg(0)$ is injective on $\ker Dr(0)$ (filler (3)) and $(1 - \rho(0))Dg(0)Dr(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · IsFillerOf.ker_deriv_le_range⟧, and it is the kernel of $Tf(0)$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · IsFillerOf.map_ker_fillerRestrict⟧ — the text's "$\ker Tf(0) \oplus \{0\} = \ker Dg(0)$ by the properties of the filler". The case distinction $d_X(x) \ge 1$ is dropped: at an interior point the partial cone is the whole tangent space and (2) holds for every subspace. For a filling germ the condition is one on the operator $Dg(0)$ (the text's $f'(x)$ is defined through the filler at the centre only), which the chart notion takes as a parameter ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · IsGoodPositionAtGerm⟧ — the notion above is the case of the derivative on $U$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · isGoodPositionAt_iff_germ⟧ — and the two kernel facts hold with the same proofs ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · IsFillingGerm.ker_deriv_le_range⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · IsFillingGerm.map_ker_fillerRestrict⟧. For a section, $(f, x_0)$ is in good position if it has the data of Definition 3.1.11 (preprint 3.11) at $x_0$ and a derivative family $Dg$ of the filling germ on a germ inside $U$, with the germ in good position at $Dg(0)$ in that chart ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGerm⟧; as $Dg(0)$ does not depend on the family, "for some family" and "for every family" agree ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGerm.forall_isGoodPositionAtGerm⟧; it is then an sc-Fredholm germ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGerm.isScFredholmGerm⟧. The definition is in a chart, not in $T_xX$; the transport of $C_xX$ to $T_xX$ (Definition 2.5.8 (preprint 2.38)) is not formalized.

### III.22 Good position along a graph: Lemma 3.6.9 (preprint 3.55)

**Lemma 3.6.9 (preprint 3.55)** (text, Appendix 3.8.4 (preprint 3.8.3)): for $N$ in good position to $C$ with good complement $Y$, $V \subseteq N \cap C$ relatively open and $\tau : V \to Y_1$ of class $C^1$ with $\tau(0) = 0$, $D\tau(0) = 0$, for $v$ near $0$: (1) $v + \tau(v) \in C_1$; (2) $Y$ is a topological complement of $N_v = \{n + D\tau(v)n\}$; (3) for some $\gamma_v > 0$, $n \in C_x \iff n + y \in C_x$ for $n \in N_v$, $y \in Y$, $|y|_0 \le \gamma_v|n|_0$, $x = v + \tau(v)$. *Formalized* ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionOpen · lemma_3_6_9_preprint⟧ in the model $\mathbb R^n \oplus W$, with the core in coordinates for any $C$ cut out by functionals ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionOpen · lemma_3_6_9_core⟧: for $N$ in good position with good complement $Y$, **$Y$ closed**, and $\tau : N \to E$ with values in $Y$, $C^1$ on $N \cap C$, $\tau(0) = 0$, $D\tau(0) = 0$: there is $\rho > 0$ such that for $v \in N \cap C$, $|v| < \rho$, $v + \tau(v) \in C$ and $Y$ is a good complement of $N_v$ (the subspace $\{n + Ln\}$ for $L = D\tau(v)$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionOpen · graphSpace⟧) with respect to the partial quadrant $C_{v + \tau(v)}$.

*The added hypothesis.* The step "$D\tau(v)n \in Y$ for all $n \in N$" needs $Y$ closed: the derivative within $N \cap C$ at a point of unique differentiability of a map with values in a closed subspace takes values there, because the tangent cone of a subset of a closed subspace lies in it ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionOpen · tangentConeAt_subset_of_subset_closed⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionOpen · fderivWithin_mem_of_mapsTo⟧; for a non-closed algebraic complement it fails. The text's $Y = Y_0$ is a topological complement, hence closed. With $Y$ closed, (2) is linear algebra: $E_0 = N_v \oplus Y$ whenever $E_0 = N \oplus Y$ and $L$ takes values in $Y$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionOpen · graphSpace_isCompl⟧, and $N_v$ is closed (finite-dimensional) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionOpen · graphSpace_isClosed⟧.

*The estimate.* (1) is Lemma 3.8.13's (preprint 3.70) smallness: $|\tau(v)| \le \varepsilon|v|$ near $0$ and good position. For (3), take $\varepsilon_0 = \min(\varepsilon, 1)$ and the neighbourhood where $\|D\tau(v)\| \le \varepsilon_0/4$; then $\gamma = \varepsilon_0/4$ works *for every $v$*, since $\gamma(1 + \varepsilon_0/4) + \varepsilon_0/4 \le \varepsilon_0$ (the text: $\gamma_v(1 + \varepsilon) + \varepsilon < \gamma$, with $\gamma_v$ chosen after $v$). If $v \in N \cap W$, $C_x = C$ and the estimate transfers the good-complement property from $N$ to $N_v$. If $v \notin N \cap W$, no normal form is needed: if $\ell_i(v) = 0$ then $i \in \Sigma$, so $\ell_i$ vanishes on $Y$ (Lemma 3.8.8 (preprint 3.65)), and $\ell_i(v + \tau(v)) = 0$ iff $\ell_i(v) = 0$ (Lemma 3.8.13 (preprint 3.70)); so $\ell_i(h + y) = \ell_i(h)$ for every coordinate $i$ that defines $C_x$, and (3) holds for every $\gamma$. The text's cases $\dim N = 1$, $\dim N > 1$ are not needed, and the sc-level of the values of $\tau$ plays no role. The formal $\tau$ is given on all of $N \cap C$ rather than on a neighbourhood $V$; the conclusion is local.

### III.23 Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54): the linear part and the graph description of the solution set

**Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54)** (text): for an sc-Fredholm section $f$ of a tame strong bundle with $f(x) = 0$ and $(f, x)$ in good position there is an open $V \ni x$ such that $S = \{y \in V : f(y) = 0\}$ satisfies (1) $(f, y)$ is in good position at every $y \in S$, and (2) $S$ is a sub-M-polyfold with the structure of a manifold with boundary with corners. The proof: the linear part (surjectivity and good position transported from $T_xX$ to the chart and the push-forward); (3), the graph description of $S$ from Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47) for $\Psi_*(g) = h + t$; then Corollary 3.5.3 (preprint 3.41) and Lemma 3.6.9 (preprint 3.55) for (1), and Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) for (2). Formalized: the linear part and (3).

*The linear part* ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · theorem_3_6_8_linear⟧. In the chart, with the germ in good position and $(1 - Dr(0))E \subseteq E_0$ (the minimal subspace at the corner $0$; Proposition 2.5.3 (preprint 2.35) for the tame retraction $r$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · theorem_3_6_8_linear_of_isTameWith⟧ ⟦Chapter2/TameRetracts · proposition_2_5_3⟧): $Dg(0)$ is onto (Proposition 3.1.5 (preprint 3.5) for the filling germ, §III.4, from the surjectivity of $Tf(0)$), and $\ker Dg(0)$ is in good position to $C$ in $E$; only the filler at $0$ enters, and $r$ is whole-chart, so $Dr(0)$ is a genuine derivative. The latter is the *transport lemma*: for a bounded idempotent $\pi$ with $(1 - \pi)E_0$ inside the minimal subspace at $0$, good position inside $\pi E_0$ gives good position in $E_0$, with the good complement $P \oplus (1 - \pi)E_0$ and the constant $\varepsilon/(\|\pi\| + 1)$ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · IsInGoodPositionIn.isInGoodPosition⟧. Two points the text passes over: for $p = z + k$ with $|p| \le \varepsilon'|n|$ the $T_0O$-component is $z = Dr(0)p$, so $|z| \le \|Dr(0)\|\varepsilon'|n|$ — the splitting must be topological; and $\ker Dr(0) \subseteq E_0$ is used only as "the $\mathbb R^n$-coordinates of $k$ vanish, so $n + z + k \in C \iff n + z \in C$". *This inclusion is the only place where tameness enters.*

*$C' = T\psi(0)C$.* Not part of the data of a conjugation (§III.6); it is a theorem: for an sc-diffeomorphism $\psi$ between relatively open subsets of partial quadrants with $\psi(0) = 0$, $x \in C \iff D\psi(0)x \in C'$, by the one-sided sign argument for $\psi$ and $\psi^{-1}$ (Part II, §II.12) ⟦Chapter2/Boundary · IsSc1With.mapsTo_partialQuadrant⟧, since $C_0 = C$ at the corner ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · partialQuadrantAtWith_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGerm · scDeriv_mem_iff_of_inverse⟧.

*The push-forward.* $\Psi_*(g)(u') = (\Psi(\psi^{-1}(u'), g(\psi^{-1}(u'))))_2$ ⟦Chapter3/GermPushforward · pushforward⟧ is an sc-smooth germ on the germ of the points that $\psi^{-1}$ maps into the germ of $g$ ⟦Chapter3/GermPushforward · IsStrongBundleIso.pullGerm⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GermPushforward · IsStrongBundleIso.pushforward_isScSmoothGerm⟧ (chain rules with the whole-chart $\Psi$ and $\psi^{-1}$, §III.48), with $\Psi_*(g)(0) = 0$ and $D(\Psi_*g)(0) = \Gamma(0)Dg(0)D\psi(0)^{-1}$ for its explicit derivative family ⟦Chapter3/GermPushforward · IsStrongBundleIso.pushforwardDeriv_zero⟧ ($Dk(0) = \Gamma(0)Dg(0)$ for $k(u) = (\Psi(u, g(u)))_2$) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · IsStrongBundleIso.pushforward_linearization⟧; so it is onto ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · IsStrongBundleIso.pushforward_surjective⟧, its kernel $D\psi(0)(\ker Dg(0))$ is in good position to $C'$ (Lemma 3.5.9 (preprint 3.45)) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · IsStrongBundleIso.pushforward_ker_isInGoodPosition⟧, and it is Fredholm (transported from $Dg(0)$, Proposition 3.6.5 (preprint 3.52)) — the hypotheses of Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47), for *any* conjugating isomorphism ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · theorem_3_6_8_pushforward⟧, and in bundle form ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · theorem_3_6_8_pushforward_section⟧. The text instead obtains the Fredholm property of $D(h + t)(0)$ from Propositions 3.1.10 and 1.1.11 (preprint 3.10 and 1.10).

**The graph description** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTG · theorem_3_6_8_graph⟧. Let the charts of the bundle have tame base retractions (`htame`, the hypothesis of the first formalization; it cannot be met, and it is replaced by good position in a tame chart, §III.47), $f(x_0) = 0$, $(f, x_0)$ in good position. The proof, in the order of the Lean.

1. *The chart and the identities.* Take the chart of Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53) centred at $x_0$, filling germ $g$ on $G_g$, sc⁺-germ $t_0$ on $G_s$ and conjugation $\Psi$ covering $\psi$, with inverse $\Psi'$ covering $\psi'$. *Shrink the basic germ* to the germ $G''$ of the points of $G$ that $\psi'$ sends into $G_g \cap G_s$ level by level: same $\Psi$, $h$, $B$ and conjugation identity ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermShrink · ConjugationData.shrink⟧ (§III.48); below, $G$ denotes $G''$. There is an open $N_1 \ni 0$ on which $\mathbf f = g$ on $O$, zeros of $g$ in $U$ lie in $O$, and $\Psi(u, (g - \iota t_0)(u))_2 = h(\psi(u))$ (filler (1), (2) and the conjugation).
2. *The sc⁺-germ $t$.* $t(u') = \Psi[1](\psi'(u'), t_0(\psi'(u')))_2$ is an sc-smooth germ into $(Q \oplus W)^1$ on $G$: $t_0 \circ \psi'$ is a germ on the preimage of $G_s$, which contains $G = G''$, and the whole-chart $\Psi[1]$ is composed on the outside (§III.48). (With $t_0$ sc-smooth on $U$, $t$ was sc-smooth on all of $U'$.) On $U \cap N_1$, $\Psi_*(g)(\psi(u)) = h(\psi(u)) + \iota t(\psi(u))$, by the linearity of the fibre maps.
3. *Absorbing the $\mathbb R^N$-component.* The data of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) carry only the $W$-component of an sc⁺-germ. Let $t_Q$, $t_W$ be the two components of $t$, through the sc-operators $(q, w) \mapsto (q, 0)$ and $(q, w) \mapsto w$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFT · ScBanach.qPartOp⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFT · ScBanach.wPartOp⟧. Then $h' = h + (t_Q, 0)$ is again a basic germ with the same contraction $B$ (Definition 3.1.7 (preprint 3.7) constrains only the $W$-component), and with the data $\mathcal S$ of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) for $s = t_W$ (they exist, §III.18) one has $\mathcal S.\mathrm{fsum}\,h' = h' + \iota t_W = h + \iota t$.
4. *The hypotheses of Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47).* Tameness gives $(1 - Dr(0))E \subseteq E_0$ (Proposition 2.5.3 (preprint 2.35)), so the push-forward step applies: $D(\Psi_*g)(0)$ is surjective with kernel in good position. Since $\mathcal S.\mathrm{fsum}\,h' = \Psi_*(g)$ only on a neighbourhood $W'$ of $0$, the two derivatives at $0$ are identified by the uniqueness of the derivative within $G''_1$ and density of $E_1$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFT · scDeriv_eq_of_eqOn_nhds⟧; $\Psi_*(g)$ is a germ on the preimage of $G_g$, which contains $G''$.
5. *Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47) for $h + t$* gives $Y$, $V_0$, $\sigma$ (good complement, $C^1$, level $1$, $\sigma(0) = 0$, $D\sigma(0) = 0$), the graph consisting of zeros, and $U''$ in which every zero lies on the graph.
6. *The neighbourhoods* (the text does not say which $V$). Let $\Omega = U'' \cap W'$ and $V' = V_0 \cap (1 + \sigma)^{-1}(\Omega)$, relatively open in $K \cap C'$ because $v \mapsto v + \sigma(v)$ is continuous. Let $V_x = \{y \in \mathrm{dom}\,\varphi : \varphi(y) \in N_1,\ \psi\varphi(y) \in \Omega \cap G_0\}$, open and containing $x_0$ by continuity of $\psi \circ \varphi$. $D\sigma(0) = 0$ persists on the smaller $V'$ (unique differentiability at $0$ of $V'$).
7. *The conclusions.* $\psi \circ \varphi$ is injective on $V_x$ ($\varphi$ is, and $\psi' \circ \psi = \mathrm{id}$ on $U$). For $y \in V_x$: $f(y) = 0 \iff \mathbf f(\varphi y) = 0 \iff g(\varphi y) = 0 \iff (h + \iota t)(\psi\varphi y) = 0$ (items 1–2). If $f(y) = 0$, then $\psi\varphi(y)$ is a zero in $U'' \cap G_0$, hence $v + \sigma(v)$ with $v \in V_0$, and $v \in V'$ since $v + \sigma(v) \in \Omega$. Conversely, for $v \in V'$, $u = \psi'(v + \sigma(v))$ lies in $U \cap N_1$, $g(u) = 0$, so $u \in O$ and $u = \varphi(y)$ for some $y$, which lies in $V_x$ and is a zero.

So $\psi\varphi(\{y \in V_x : f(y) = 0\}) = \{v + \sigma(v) : v \in V'\}$. When this section was written, (1) and (2) were not formalized; they are now: the kernel and good position at the nearby zeros in §§III.32–III.33 (the surjectivity of $f'(y)$ under an added hypothesis), the data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) at every zero in §§III.34–III.36, and the theorem in §III.37. The graph description also records, since then, that $Y$ is closed, that the kernel is a smooth subspace and the level-wise lifts of $\sigma$ (Theorem 3.5.11 (1) (preprint Corollary 3.47)), and it is a corollary of the version with a good complement whose projection maps $C'$ into itself (§III.29).

**Theorems 3.6.10 and 3.6.11 (preprint 3.56 and 3.57), the step from the tangent germ property.** With $S = \{f = 0\}$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFT · solutionSet⟧: if $S \ne \emptyset$ has at every point regular data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) (§III.24), then $S$ is a sub-M-polyfold whose induced structure is tame and an $n$-dimensional manifold with boundary with corners (Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27)) ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFT · theorem_3_6_11_of_tangentGerm⟧; if $X$ has no boundary, the induced structure is without boundary, since $d_S \le d_X = 0$ (Lemma 2.4.6 (preprint 2.26)) ⟦Chapter2/SubMPolyfold · lemma_2_4_6⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFT · theorem_3_6_10_of_tangentGerm⟧. The first step of the text's proof — good position at every zero gives the tangent germ property — was not formalized when this section was written; the theorems with the text's hypotheses are in §III.37.

### III.24 The tangent germ property: Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) and Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27)

**Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25).** The data at $x \in M$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart⟧: a chart $\varphi : (U, x) \to (O, 0)$ onto a tame retract $(O, C, E)$; an $n$-dimensional smooth $N \subseteq E$ in good position to $C$ with a good complement $Y$, **$Y$ closed**; a relatively open $V \ni 0$ in $N \cap C$; $\delta : V \to E$ continuous with values in $Y$; (i) $\varphi(M \cap U) = \{v + \delta(v) : v \in V\}$; (ii) $\delta(0) = 0$, $D\delta(0) = 0$ and $\delta$ an sc^∞-germ, read as: for every $m$, $k$ a relatively open $V_{m,k} \ni 0$ in $N \cap C$ with $\delta(V_{m,k}) \subseteq E_m$ and $\delta : V_{m,k} \to E_m$ of class $C^k$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · IsScInftyGermAt⟧. This $k$-dependent reading is what the germ implicit function theorem delivers (§III.11); the reading "sc-smooth on one fixed germ" implies it by Proposition 1.2.3 (preprint 1.18) for germs ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · IsScSmoothGerm.isScInftyGermAt⟧, as does sc-smoothness on $V$ as a set ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · IsScSmooth.isScInftyGermAt⟧, so the theorem covers all three. $M$ has the property if $M \subseteq X_\infty$ and every point has such data ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.HasTangentGermProperty⟧. With these data: $N \cap C$ is a partial quadrant in $N$ (Proposition 3.1.25 (preprint 3.22)) ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.isPartialQuadrant⟧, and $N$, $Y$ are topological complements — this is where $Y$ closed is used — so the projection $\pi : E \to N$ along $Y$ is continuous ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.isTopCompl⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.π⟧; with $\Gamma(v) = v + \delta(v)$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.Γ⟧, $\pi\Gamma = \mathrm{id}$ and $\varphi(y) = \Gamma(\pi\varphi(y))$ for $y \in M \cap U$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.exists_eq_Γ⟧.

**Proposition 3.2.2 (preprint 3.26)** ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · proposition_3_2_2⟧. For an sc-diffeomorphism $\psi : X \to X'$ (sc-smooth on all of $X$ with sc-smooth inverse $\psi'$ on all of $X'$) and $\psi(M) = M'$, $M'$ has the $n$-dimensional tangent germ property iff $M$ has it. One direction suffices, applied to $\psi$ and $\psi'$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · proposition_3_2_2_image⟧: $\psi(M) \subseteq X'_\infty$ because sc-smooth maps preserve the levels, and the data at $\psi(x)$ are the transported chart $\varphi \circ \psi^{-1}$ ⟦Chapter2/DegeneracyIndex · transportChart⟧, whose model is that of $\varphi$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.transportChart_model_O⟧, with the same $N$, $Y$, $V$, $\delta$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.transport⟧: condition (i) is transported because $\varphi\psi^{-1}(\psi(M) \cap \psi(U)) = \varphi(M \cap U)$. As in the text; tameness of $X$ plays no role.

**Two gaps in the text's proof of Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27), and the conditions that close them** ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.IsRegular⟧. (a) The text asserts that $\delta$ is an sc^∞-germ near every $v_0 \in V$, by linearizing at $v_0$ and claiming that a certain map $N' \to N$ is an isomorphism. That needs $\delta$ differentiable at $v_0$, known only near $0$, and fails: in $X = \mathbb R^2$ take $M$ the graph of $g$ with $g(s) = s^2$ near $0$ and $g(s) = 4 + (s - 2)^{1/3}$ near $2$, a smooth embedded curve with a vertical tangent at $(2, 4)$; with $\varphi = \mathrm{id}$, $N$ the horizontal axis, $Y$ the vertical one and $\delta = g$, the data at $0$ satisfy Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25), $M$ has the tangent germ property (at $(2, 4)$ use a rotated chart), but $\delta$ is not differentiable at $2$ and the chart $\pi\varphi$ on all of $M \cap U$ is not smoothly compatible with the chart at $(2, 4)$. So condition (a): $\delta$ is sc-smooth on $V$ as a map on a set. (b) The text's $\Sigma = \{v + y \in C : v \in V, y \in Y\}$ is claimed relatively open in $C$, so that $t(v + w) = v + \delta(v)$ is defined near $0$ in $O$. False: $E = \mathbb R^3$, $C = [0, \infty)^3$, $N = \mathbb R(1, 1, 1)$, $Y = \{x + y - z = 0\}$ is a good complement, and $\pi(x, y, z) = (x + y - z)(1, 1, 1)$, so $\pi z \in C$ iff $x + y \ge z$; $\Sigma$ is not open at $(1, 0, 1)$ and points $(\varepsilon, 0, \varepsilon + \varepsilon^2) \notin \Sigma$ approach $0$. So condition (b): $\pi(O) \subseteq C$; then $\Sigma \cap O = \{z \in O : \pi z \in V\}$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.Sig⟧ is relatively open in $O$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.Sig_eq⟧. (A good complement with $\pi'(C) \subseteq N \cap C$ always exists, via the normal form, but then $M \cap U$ must be rewritten as a graph along it with the quadrant inverse function theorem, and the regularity problem of (a) returns; this is not done.)

**Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27)** ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · theorem_3_2_3_of_isRegular⟧. If $M \ne \emptyset$ has at every point data satisfying (a), (b), then:

*$M$ is a sub-M-polyfold* ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · theorem_3_2_3_isSubMPolyfold⟧, with the text's retraction. $t(v + w) = v + \delta(v)$, i.e. $t = \Gamma \circ \pi$ on $\Sigma \cap O$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.t⟧, is idempotent with image $\Gamma(V)$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.t_t⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.t_eq_self_of_mem⟧. It is sc-smooth: $\pi$ is a continuous linear map into a finite-dimensional space, hence sc-smooth (Corollary 1.2.5 (preprint 1.20)) ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.π_isScSmooth⟧; $\Gamma = \iota + \delta$ is sc-smooth on $V$ by (a) and because the inclusion of a smooth finite-dimensional subspace is sc-smooth, its level maps being the linear lifts $N \to E_m$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · finSmoothLift⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · isScSmooth_subtype_val⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.Γ_isScSmooth⟧; and for a retraction $R$ onto $\Sigma \cap O$, $t \circ R = \Gamma \circ \pi \circ R$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.t_isScSmoothRetractMap⟧. The text says nothing about either. On $U_0 = \varphi^{-1}(\Sigma \cap O)$, open ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.U₀⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.isOpen_U₀⟧, the map $s = \varphi^{-1} \circ t \circ \varphi$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.s⟧ is an sc-smooth retraction ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.s_s⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.s_isScSmoothOn⟧ onto $M \cap U_0$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.image_s⟧ — Definition 2.3.18 (preprint 2.20) at $x$.

*The charts $\pi \circ \varphi$.* On $M \cap U_0$, $y \mapsto \pi\varphi(y)$ is a chart with model $(V, N \cap C, N)$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.model⟧, a tame retract (the identity retraction) ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.model_isTameRetract⟧, with inverse $v \mapsto \varphi^{-1}(v + \delta(v))$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.chartInv⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.chart⟧, a finite-dimensional corner chart of dimension $n$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.chart_isCorner⟧ containing $x$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.mem_chart_source⟧. It is compatible with every chart $\varphi'|_{W \cap M}$ of the induced structure of Proposition 2.3.19 (preprint 2.21): the transition maps are $\varphi' \circ \varphi^{-1} \circ \Gamma$ and $\pi \circ \varphi \circ \varphi'^{-1}$, compositions of sc-smooth maps between retracts ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.Γ_isScSmoothRetractMap⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.π_isScSmoothRetractMap⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.chart_compatible_subChart⟧. So it is a chart of the induced structure — **Theorem 3.2.3 (2) (preprint 3.27)** ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_isChart⟧ ⟦Chapter2/SubMPolyfold · subPolyfold⟧; these charts are pairwise compatible by locality ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.tangentCharts_compatible⟧ and form an atlas ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.germAtlas⟧ equivalent to the induced one ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_atlas_equiv⟧, with tame models: **the induced structure is tame** ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_isTame⟧, and **sc-smoothly equivalent to an $n$-dimensional manifold with boundary with corners** ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_isManifoldWithCorners⟧. "Manifold with boundary with corners" belongs to Chapter 4; here it is an equivalent atlas of charts on relatively open subsets of $n$-dimensional partial quadrants ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.IsManifoldWithCorners⟧, whose transition maps are classically smooth because sc-smooth maps between finite-dimensional constant structures are $C^\infty$ ⟦Chapter1/ExpositionLemmas · isScSmooth_iff_contDiffOn_const⟧. Tameness of $X$ is not used: the text's "since $O$ is a tame retract, there exists … a tame sc-smooth retraction $r$" is Proposition 2.3.1 (1) (preprint 2.10) for any retract. *The degeneracy index*: since $\pi\varphi$ has a tame model, $d_M(y) = d_{N \cap C}(\pi\varphi(y))$ (Proposition 2.5.6 (preprint 2.36)) ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_degIndex⟧; Lemma 3.8.13 (preprint 3.70) relates this to $d_C(v + \delta(v)) = d_X(y)$ in the model $\mathbb R^n \oplus W$ only.

### III.25 Lemmas 3.7.2–3.7.4 (preprint 3.59–3.61): towards the basic germ criterion

**Lemmas 3.7.2 and 3.7.3 (preprint 3.59 and 3.60), abstract form.** In the proof of Theorem 3.7.1 (preprint 3.58) the family $L(b) = P_Y \circ Df(b)|_X : X \to Y$ is shown to be injective for $b$ near $0$ (Lemma 3.7.2 (preprint 3.59)) and to satisfy uniform lower bounds $|L(b)h|_m \ge c_m|h|_m$ (Lemma 3.7.3 (preprint 3.60)) — the hypotheses of Lemma 3.7.4 (preprint 3.61). Both are sequence arguments, and all they use of the compactness condition (2) of Theorem 3.7.1 (preprint 3.58) is a property of the family itself: if $b_j \to b$, $(h_j)$ is bounded in $X_m$ and $L(b_j)h_j \to 0$ in $Y_m$, then $(h_j)$ has a convergent subsequence in $X_m$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermCriterion · FamilyCompactness⟧. (For $L = P_Y \circ Df|_X$ it follows from (2), since $Df(b_j)h_j - L(b_j)h_j = (1 - P_Y)Df(b_j)h_j$ lies in the finite-dimensional smooth complement $A$ and is bounded on every level.) The two lemmas are formalized for an abstract family of sc-operators with jointly continuous level maps. *Lemma 3.7.2 (preprint 3.59)*: if $L(0)$ is injective, so is $L(b)$ for $b$ near $0$ — otherwise $b_j \to 0$ and unit vectors $h_j$ with $L(b_j)h_j = 0$ give, by the compactness property, a limit $h$ with $|h| = 1$ and $L(0)h = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermCriterion · lemma_3_7_2⟧. *Lemma 3.7.3 (preprint 3.60)*: on a compact parameter set $K$ on which every $L(b)_m$ is injective there is $c_m > 0$ with $c_m|h|_m \le |L(b)h|_m$ — otherwise $b_j \in K$ and unit $h_j$ with $|L(b_j)h_j|_m \to 0$ give, after subsequences, $b_j \to b \in K$, $h_j \to h$, $|h|_m = 1$, $L(b)h = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermCriterion · lemma_3_7_3⟧. The text's step "$P_Y \circ Df(b)|_X$ is an injective sc-Fredholm operator of index $0$, hence an sc-isomorphism" is needed only to feed Lemma 3.7.4 (preprint 3.61). The setting of Theorem 3.7.1 (preprint 3.58) (the splitting as coordinates, the complement $A$, the index computation), the strong bundle map $\Phi((b, x), (c, y)) = ((b, x), (c, L(b)^{-1}y))$ and the contraction estimate from condition (3) were not formalized when this section was written; they are now, in the coordinates of the text's proof, and there the hypotheses of the two lemmas are derived from those of the theorem (§III.38).


**Lemma 3.7.4 (preprint 3.61)** (quoted by the text from [25, Proposition 4.8] without proof). Let $V$ be a germ of relatively open neighbourhoods of $0$ in a partial quadrant $\widetilde C$ of a finite-dimensional $G$, $V \oplus E$ the product germ with levels $V_m \times E_m$ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · ScNbhdGerm.prodFull⟧, and $L(v) : E \to F$ sc-isomorphisms such that $\Phi(v, h) = L(v)h$ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · famMap⟧ is an sc-smooth germ on $V \oplus E$ and $c_m|h|_m \le |L(v)h|_m$ for $v \in V_0$. Then $\Lambda(v, k) = L(v)^{-1}k$ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · famInv⟧ is an sc-smooth germ on $V \oplus F$ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · lemma_3_7_4⟧. Operator-norm continuity of $v \mapsto L(v)$ is not assumed.

*sc⁰.* On level $m$, with $h_0 = L(v_0)^{-1}k_0$, $|L(v)^{-1}k - L(v_0)^{-1}k_0|_m \le c_m^{-1}(|k - k_0|_m + |(L(v_0) - L(v))h_0|_m)$, and $v \mapsto L(v)h_0$ is continuous ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · famInv_isSc0Germ⟧.

*Differentiability* ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · famInv_hasFDerivWithinAt⟧. The candidate is $D\Lambda(v, k)(\delta v, \delta k) = L(v)^{-1}(\delta k - D_1\Phi(v, L(v)^{-1}k)\delta v)$ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · famInvDeriv⟧. With $h = \Lambda(v, k)$ and $w = D\Lambda(v, k)(\delta v, \delta k)$, $\Lambda(v', k') - \Lambda(v, k) - w = L(v')^{-1}[(L(v) - L(v'))w] - L(v')^{-1}r$, where $r = \Phi(v', h) - \Phi(v, h) - D_1\Phi(v, h)\delta v = o(|\delta v|)$ is the remainder of $\Phi$ along $(\delta v, 0)$ with $h$ fixed, so that only the finite-dimensional variable moves. The term $(L(v) - L(v'))w$ is $o(|(\delta v, \delta k)|_1)$ *because the increment is measured on level $1$*: $w/|(\delta v, \delta k)|_1$ lies in the compact set $D\Lambda(v, k)(\iota(B_1))$ ($\iota : F_1 \to F_0$ compact), and $(L(v) - L(v'))u \to 0$ uniformly for $u$ in a compact set — a tube lemma for jointly continuous maps vanishing on $\{x_0\} \times K$ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · exists_forall_norm_le_of_isCompact⟧. Strong continuity plus the compactness of $F_1 \to F_0$ replace the operator-norm continuity; the differentiability of $\Lambda$ as a map $(V \oplus F)_0 \to E_0$ would need the latter and is not claimed.

*The tangent map and the induction.* $T\Lambda((v, k), (\delta v, \delta k)) = (\Lambda(v, k), \Lambda(v, \delta k - (T\Phi((v, \Lambda(v, k)), (\delta v, 0)))_2))$, a composition of $\Lambda$, $T\Phi$ and sc-operators (the projections ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · tangentBaseOp⟧ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · tangentInlOp⟧ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · tangentFiberOp⟧ and subtraction ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · ScBanach.subOp⟧); it is sc⁰ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · famInv_tangent_isSc0⟧, so $\Lambda$ is sc¹ ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · famInv_isSc1GermWith⟧; and if $\Phi$ is sc^{k+1}, then $\Lambda$ (sc^k by induction) and $T\Phi$ (sc^k) make $T\Lambda$ sc^k, so $\Lambda$ is sc^{k+1} ⟦Chapter3/InverseFamily · famInv_isSckGerm⟧. The sc¹ step uses no quadrant hypothesis; the induction uses the composition rules for germs.

### III.26 What the formalization taught us

1. **The germ implicit function theorem holds with the text's hypotheses**, but on shrinking germs: the solution germ is sc^k on the germ shifted by $2^k - 1$ levels. In the text's reading of germs (up to shrinking) this is the theorem; read with fixed germs, "if $f$ is sc-smooth so is its solution germ" holds for every $k$ on a germ depending on $k$. Every later "sc-smooth germ" (Definition 3.2.1 (ii) (preprint 3.25), Theorem 3.5.6 (1) (preprint 3.43), Theorem 3.5.11 (1) (preprint Corollary 3.47)) must be read the same way. Whether the shrinking is intrinsic is open.
2. **Theorem 3.3.3 (preprint 3.30) has a short proof**, which the text cites from [21]: an approximate fixed point and the $\tfrac12$-contraction on level $0$, at parameters where the level-$1$ operators exist.
3. **The contraction regions must be explicit** for "from the uniqueness we conclude $\delta^{(1)} = T\delta$" (Lemma 3.3.4 (preprint 3.31)) to be true.
4. **Several "immediate consequences" are proofs of their own**: the compactness of Theorem 3.1.13 (preprint 3.12) (not in Theorem 3.5.1 (preprint 3.40)), the induction of Theorem 3.5.1 (preprint 3.40) (lifts, bounds and continuity together), the sc-smoothness and regularizing property in Theorem 3.1.18 (preprint 3.15), the sc⁺ property in Proposition 3.1.10 (preprint 3.10), the projection of solutions into the quadrant in Theorem 3.5.6 (preprint 3.43).
5. **Three added hypotheses**: $Y$ closed in Lemma 3.6.9 (preprint 3.55) and Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25); and the two regularity conditions of Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27), each with a counterexample to the step of the text's proof it replaces.
6. **Tameness is nearly unused**: not in §3.1 at all, and in §3.6 only through $(1 - Dr(0))E \subseteq E_0$ and, for the tangent germ property of the solution set, the tameness of the model of the chart $\psi \circ \varphi$ (§III.27).
7. **Linear coordinate changes** in Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) make the text's degeneracy-index argument for Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47) unnecessary.
8. **The extreme-ray lemmas and the iterated tangent maps are not needed**: Proposition 3.1.25 (preprint 3.22) by a segment argument, the regularity in Theorems 3.8.15 and 3.8.22 (preprint 3.72 and 3.78) by bootstrapping, without shrinking.
9. **What remained of Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54)** when this list was written — the re-centring of good position at the nearby zeros, and the level-wise regularity of the solution map in the data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) at every zero — is done in §§III.31–III.37, with one added hypothesis for Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54); see §III.39.

### III.27 Tameness is carried by sc-diffeomorphisms, and by open subsets

The text never states that tameness is invariant under sc-diffeomorphisms, or that an open piece of a tame retract is a tame retract, but it uses both. In the proof of Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54) the chart $\psi \circ \varphi$ onto $\psi(O)$ must be a chart of the tame M-polyfold $X$ for Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) to apply; in the proof of Proposition 3.2.2 (preprint 3.26) it writes "$X' = \psi(X)$ is tame"; and wherever a chart is restricted to a smaller domain (the shrinking of the data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) in §§III.36–III.37) the model of the restricted chart must again be a tame retract. Both facts are proved here, with Definition 2.5.2 (preprint 2.34) as the formalization states it (Part II).

**The objects.** An sc-diffeomorphism between relatively open subsets $U \subseteq C$, $U' \subseteq C'$ of partial quadrants is a pair $\psi : U \to U'$, $\psi' : U' \to U$ of sc-smooth maps, each mapping its domain into the other's, inverse to each other on $U$ and on $U'$ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant⟧ (its inverse is again one ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.symm⟧). A retraction $r : U \to U$ is *tame* for a presentation $T$ of $C$ if (1) $d_C(r(x)) = d_C(x)$ for $x \in U$ and (2) at every smooth point $x$ of $O = r(U)$ on level $1$ the tangent space $T_xO = Dr(x)E$ has an sc-complement $A$ contained in the minimal subspace $E_x$ ⟦Chapter2/Tame · ScRetraction.IsTameWith⟧; it is tame if it is tame for every presentation ⟦Chapter2/Tame · ScRetraction.IsTame⟧, and $(O, C, E)$ is a tame retract if $O$ is the image of a tame retraction ⟦Chapter2/Tame · IsTameRetract⟧.

**The transported retraction.** For an sc-smooth retraction $r$ with domain $U$, put $r' = \psi \circ r \circ \psi' : U' \to U'$ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.transport⟧. It maps $U'$ into $U'$ because $\psi'(U') \subseteq U$, $r(U) \subseteq U$ and $\psi(U) \subseteq U'$; it is sc-smooth by the chain rule applied twice; and it is idempotent, $r'(r'(u')) = \psi(r(\psi'\psi(r(\psi'u')))) = \psi(r(r(\psi' u'))) = r'(u')$, since $\psi'\psi = \mathrm{id}$ on $U$ and $r$ is idempotent. Its image is $\psi(O)$ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.transport_retract⟧: $r'(u') = \psi(r(\psi'u'))$ with $r(\psi'u') \in O$; conversely, for $z \in O$, $r'(\psi z) = \psi(r(z)) = \psi(z)$.

*Condition (1).* The degeneracy index is preserved by $\psi$, $d_{C'}(\psi(u)) = d_C(u)$ for $u \in U$: this is Theorem 1.3.5 (preprint 1.25) ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.degIndex_eq⟧ ⟦Chapter2/DegeneracyIndex · theorem_1_3_5⟧. Then for $u' \in U'$: $d_{C'}(r'(u')) = d_C(r(\psi'u')) = d_C(\psi'u') = d_{C'}(\psi\psi'u') = d_{C'}(u')$, the middle step being condition (1) for $r$.

*The derivative of $r'$.* On level $0$, $D\psi'(\psi(x)) \circ D\psi(x) = 1$ for $x \in U_1$ and $D\psi(\psi'(y)) \circ D\psi'(y) = 1$ for $y \in U'_1$, by the chain rule for $\psi' \circ \psi = \mathrm{id}$ and $\psi \circ \psi' = \mathrm{id}$ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.deriv_inv_deriv⟧ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.deriv_deriv_inv⟧ ⟦Chapter2/DegeneracyIndex · scDeriv_comp_eq_self⟧. If $y \in U'_1$ and $\psi'(y) \in O$, then with $x = \psi'_1(y)$,
$$Dr'(y) = D\psi(x) \circ Dr(x) \circ D\psi'(y)$$
⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.scDeriv_transport⟧. The chain rule is applied to $\psi \circ (r \circ \psi')$ and then to $r \circ \psi'$ ⟦Chapter2/TameFaces · scDeriv_comp_apply_of_isScDomain⟧; the outer derivative of $\psi$ is taken at the level-$1$ point of $r \circ \psi'$ over $y$, and this point is $x$ because $r$ fixes $\psi'(y) \in O$. Without $\psi'(y) \in O$ the formula would involve $Dr$ at $\psi'(y)$ and $D\psi$ at the different point $r(\psi'(y))$.

*Sc-isomorphisms carry sc-splittings.* Let $D : E \to E'$ be an sc-operator with an sc-operator inverse $D'$. Its level maps are topological linear isomorphisms $E_m \cong E'_m$ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · scIsoLevel⟧ (inverse to each other by the injectivity of the inclusions into level $0$); they carry $A_m = A \cap E_m$ onto $(DA)_m$ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · levelSub_map_eq⟧ and smooth points to smooth points ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · mem_smoothLvl_of_isScOperator⟧. Hence an sc-subspace $A$ (closed, with the smooth points of $A_m$ dense in $A_m$ for every $m$) is carried to the sc-subspace $DA$: $DA = (D')^{-1}(A)$ is closed, and the image of the closure of the smooth points of $A_m$ lies in the closure of the image ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScSubspace.map⟧. An sc-splitting $E = F \oplus A$ (sc-subspaces, complementary on every level) is carried to the sc-splitting $E' = DF \oplus DA$, since an isomorphism of each level carries complements to complements ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScSplitting.map⟧.

*Condition (2)* ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.isTameWith_transport⟧. Let $T$, $T'$ be presentations of $C$, $C'$, $r$ tame for $T$, and $y$ a smooth point of $\psi(O)$ on level $1$. Then $x = \psi'_1(y)$ lies on level $1$ of $O$ (if $y = \psi(z)$ with $z \in O$, then $\psi'(y) = z$) and is smooth, because sc-smooth maps carry smooth points to smooth points. Tameness of $r$ at $x$ gives an sc-splitting $E = T_xO \oplus A$ with $A \subseteq E_x$. Because $x$ and $y$ are smooth points, $D\psi(x)$ and $D\psi'(y)$ are sc-operators (the remark after Proposition 1.2.1 (preprint 1.16)) ⟦Chapter1/Properties · IsSc1With.scOperator_deriv⟧ — at a point that is only on level $1$ they need not be — and they are inverse to each other. From the formula for $Dr'(y)$ and $D\psi'(y)D\psi(x) = 1$: $T_y\psi(O) = \mathrm{range}\,Dr'(y) = D\psi(x)(\mathrm{range}\,Dr(x)) = D\psi(x)(T_xO)$. So $E' = T_y\psi(O) \oplus D\psi(x)A$ is an sc-splitting. Finally $D\psi(x)A \subseteq E'_y$: the derivative of an sc¹-map between relatively open subsets of partial quadrants maps the minimal subspace $E_x$ into $E'_{\psi(x)}$ (the one-sided sign argument, and density) ⟦Chapter2/DegeneracyIndex · IsSc1With.mapsTo_minimalSubspace⟧, which needs $\psi(U) \subseteq C'$, and $\psi(x) = y$. Since $T'$ was arbitrary and the presentation $T$ of $C$ is available, the transported retraction of a tame retraction is tame ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.isTame_transport⟧, and **the image $(\psi(O), C', E')$ of a tame retract under an sc-diffeomorphism is a tame retract**, with retraction $\psi \circ r \circ \psi^{-1}$ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.isTameRetract_image⟧.

**Open subsets.** For an open $W \subseteq E_0$, the restriction of $r$ to $U \cap r^{-1}(W)$ is an sc-smooth retraction onto $O \cap W$ (Proposition 2.3.1 (1) (preprint 2.10)) ⟦Chapter2/Retracts · ScRetraction.restrict⟧. It is tame if $r$ is ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · ScRetraction.restrict_isTame⟧: condition (1) is inherited pointwise; for (2), at a smooth point $x$ of $O \cap W$ on level $1$ the derivative of the restricted retraction is $Dr(x)$, because the two maps agree on the smaller relatively open domain, which contains $x$ since $r(x) = x \in W$, and the derivative at a level-$1$ point depends only on the map near the point ⟦Chapter3/LinearizationCharts · scDeriv_eq_of_eqOn_relOpen⟧; so the complement $A$ of $r$ at $x$ serves. Hence $O \cap W$ is a tame retract whenever $O$ is ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · IsTameRetract.inter_isOpen⟧.
