The isomorphism $S = D\psi'(x_1) : E'_0 \cong E_0$ (inverse $D\psi(z_{0,1})$, ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyGraph · IsStrongBundleIso.derivInvEquiv⟧) carries $C'_{v + \sigma(v)}$ onto $C_{z_0}$ by the corresponding-quadrants lemma, so Lemma 3.5.9 (preprint 3.45) ⟦Chapter3/GoodPosition · IsInGoodPosition.map⟧ gives $S(N_v) = \ker Dg(z_{0,1})$ (by (a), (b)) in good position to $C_{z_0}$ in $E$; by (c) and the restriction lemma it is in good position inside $T_{z_0}O$.

**Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) at the nearby zeros, the kernel, for the section** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · theorem_3_6_8_nearby_kernel⟧. *Statement.* For a section $f$ of a strong bundle whose charts have tame base retractions, $f(x_0) = 0$ and $(f, x_0)$ in good position: the data of the graph description, with the chart $c$ centred at $x_0$, its filling germ $g$ with a derivative family and the sc⁺-germ $t_0$ exposed (and $K$ smooth, $Y$ closed, the lifts), and an open $V_{x,2} \subseteq V_x$ containing $x_0$, such that for every zero $y \in V_{x,2}$: $\mathbf f = g$ on $O$ near $\varphi(y)$, $g(\varphi(y)) = 0$, and with $z_0 = \varphi(y)$ the conclusions (i), (a)–(d) of the chart statement hold, with $z_{0,1}$ on level $1$ of the germ of $g$. *Proof.* Tameness gives $(1 - Dr(0))E \subseteq E_0$ (Proposition 2.5.3 (preprint 2.35), ⟦Chapter2/TameRetracts · proposition_2_5_3⟧, at the smooth corner point $0$ of the tame base retraction; §III.23). The conjugation data are first shrunk to $G''$ (§III.48), so that the chart statements apply; they give $N_1$, $N_2$, $N_3$, with $\psi(U \cap N_2) \subseteq G''_0$; put $V_x = \varphi^{-1}(N_2)$ and $V_{x,2} = \varphi^{-1}(N_3)$ within the chart domain, both open by continuity of $\varphi$. On $V_x$, $\mathbf f(\varphi(y)) = g(\varphi(y))$ (filler (1) on $N_1$), so $f(y) = 0 \iff g(\varphi(y)) = 0 \iff (h + t)(\psi\varphi(y)) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.isZeroAt_iff⟧; $\psi \circ \varphi$ is injective on $V_x$; and the image of the zero set is the graph. For a zero $y \in V_{x,2}$, $\varphi(y) \in O \cap N_3$ is a zero of $g$, and the kernel statement applies. The hypotheses are those of the graph description: **the kernel part of Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) needs no added hypothesis**, and of $f$ only that it is a section (the regularity enters through the filling germ $g$, at $0$ and at the lifts $z_{0,1}$ of the nearby zeros, which lie in its germ).

### III.33 Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) under the filler condition at the nearby zeros, and the λ-extension

At a zero $y$ near $x_0$, with $z_0 = \varphi(y)$ in the chart at $x_0$, §III.32 gives the kernel condition (b) of the assembly step of §III.31 and the surjectivity of the *filled* $Dg(z_0) : E \to F$. Condition (a) asks for more: that $Tf(z_0) = Dg(z_0)|_{T_{z_0}O}$ maps onto $K_{z_0} = \rho(z_0)F$ (or onto a dense part of it). The text obtains it from Corollary 3.5.3 (preprint 3.41). This section shows exactly what is missing, proves Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) under the **added hypothesis** that closes the gap, and records the formalized part of an attempt to close it without that hypothesis (Findings 100, 105–107).

**The block form at a zero.** With respect to $E = T_{z_0}O \oplus \ker Dr(z_0)$ and $F = K_{z_0} \oplus \ker\rho(z_0)$ ($Dr(z_0)$ and $\rho(z_0)$ are idempotent),
$$Dg(z_0) = \begin{pmatrix} A & B \\ 0 & C \end{pmatrix}, \qquad A = Tf(z_0), \quad C = (1 - \rho(z_0))Dg(z_0)|_{\ker Dr(z_0)} : \ker Dr(z_0) \to \ker\rho(z_0).$$
The lower left block vanishes at a zero: $Dg(z_0)Dr(z_0) = D(\mathbf f \circ r)(z_0)$ (the bridge, §III.31) takes values in $K_{z_0}$ (the values of the linearization at a zero, §III.31). If $Dg(z_0)$ is onto, $C$ is onto, and (Finding 100) $b \mapsto [Bb]$ induces an isomorphism $\ker C \cong \mathrm{coker}\,A$: so **$Tf(z_0)$ is onto if and only if $C$ is injective** — condition (3) of Definition 3.1.4 (preprint 3.4) at $z_0$. Definition 3.1.4 (preprint 3.4) imposes it at $0$ only, and it does not propagate: $u \mapsto Dg(u)$ is continuous for the strong operator topology, not in norm, and a strongly continuous family of surjections may pass from an isomorphism to a non-injective operator. In index terms: Corollary 3.5.3 (preprint 3.41) gives $\mathrm{ind}\,Dg(z_0) = \dim K$ at all nearby zeros, $\mathrm{ind}\,Dg(z_0) = \mathrm{ind}\,A + \mathrm{ind}\,C = \dim\ker A$ when $C$ is onto, and $\mathrm{ind}(f, y) = \mathrm{ind}\,A = \dim K - \dim\mathrm{coker}\,A$; so $\mathrm{coker}\,Tf(z_0) = 0$ is the local constancy $\mathrm{ind}(f, y) = \mathrm{ind}(f, x_0)$ along the solution set — the index stability of Proposition 6.2.2 (preprint 6.2) of the text, whose proof uses the same step ("$F'$ surjective" for the intrinsic linearization, from the implicit function theorem for the filled one). The text's "(4) follows from Corollary 3.41 and Lemma 3.55" therefore has a gap for the surjectivity of $f'(y)$; the same sentence stands in the book's arXiv version (Theorem 3.13 there, with Definition 3.13, the filling, still imposing (3) at $0$ only; Finding 157). Formalized of this paragraph is the direction used:

**Proposition 3.1.5 (1) (preprint 3.5) at a zero of a filler** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurj · exists_range_scDeriv_eq_ρ_of_normal_injective⟧. *Statement.* Let $U$ be relatively open in a partial quadrant, $R$ a strong bundle retraction on $U \triangleleft F$, $\mathbf f$ with $\mathbf f \circ r$ sc¹ and $(x, \mathbf f(x)) \in K$ on $O$, $g$ sc¹, $z$ on level $1$ with $\iota z \in O$, $\mathbf f(\iota z) = 0$, $\mathbf f = g$ on $O$ near $\iota z$. If $Dg(z)$ is surjective and $(1 - \rho(z))Dg(z)$ is injective on $\ker Dr(z)$ (in the form: $Dr(z)b = 0$ and $\rho(z)Dg(z)b = Dg(z)b$ imply $b = 0$), then for every $k \in F$, $\rho(z)k = Dg(z)x$ for some $x \in T_zO$. For a germ $g$, with $Dg(z)$ the value of a derivative family at a level-$1$ point of its germ, the proof is the same with the bridge for germs (§III.31) ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · exists_range_deriv_eq_ρ_of_normal_injective_germ⟧. *Proof.* Write $Dg(z)e = \rho(z)k$, $a = Dr(z)e$, $b = e - a \in \ker Dr(z)$ (idempotence). $Dg(z)a = D(\mathbf f \circ r)(z)e$ lies in $K_z$ (bridge and values at a zero), so $\rho(z)Dg(z)a = Dg(z)a$; then $\rho(z)Dg(z)b = \rho(z)(\rho(z)k - Dg(z)a) = \rho(z)k - Dg(z)a = Dg(z)b$ ($\rho(z)$ is linear and idempotent on the fibre). So $b = 0$, $e = a \in T_zO$. The converse direction and the index identity above are not formal statements; they explain why the hypothesis is the right one.

**The added hypothesis** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGermFilled⟧. $(f, x_0)$ is in good position *with a filler satisfying condition (3) at the nearby zeros* if $x_0$ is smooth and there are the data of Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53) in the chart form (a strong bundle chart centred at $x_0$, tame since §III.47, a filling germ $g$ of $\mathbf f$ with a derivative family $Dg$ on a germ $G$ inside $U$, a local sc⁺-germ $s$ with $s(0) = g(0)$ and $g - s$ conjugated to a basic germ, the germ in good position at $Dg(0)$) together with an open $N \ni 0$ such that at every level-$1$ point $z_1$ *of $G$* with $\iota z_1 \in O \cap N$ and $g(\iota z_1) = 0$, $(1 - \rho(\iota z_1))Dg(z_1)$ is injective on $\ker Dr(z_1)$. The condition is imposed only where $Dg$ is defined, which is where the lifts of the nearby zeros lie (§III.32); the whole-chart form imposed it at every level-$1$ point over $O \cap N$ and implies it ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGermFilledWholeChart.isGoodPositionGermFilled⟧. It implies Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53) ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGermFilled.isGoodPositionGerm⟧ (forget $N$). According to the docstring, the fillers of the applications satisfy (3) at every point; Definition 3.1.4 (preprint 3.4) as stated does not ask for it. It is recorded as an added hypothesis in the docstrings and in the Findings (items 100, 153).

**Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54)** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · theorem_3_6_8_one⟧. *Statement.* Let $f$ be an sc-Fredholm section of a strong bundle whose charts have tame base retractions, $f(x_0) = 0$, and $(f, x_0)$ in good position with a filler satisfying condition (3) at the nearby zeros. Then there is an open $V_x \ni x_0$ such that $(f, y)$ is in good position (Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53), `IsGoodPositionGerm`) at every zero $y \in V_x$. *Proof.*

1. In the chart $c$ at $x_0$ with the filling germ $g$: $(1 - Dr(0))E \subseteq E_0$ from tameness (Proposition 2.5.3 (preprint 2.35)); the germ $G$ of the condition is intersected with the germ of $g$, and the basic germ is shrunk to the points that $\psi'$ maps into this intersection and into the germ of $s$ (§III.48); then the graph description in the chart and the kernel statement of §III.32 give $N_1$, $N_2$, $N_3$. Put $V_x = \varphi^{-1}(N_3 \cap N)$ in the chart domain, with $N$ the neighbourhood of the added hypothesis.
2. For a zero $y \in V_x$: $z_0 = \varphi(y) \in O \cap N_3$, $\mathbf f(z_0) = 0$, and $g(z_0) = \mathbf f(z_0) = 0$ by filler (1) on $N_1$; $\mathbf f = g$ on $O$ near $z_0$. The kernel statement gives the level-$1$ point $z_{0,1}$, on level $1$ of $G$, $Dg(z_{0,1})$ surjective, $\ker Dg(z_{0,1}) \subseteq T_{z_0}O$, and $\ker Dg(z_{0,1})$ in good position to $C_{z_0}$ inside $T_{z_0}O$.
3. $y$ is a smooth point, because $f$ is regularizing ⟦Chapter3/TameGermsG · IsScFredholmSection.mem_smoothSet_of_isZeroAt⟧, so $(f, y)$ is an sc-Fredholm germ (Definition 3.1.16 (preprint 3.14)); and $\mathbf f \circ r$ is sc¹ for an sc-smooth section ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurj · StrongBundle.IsScSmoothSection.isSc1_principal_comp_r⟧.
4. Apply the assembly step of §III.31 in the chart $c$ at $z_{0,1}$ with $g$, in its germ form, the closed range in the tame chart at $y$ coming from Proposition 3.6.5 (preprint 3.52) for germs ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · StrongBundle.isGoodPositionGermTame_of_chart_dense⟧. *Condition (a)*: the added hypothesis at $z_{0,1}$ (with $\iota z_{0,1} = z_0 \in O \cap N$, $g(z_0) = 0$) is the injectivity of $C$, and Proposition 3.1.5 (1) (preprint 3.5) at $z_0$ turns the surjectivity of $Dg(z_{0,1})$ into $\rho(z_0)k \in Dg(z_{0,1})(T_{z_0}O)$ for every $k$, smooth or not. *Condition (b)*: $\ker Dg(z_{0,1}) \cap T_{z_0}O = \ker Dg(z_{0,1})$ by the tangency (c), and the good position is (d).

So Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) holds under condition (3) of the filler at the nearby zeros; the kernel half holds with the text's hypotheses (§III.32). When this section was written, **the surjectivity of $f'(y)$ at the nearby zeros with Definition 3.1.4 (preprint 3.4) as stated was open in the formalization**; it is proved in §§III.41–III.43 by another argument, and the condition (3) at the nearby zeros turns out to be a consequence of good position (§III.45). The global theorems are not affected: they assume good position at every zero, which supplies at each $x_0$ the nearby good position that the regularity argument needs (§III.36–§III.37, Finding 156).

**The λ-extension: what the perturbation argument of Proposition 6.2.2 (preprint 6.2) gives.** The text's device for index stability (Proposition 6.2.2 (preprint 6.2)) is to add finitely many parameters $\lambda$ multiplying sc⁺-sections, so that the extended problem is surjective, and to apply the implicit function theorem to it. In the chart at $x_0$ it can be set up without new bundle isomorphisms: for a finite-dimensional $\Lambda_0 \subseteq F_\infty$, the sc⁺-germ $\tilde e(\lambda, u) = \rho(r(u))(\lambda_1(\pi_K\psi(u)) + \hat\jmath(\lambda_2))$ (Finding 105). Two points are not addressed by the text, and the first is formalized.

*The obstruction at the corner* ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · mem_lineality_of_goodComplement_prod⟧. Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47) for the extended problem needs its kernel in good position to the extended partial quadrant at the corner. Let $C \subseteq V$ contain $0$ and be closed under nonnegative scaling, $N \subseteq V$ a subspace, and $P \subseteq \mathbb R \times V$ a subspace with the $\varepsilon$-condition of Definition 3.1.24 (preprint 3.21) for $\mathbb R \times N$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · prodSub⟧ with respect to $\mathbb R \times C$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · prodCone⟧. Then every $p = (\mu, y) \in P$ has $y \in C$ and $-y \in C$. *Proof.* $n = (1, 0)$ lies in $\mathbb R \times N$ and in $\mathbb R \times C$, $|n| = 1$; for $t = \pm\varepsilon/|p|$, $|tp| \le \varepsilon|n|$, so $n + tp \in \mathbb R \times C$, i.e. $ty \in C$, and scaling by $1/|t|$ gives $\pm y \in C$. Hence a good complement projects into the lineality space $C \cap (-C)$, and $\mathbb R \times N$ is in good position only if every $v \in V$ is $n + y$ with $n \in N$, $y \in C \cap (-C)$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · sup_lineality_eq_top_of_goodComplement_prod⟧. For the naive extension (parameters multiplying smooth vectors with a factor vanishing at $0$) the kernel is $\mathbb R \times K$, so good position fails unless $K$ maps onto all boundary coordinates — the text's "has a complement contained in $T^R$" is exactly the statement that the complement lies in the lineality space.

*The fix: a tilted kernel* ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · isInGoodPosition_extendedKernel⟧. In $(\Lambda \times P_n) \oplus W$ with the partial quadrant $(\Lambda \times \widetilde C) \oplus W$ (the parameters $\lambda$ unconstrained), let $K$, $Y'$ be subspaces of $P_n \oplus W$ and $j : \Lambda \to P_n \oplus W$ linear with $K \cap j(\Lambda) = 0$, $(K + j(\Lambda)) \oplus Y' = P_n \oplus W$, $Y'$ in the lineality space of $\widetilde C \oplus W$ ($v + y \in \widetilde C \oplus W \iff v \in \widetilde C \oplus W$ for $y \in Y'$), and $K + j(\Lambda)$ containing an interior point of $\widetilde C \oplus W$. Then the kernel $\widetilde N = \{((\lambda, a), w) : (a, w) + j(\lambda) \in K\}$ of $((\lambda, a), w) \mapsto D(h + t)(0)((a, w) + j(\lambda))$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · extendedKernel⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · extProj⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · extParam⟧ is in good position. *Proof.* Choose a complement $\Lambda_c$ of $\ker j$; every element of $j(\Lambda)$ is $j(\mu)$ with $\mu \in \Lambda_c$. *Interior*: if $k_0 + j(\mu_0)$ is interior, $n_0 = ((-\mu_0, k_0 + j(\mu_0)))$ lies in $\widetilde N$ and its $(P_n \oplus W)$-part is interior, an open condition. *The complement* $\Lambda_c \times Y'$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyPerturb · extendedCompl⟧: an element of both has $(a, w) \in (K + j(\Lambda)) \cap Y' = 0$, then $j(\lambda) \in K \cap j(\Lambda) = 0$, $\lambda \in \ker j \cap \Lambda_c = 0$; and every $x = ((\lambda, a), w)$ splits by decomposing $(a, w) + j(\lambda) = k + j(\mu) + y$. *The constant*: since $Y'$ lies in the lineality space, adding an element of $\Lambda_c \times Y'$ never changes membership in the quadrant, so the $\varepsilon$-condition holds for every $\varepsilon$ (the proof takes $\varepsilon = 1$). The directions $j(\Lambda) \subseteq T_0(\psi(O)) \cap E'_\infty$ with $K + j(\Lambda) + E'_0 = E'$ exist by tameness ($T_0O + E_0 = E$) and would be realized by the sc⁺-perturbations $\rho(r(u))Dg(0)D\psi^{-1}(0)j(\lambda)$.

*Why the argument does not close the gap* (Findings 105–106). Carried through (not formalized: the extended basic germ and its sc⁺-perturbation, its derivative at the zeros, Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47) for it, and the tangency argument), the argument gives for each finite-dimensional $\Lambda_0 \subseteq F_\infty$ a neighbourhood $V(\Lambda_0)$ of $x_0$ with $\rho(z_0)\Lambda_0 \subseteq \mathrm{range}\,Tf(z_0)$ at every zero in $V(\Lambda_0)$. The assembly step at a fixed zero $y$ needs $\rho(z_0)\hat k$ in the range for *all* smooth $\hat k$, and $V(\Lambda_0)$ comes from Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47) for the $\Lambda_0$-extended problem with no control in $\Lambda_0$: the radii of Theorems 3.5.1, 3.5.6, 3.5.11 (preprint 3.40, 3.43, 3.47) would have to be uniform in the finite-dimensional parameter space, which the statements do not provide; $\mathrm{coker}\,Tf(z_0)$ is finite-dimensional and spanned by smooth vectors, but the spanning set depends on $y$. The formalization of this route was stopped there; §§III.41–III.43 take another one.

### III.34 Theorem 3.6.8 (2) (preprint 3.54) at the centre: the data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) for the solution set at $x_0$

The text ends the proof of Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54) with $\psi \circ \varphi(\{y \in V : f(y) = 0\}) = \{v + \sigma(v) : v \in V'\}$ and concludes that the solution set has the $d$-dimensional tangent germ property, $d = \dim\ker f'(x)$, so that Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) applies. Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) asks for much more than the graph identity (§III.24): a chart of $X$ centred at the point onto a *tame* retract, a *smooth* finite-dimensional subspace $N$ in good position with a *closed* good complement $Y$, a relatively open $V \ni 0$ in $N \cap C$, and $\delta : V \to Y$ continuous with the graph identity, $\delta(0) = 0$, $D\delta(0) = 0$ within $N \cap C$, and the sc$^\infty$-germ property in the $k$-dependent reading ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · IsScInftyGermAt⟧. This section assembles all of it at the centre $x_0$ from the graph description of §III.29, adding the three facts the text leaves silent: the chart is $\psi \circ \varphi$ and it is a chart of $X$; its model $\psi(O)$ is a tame retract; and $K = \ker D(h + t)(0)$ consists of smooth points. With the projecting complement of §III.29 the data also satisfy condition (b) of the regularity conditions. The data at the other zeros and condition (a) come in §§III.36–III.37.

**The sc$^\infty$-germ property from the level-wise lifts.** Theorem 3.5.11 (1) (preprint Corollary 3.47) gives, for every $k \ge 1$, a relatively open $V_k \ni 0$ in the cone and a $C^k$ map $\sigma_k : V_k \to E_k$ with $\iota_k\sigma_k = \sigma$; Definition 3.2.1 (ii) (preprint 3.25) asks, for every level $m$ and every $k$, for a relatively open $V_{m,k} \ni 0$ on which $\sigma$ takes values in $E_m$ and is $C^k$ into $E_m$. Given $m$ and $k$, take $j = \max(m, k) + 1$, $V_{m,k} = V_j$, and compose $\sigma_j$ with the inclusion $E_j \to E_m$, a continuous linear map compatible with the inclusions into $E_0$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRegularity · exists_clm_toBase_eq⟧ (an iterated composition of the level inclusions): it is a lift of $\sigma$ into $E_m$, of class $C^j$, hence $C^k$, and it coincides with the level map of $\sigma$ on $V_j$ by the injectivity of the inclusions ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRegularity · isScInftyGermAt_of_lifts⟧. So the level-wise reading of Theorem 3.5.11 (1) (preprint Corollary 3.47) (§III.20) is exactly what Definition 3.2.1 (ii) (preprint 3.25) needs (item 94 (a)).

**The kernel is smooth** ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRegularity · StabilityData.ker_Dfs_le_smooth⟧. For a basic germ $f$ with contraction map $B$ and the data $\mathcal S$ of Theorem 3.4.2 (preprint 3.38) (sc$^+$-germ $s$ into $W^1$), every $x = (a, w) \in \ker D(f + \iota s)(0)$ is a smooth point of $\mathbb R^n \oplus W$. The text takes the kernel of an sc-Fredholm operator to be smooth without comment. *The kernel equation.* $D(f + \iota s)(0) = Df(0) + \iota Ds(0)$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.Dfs'_eq⟧, and the $W$-component of $Df(0)x$ is $w - DB(0)x$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.snd_Df_eq⟧. Lemma 3.1.9 (preprint 3.9) at $(0, 0)$ gives $DB(0)(0, w) = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · IsSc0ContractionGerm.deriv_zero_snd⟧, so $DB(0)x = DB(0)(a, 0)$, and the $W$-component of the kernel equation reads
$$w = DB(0)(a, 0) - \iota Ds(0)x.$$
*The bootstrap.* $DB(0)$ is an sc-operator (the derivative of an sc$^1$-germ at the smooth point $0$) ⟦Chapter3/BasicGermLinearization · IsSc1GermWith.isScOperator_deriv_zero⟧, and so is $Ds(0) : E \to W^1$, which raises the level by one. By induction on $m$, $w \in W_m$: if $w = \iota y$ with $y \in W_m$, then $(a, 0)$ lies on level $m + 1$ (the parameter space has all levels equal), so $DB(0)(a, 0) \in W_{m+1}$, and $(a, y) \in E_m$ gives $Ds(0)x \in W_{m+1}$; hence $w \in W_{m+1}$. So $x \in E_\infty$.

**The chart $\psi \circ \varphi$.** An sc-smooth map $g$ on a relatively open $U$ is an sc-smooth map between retracts $(O, C, E) \to (O', C', E')$ for every retract $O \subseteq U$ mapped into $O'$: for every sc-smooth retraction $R$ onto $O$, $g \circ R$ is sc-smooth by the chain rule ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTCenter · IsScSmooth.isScSmoothRetractMap_of_subset⟧. For a chart $\varphi : V \to O$ of $X$ and an sc-diffeomorphism $\psi : U \to U'$ between relatively open subsets of partial quadrants with inverse $\psi'$ (§III.27) ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant⟧ and $O \subseteq U$, given that $\psi(O)$ is a retract of $C'$, the map $\psi \circ \varphi$ is a chart onto the model $(\psi(O), C', E')$, with inverse $\varphi^{-1} \circ \psi'$ and the source of $\varphi$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTCenter · MChart.compDiffeo⟧ (continuity of both maps: $\psi$, $\psi'$ are sc$^0$, hence continuous on level $0$). *It is a chart of the M-polyfold structure* ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTCenter · MChart.compDiffeo_isChart⟧: for a chart $\varphi'$ of the atlas, the overlap of $\psi\varphi$ with $\varphi'$ is $\psi$ of the overlap of $\varphi$ with $\varphi'$, and the two transition maps are $(\varphi' \circ \varphi^{-1}) \circ \psi'$ and $\psi \circ (\varphi \circ \varphi'^{-1})$; both are compositions of sc-smooth maps between retracts — the transitions of $\varphi$ and $\varphi'$, compatible because both are charts of $X$, and $\psi'$ resp. $\psi$ restricted to the overlap retracts by the lemma above.

**The tame model.** For the base retraction $r$ of the centred strong bundle chart, tame by the hypothesis on the charts, and the sc-diffeomorphism $\psi$ of the conjugation, $(\psi(O), C', E')$ is a tame retract with the retraction $\psi \circ r \circ \psi'$ ⟦Chapter2/TameInvariance · IsScDiffeoQuadrant.isTameRetract_image⟧ (§III.27); the text takes this for granted when it calls $\psi \circ \varphi$ a chart of the tame M-polyfold (item 95).

**Condition (b) from the choice of complement.** For data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) whose complement $Y$ has the projection property for $C$, $\pi(O) \subseteq C$: the retract lies in $C$, and $\pi$ maps $C$ into $C$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTCenter · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.π_mem_of_projectsInto⟧. So the regularity conditions reduce to condition (a), the sc-smoothness of $\delta$ on $V$ in the set sense ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTCenter · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.isRegular_of_projectsInto⟧.

**Theorem 3.6.8 (2) (preprint 3.54) at the centre** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTCenterG · theorem_3_6_8_tangentGermChart_proj⟧. *Let $f$ be a section of a strong bundle whose charts compatible with the atlas have tame base retractions, $f(x_0) = 0$ and $(f, x_0)$ in good position. Then there are an open $V_x \ni x_0$ and a dimension $d$ such that $S_{V_x} = \{y \in V_x : f(y) = 0\}$ has at $x_0$ the data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) of dimension $d$, with $\pi(O) \subseteq C$ for their projection and retract.* The proof fills the fields of the data one by one from the graph description, closed and projecting (§III.29) ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTG · theorem_3_6_8_graph_proj⟧:

1. *The chart.* $\psi$ and $\psi'$ form an sc-diffeomorphism $U \to U'$ (item (i) of §III.29); $O = r(U) \subseteq U$ since the retraction maps $U$ into $U$ ⟦Chapter3/Filler · StrongRetr.r_mem⟧; $\psi(O)$ is a tame retract (above, with the retract of the base retraction identified with $O = r(U)$ ⟦Chapter3/StrongBundleSections · StrongBundleChart.r_image⟧); the chart is $\chi = \psi \circ \varphi$, a chart of $X$, containing $x_0$, with $\chi(x_0) = \psi(0) = 0$.
2. *The subspace.* $N = K = \ker D(h + t)(0)$, finite-dimensional because $D(h + t)(0)$ is Fredholm ⟦Chapter3/SolutionLinearization · StabilityData.isFredholm_Dfs⟧, smooth (above), in good position to $C'$ (the hypothesis of Theorem 3.5.11 (preprint Corollary 3.47) established in §III.23); $d := \dim K$.
3. *The complement and the domain.* $Y$ closed, a good complement; $V = V'$, relatively open in $K \cap C'$, containing $0$; $\delta = \sigma$, continuous (it is $C^1$), with values in $Y$.
4. *The graph identity.* $\chi(S_{V_x} \cap \text{source}) = \psi\varphi(S_{V_x})$, since $V_x$ lies in the source of $\varphi$; this is the graph identity of Theorem 3.6.8 (3) (preprint 3.54).
5. *Regularity at $0$.* The sc$^\infty$-germ property from the lifts (above); $\sigma(0) = 0$; and $D\sigma(0) = 0$ within the whole cone $K \cap C'$, from $D\sigma(0) = 0$ within $V' = O \cap (K \cap C')$ with $O$ open, since derivatives within a set do not change when the set is intersected with a neighbourhood of the point.
6. *Condition (b).* From the projection property of $Y$, by the lemma above.

Without the last item this is the plain form ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTCenterG · theorem_3_6_8_tangentGermChart⟧. *What the statement gives about $d$.* The dimension is existentially quantified in the statement; the proof takes $d = \dim\ker D(h + t)(0)$, the dimension of the kernel of the linearization of the push-forward, and the statement does not identify $d$ with $\dim\ker f'(x_0)$ (the docstring's "$n = \dim\ker f'(x_0)$" is not part of the statement). The local constancy of §III.37 makes $d$ intrinsic in another way: it is the dimension of every datum of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) for the solution set at points of the chart domain.

*What is missing at this stage, and where it is supplied.* Condition (a), the sc-smoothness of $\sigma$ on $V'$ in the set sense, is not provided by Theorem 3.5.11 (1) (preprint Corollary 3.47), which gives the sc$^\infty$-germ property at $0$ only (item 82); it is obtained in §III.36 by re-centring the data at the nearby zeros, which needs the data of this section at every zero near $x_0$, hence good position there (Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54), §§III.32–III.33). The solution set here is the one in $V_x$; §III.37 restricts the chart so that it describes the global solution set.

### III.35 The re-centring lemma

The text's proof of Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) begins (preprint p. 84) by showing that the map $\delta$ of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) is an sc$^\infty$-germ near *every* point $v_0 \in V$, not only near $0$: take the point $x_0 = \varphi^{-1}(v_0 + \delta(v_0))$ of $M$, the data $(\psi, N', Y', V', \tau)$ of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) at $x_0$, and consider $w \mapsto \pi \circ \varphi \circ \psi^{-1}(w + \tau(w))$; its linearization at $0$ is claimed to be an isomorphism $N' \to N$, so it is a local diffeomorphism near $0$, and $\delta \circ (\pi\varphi\psi^{-1}(1 + \tau)) = (1 - \pi)\varphi\psi^{-1}(1 + \tau)$ shows that $\delta$ is regular near $v_0$. For an arbitrary set with the tangent germ property the claim is false: $\delta$ need not even be differentiable at $v_0$ (the curve with a vertical tangent of §III.24), and that is why condition (a) of `IsRegular` had to be added to Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27). What the argument really needs is that $\delta$ be $C^1$ at $v_0$. The formal *re-centring lemma* is the text's argument under that hypothesis, for two graph descriptions of the same set: one in a chart $\chi_1$ by a map $\sigma_1$ of class $C^1$ on all of its domain, one in a chart $\chi_2$ centred at the point over $v_0$ by a map $\sigma_2$ with the level-wise $C^k$-lifts of Theorem 3.5.11 (1) (preprint Corollary 3.47) at $0$. Its conclusion is that $\sigma_1$ is $C^k$ into every level $E_{1,m}$ near $v_0$, and that the two subspaces have the same dimension. Section III.36 applies it to the shrunk data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25); for the solution set of an sc-Fredholm section, §III.37 supplies the second description at every nearby zero. Four ingredients are proved first: the inverse function machinery of Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73) with the hypotheses its proofs use, a local inverse theorem for $C^k$ homeomorphisms between relatively open subsets of convex sets, Sard's lemma in fixed dimension, and two facts about relatively open subsets of convex sets.

**The inverse function machinery with the hypotheses its proofs use** ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW⟧. On a finite-dimensional space $V$, let $U \subseteq V$ be convex with unique differentiability at each of its points, $f : V \to V$ of class $C^j$ on $U$ with $j \ge 1$, $\|Df(x) - 1\| \le \tfrac12$ for $x \in U$ (derivatives within $U$), and let the image $f(U)$ have unique differentiability at each of its points. These are the data of Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73) as formalized in §III.16 (`InvData`, ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData⟧) with the partial quadrant removed: `InvData` asks for a partial quadrant $C$, $U$ relatively open in $C$ and convex, $f(U) \subseteq C$, and that $x$ be an interior point of $C$ iff $f(x)$ is; from the last hypothesis it derives that $f(U)$ is relatively open in $C$ ⟦Chapter3/QuadrantInverse · InvData.image_relOpen⟧, and the only use of that openness in the regularity proof is the unique differentiability of $f(U)$. The weak data keep convexity and the derivative bound and replace the quadrant hypotheses by unique differentiability of $U$ and of $f(U)$. The proofs are those of §III.16, retold here because they now stand on their own.

*The bi-Lipschitz estimate* (Lemma 3.8.18 (preprint 3.74), (70)). $f - \mathrm{id}$ has derivative $Df - 1$ of norm $\le \tfrac12$ on the convex $U$, so by the mean value inequality it is a $\tfrac12$-contraction there: $|(f(x') - x') - (f(x) - x)| \le \tfrac12|x' - x|$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.norm_sub_sub_le⟧. Writing $x' - x = (f(x') - f(x)) - ((f(x') - x') - (f(x) - x))$ gives $\tfrac12|x' - x| \le |f(x') - f(x)|$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.half_norm_le⟧, hence $f$ is injective on $U$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.injOn⟧. *The derivative is invertible*: $Df(x) = 1 - (1 - Df(x))$ with $\|1 - Df(x)\| \le \tfrac12 < 1$ is a unit by the Neumann series ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.derivUnit⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.isUnit_deriv⟧. *The inverse* $g$ on $f(U)$ is the inverse of $f$ on $U$ (a choice of preimage, unique by injectivity) ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.inv⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.inv_apply⟧; the estimate gives $|g(y') - g(y)| \le 2|y' - y|$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.norm_inv_sub_le⟧, so $g$ is Lipschitz, hence continuous on $f(U)$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.continuousOn_inv⟧.

*Lemma 3.8.21 (preprint 3.77)* ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.hasFDerivWithinAt_inv⟧: $g$ is differentiable within $f(U)$ at every $y \in f(U)$ with $Dg(y) = [Df(g(y))]^{-1}$. With $x = g(y)$: as $y' \to y$ in $f(U)$, $g(y') \to x$ in $U$ (continuity), so $f(g(y')) - f(x) - Df(x)(g(y') - x) = o(|g(y') - x|)$, and $|g(y') - x| \le 2|y' - y|$ turns this into $o(|y' - y|)$; applying the bounded operator $Df(x)^{-1}$ and using $f(g(y')) = y'$ gives $g(y') - g(y) - Df(x)^{-1}(y' - y) = o(|y' - y|)$. No hypothesis on $f(U)$ enters here.

*Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73), the regularity of the inverse* ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.contDiffOn_inv⟧: $g$ is $C^j$ on $f(U)$. First, $x \mapsto [Df(x)]^{-1}$ is $C^{j-1}$ on $U$: the derivative within $U$ of a $C^j$ map is $C^{j-1}$ there because $U$ has unique differentiability, and inversion is smooth near the units ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · InvDataW.contDiffOn_inverse_deriv⟧. Then by induction on $i \le j$: $g$ is $C^0$ (continuous); if $g$ is $C^i$ with $i < j$, then $g$ has the derivative $y \mapsto [Df(g(y))]^{-1}$ at every point of $f(U)$, which is $C^i$ as the composite of the $C^{j-1}$ map $x \mapsto [Df(x)]^{-1}$ with $g$, and a map with a $C^i$ derivative on a set of unique differentiability is $C^{i+1}$ there ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/Calculus/ContDiff/Defs · contDiffOn_succ_iff_hasFDerivWithinAt_of_uniqueDiffOn⟧. This last step is the one place where the unique differentiability of $f(U)$ is used. It is the bootstrapping of §III.16 in place of the text's iterated tangent maps (Lemma 3.8.20 (preprint 3.76)).

**Relatively open subsets of convex sets.** Let $D \subseteq V_1$ be convex with nonempty interior and $A = O \cap D$ with $O$ open. Then $A$ has unique differentiability at each of its points (a convex set with interior has it, and intersecting with an open set keeps it) ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · uniqueDiffOn_relOpen_convex⟧; and if $A$ is nonempty, it has nonempty interior: for $a \in A$ and an interior point $w$ of $D$, the points $a + t(w - a)$ with $0 < t$ small lie in $O$ by continuity and in the interior of $D$, because the open segment from a point of the closure of a convex set to an interior point lies in the interior ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · interior_nonempty_of_relOpen_convex⟧.

**Sard's lemma in fixed dimension** ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · finrank_le_of_interior_image_nonempty⟧. If $f : V_1 \to V_2$ between finite-dimensional spaces is differentiable on $s$ and $f(s)$ has nonempty interior, then $\dim V_2 \le \dim V_1$. Suppose $\dim V_1 < \dim V_2$. There is a linear surjection $p : V_2 \to V_1$ (send the first $\dim V_1$ vectors of a basis of $V_2$ to a basis of $V_1$ and the rest to $0$) ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · exists_linearMap_surjective⟧. The map $f \circ p$ on $p^{-1}(s) \subseteq V_2$ has the derivative $Df(p(x)) \circ p$, an endomorphism of $V_2$ whose range lies in the range of $Df(p(x))$, of dimension $\le \dim V_1 < \dim V_2$; so it is not injective and its determinant vanishes. By Mathlib's change-of-variables estimate, a set on which a map is differentiable with vanishing Jacobian determinant has an image of Haar measure zero ⟦Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/Jacobian · MeasureTheory.addHaar_image_eq_zero_of_det_fderivWithin_eq_zero⟧. But $f(s) \subseteq (f \circ p)(p^{-1}(s))$ since $p$ is onto, and the nonempty open interior of $f(s)$ has positive Haar measure: a contradiction. The lemma uses only differentiability (not continuity of the derivative) and only the finite dimension of both spaces.

**A $C^k$ homeomorphism with invertible derivative has a $C^k$ inverse** ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · exists_contDiffOn_inverse⟧. Let $D_1 \subseteq V_1$, $D_2 \subseteq V_2$ be convex with nonempty interior, $A$ relatively open in $D_1$, $\beta$ of class $C^k$ on $A$ with $k \ge 1$, $\beta(A)$ relatively open in $D_2$, $\alpha$ continuous on $\beta(A)$ with $\alpha(\beta(w)) = w$ for $w \in A$, and $D\beta(w_0) = L$ (derivative within $A$) a linear isomorphism $V_1 \to V_2$ at some $w_0 \in A$. Then there is a relatively open $B' \subseteq \beta(A)$ in $D_2$ containing $\beta(w_0)$ on which $\alpha$ is $C^k$. *Proof.* Normalize: $\hat\beta = L^{-1} \circ \beta$ is $C^k$ on $A$ with $D\hat\beta(w_0) = 1$. Its derivative is continuous within $A$ ($C^k$, $k \ge 1$, on a set of unique differentiability), so $\|D\hat\beta(w) - 1\| \le \tfrac12$ for $w \in A$ in a ball $B(w_0, r_1)$; take $r \le r_1$ so small that $B(w_0, r) \subseteq O$, where $A = O \cap D_1$. The set $U = D_1 \cap B(w_0, r)$ is convex, lies in $A$, is relatively open in $D_1$ (hence has unique differentiability), and the derivatives of $\hat\beta$ within $U$ and within $A$ agree there (locality of the derivative within a set), so $\|D\hat\beta - 1\| \le \tfrac12$ on $U$. Its image $\beta(U) = \beta(A) \cap \alpha^{-1}(B(w_0, r))$ — because $\alpha$ inverts $\beta$ on $A$ — is relatively open in $D_2$ by the continuity of $\alpha$, so $\beta(U)$, and with it $\hat\beta(U) = L^{-1}(\beta(U))$, has unique differentiability. So $(U, \hat\beta, k)$ are weak inverse data, and $\hat\beta^{-1}$ is $C^k$ on $\hat\beta(U)$ (Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73) above). On $B' = \beta(U)$, $\alpha = \hat\beta^{-1} \circ L^{-1}$, since $\alpha(\beta(w)) = w = \hat\beta^{-1}(\hat\beta(w))$; hence $\alpha$ is $C^k$ on $B'$. The relative openness of $\beta(U)$ comes from the continuity of the given inverse $\alpha$, not from a degeneracy index; that is the point of the weak data.

**The re-centring lemma** ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · recentre_contDiffOn_levels⟧. Let $\chi_1$, $\chi_2$ be charts of the M-polyfold $X$ ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MPolyfold.IsChart.compatible⟧, with models $(O_i, C_i, E_i)$, and $S \subseteq X$. Suppose:

- (in $\chi_1$) $K_1 \subseteq E_1$ is a finite-dimensional smooth subspace in good position to $C_1$, $Y_1$ a closed algebraic complement of $K_1$, $V_1$ relatively open in $K_1 \cap C_1$, $\sigma_1 : K_1 \to E_{1,0}$ with values in $Y_1$ and of class $C^1$ on $V_1$; $V_{x,1}$ is open in $X$ inside the domain of $\chi_1$ and $\chi_1(S \cap V_{x,1}) = \{v + \sigma_1(v) \mid v \in V_1\}$;
- (in $\chi_2$) the same kind of data $K_2$, $Y_2$, $V_2 \ni 0$, $\sigma_2$ with values in $Y_2$, continuous on $V_2$, $\sigma_2(0) = 0$, $D\sigma_2(0) = 0$ within $V_2$, and for every $k \ge 1$ a relatively open $V_{2,k} \subseteq V_2$ containing $0$ and a map $\sigma_{2,k} : K_2 \to E_{2,k}$ of class $C^k$ on $V_{2,k}$ with $\iota_k \sigma_{2,k} = \sigma_2$ there (the lifts of Theorem 3.5.11 (1) (preprint Corollary 3.47)); $V_{x,2}$ open inside the domain of $\chi_2$ with $\chi_2(S \cap V_{x,2}) = \{w + \sigma_2(w) \mid w \in V_2\}$;
- $y \in V_{x,1} \cap V_{x,2}$ with $\chi_2(y) = 0$, and $v_0 \in V_1$ with $\chi_1(y) = v_0 + \sigma_1(v_0)$.

Then $\dim K_1 = \dim K_2$, and for every level $m$ and every $k$ there is a relatively open $B' \subseteq V_1$ in $K_1 \cap C_1$ containing $v_0$ on which $\sigma_1$ takes values in $E_{1,m}$ and its level map $\sigma_1 : B' \to E_{1,m}$ is of class $C^k$.

The hypotheses are exactly what each step uses: the closedness of $Y_i$ makes the projection $\pi_i$ onto $K_i$ along $Y_i$ continuous; good position gives $K_i \cap C_i$ a nonempty interior in $K_i$ (for unique differentiability and for Sard); smoothness of $K_i$ gives the linear lifts of $K_i$ to every level ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · ScBanach.finLift⟧. Note that $V_1$ need not contain $0$ and $\sigma_1$ need not vanish anywhere: $\chi_1$ is an arbitrary chart in which $S$ is a $C^1$ graph.

*Step 1: notation and the points of $S$.* Write $\Gamma_1(v) = v + \sigma_1(v)$, $\Gamma_2(w) = w + \sigma_2(w)$, and $\pi_1$, $\pi_2$ for the continuous projections along $Y_1$, $Y_2$, so that $\pi_i\Gamma_i = \mathrm{id}$. ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · recentre_contDiffOn_levels⟧ For $v \in V_1$ the point $\chi_1^{-1}(\Gamma_1(v))$ lies in $S \cap V_{x,1}$ and $\chi_1$ maps it back to $\Gamma_1(v)$, which therefore lies in $O_1$; likewise for $w \in V_2$. Since $\sigma_2(0) = 0$, $\Gamma_2(0) = 0$, and both $\chi_1^{-1}(\Gamma_1(v_0))$ and $\chi_2^{-1}(\Gamma_2(0))$ are $y$.

*Step 2: the transition and the retractions onto the overlaps.* The charts are compatible, so the transition $T = \chi_1 \circ \chi_2^{-1}$ is an sc-smooth map from the overlap retract $\chi_2(V_{\chi_2} \cap V_{\chi_1})$ to $\chi_1(V_{\chi_1} \cap V_{\chi_2})$, and $T' = \chi_2 \circ \chi_1^{-1}$ in the other direction ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MChart.transition⟧ ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MChart.overlap⟧. The overlaps are sc-retracts ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MChart.isScRetract_overlap⟧: choose sc-smooth retractions $r_2$, $r_1$ onto them, with domains $U_{r_2}$, $U_{r_1}$. Then $T \circ r_2$ is sc-smooth on $U_{r_2}$, $T' \circ r_1$ on $U_{r_1}$, $T(\chi_2(q)) = \chi_1(q)$ on the domain of $\chi_2$, and $T' \circ T = \mathrm{id}$ on the overlap. Here the formal proof departs from the text, which treats $\varphi \circ \psi^{-1}$ as a map one can differentiate along the graph; a transition is only an sc-smooth map between retracts, and every derivative below is taken of $T \circ r_2$ or $T' \circ r_1$, on relatively open subsets of partial quadrants.

*Step 3: the parameter sets and the maps $\beta$, $\alpha$.* Let $A = \{w \in V_2 \mid \chi_2^{-1}(\Gamma_2(w)) \in V_{x,1}\}$ and $B_s = \{v \in V_1 \mid \chi_1^{-1}(\Gamma_1(v)) \in V_{x,2}\}$: the parameters whose points of $S$ lie in both chart neighbourhoods. By continuity of $w \mapsto \chi_2^{-1}(\Gamma_2(w))$ on $V_2$ (and of its counterpart), $A$ is relatively open in $K_2 \cap C_2$, $B_s$ in $K_1 \cap C_1$; $0 \in A$ and $v_0 \in B_s$. Define
$$\beta(w) = \pi_1(T(\Gamma_2(w))), \qquad \alpha(v) = \pi_2(T'(\Gamma_1(v))).$$
For $w \in A$, with $q = \chi_2^{-1}(\Gamma_2(w)) \in S \cap V_{x,1} \cap V_{x,2}$: $T(\Gamma_2(w)) = \chi_1(q)$ lies on the graph of $\sigma_1$, say $= \Gamma_1(v)$ with $v \in V_1$; applying $\pi_1$ gives $\beta(w) = v$, so $T(\Gamma_2(w)) = \Gamma_1(\beta(w))$, $\beta(w) \in B_s$, $\alpha(\beta(w)) = w$, and $\Gamma_2(w)$ lies in the overlap. Symmetrically, for $v \in B_s$: $T'(\Gamma_1(v)) = \Gamma_2(\alpha(v))$, $\alpha(v) \in A$, $\beta(\alpha(v)) = v$. Moreover $\beta(0) = v_0$, and $\alpha$ is continuous on $B_s$ ($\pi_2 \circ \chi_2 \circ \chi_1^{-1} \circ \Gamma_1$). Consequently, for every $A' \subseteq A$, $\beta(A') = \{v \in B_s \mid \alpha(v) \in A'\}$, which is relatively open in $K_1 \cap C_1$ whenever $A'$ is relatively open in $K_2 \cap C_2$. This is the text's "local diffeomorphism" in its topological half, obtained without any derivative. ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · recentre_contDiffOn_levels⟧

*Step 4: the lifted graph maps.* For $j \ge 1$ let $A_j = A \cap V_{2,j}$ and $\Gamma_{2,j}(w) = \lambda_j(w) + \sigma_{2,j}(w) \in E_{2,j}$, where $\lambda_j : K_2 \to E_{2,j}$ is the linear lift ⟦Chapter3/BasicClass · ScBanach.finLift⟧. Then $A_j$ is relatively open and contains $0$, $\Gamma_{2,j}$ is $C^j$ on $A_j$ with $\iota_j\Gamma_{2,j} = \Gamma_2$ there, $\Gamma_{2,j}$ maps $A_j$ into the level-$j$ part of the domain of $r_2$, and $r_2(\Gamma_2(w)) = \Gamma_2(w)$ for $w \in A_j$ (the graph points lie in the overlap, where $r_2$ is the identity). Its derivative within $A_j$ at $0$ is $\lambda_j$: indeed $\iota_j \circ D\sigma_{2,j}(0) = D\sigma_2(0)$ within $A_j$, which equals the derivative within $V_2$ (the two sets agree near $0$) and so vanishes; $\iota_j$ is injective, so $D\sigma_{2,j}(0) = 0$. Here the unique differentiability of $A_j$ at $0$ is that of a relatively open subset of the convex cone $K_2 \cap C_2$ with interior ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · uniqueDiffOn_relOpen_convex⟧.

*Step 5: $\beta$ is $C^j$ on $A_j$.* On $A_j$, $\beta = \pi_1 \circ ((T \circ r_2) \circ \iota_j) \circ \Gamma_{2,j}$, because $r_2$ fixes $\Gamma_2(w) = \iota_j\Gamma_{2,j}(w)$. The map $(T \circ r_2) \circ \iota_j$ is $C^j$ on the level-$j$ part of $U_{r_2}$ — Proposition 1.2.3 (preprint 1.18): an sc$^j$ map is $C^j$ from level $j$ to level $0$ ⟦Chapter1/Levels · IsSck.contDiffOn_base⟧ — and $\pi_1$ is continuous linear; so $\beta$ is $C^j$ on $A_j$. This is where the lifts are indispensable: $\sigma_2$ itself is only continuous into level $0$, and $T \circ r_2$ is not differentiable on level $0$.

*Step 6: the chain rule at $0$.* Take $j = 2$; then $\beta$ is $C^2$, in particular $C^1$, on $A_2$, with derivative $D\beta = D\beta(0)$ within $A_2$ at $0$. Read the lift on level $1$: $\Gamma_{2,1} = \iota_{1,2} \circ \Gamma_{2,2}$, with $\iota_1\Gamma_{2,1} = \Gamma_2$ on $A_2$, values in the level-$1$ part of $U_{r_2}$, derivative $\iota_{1,2}\lambda_2$ at $0$, and $\iota_1\iota_{1,2}\lambda_2(n) = n$ for $n \in K_2$. Let $\mathcal D = D(T \circ r_2)(\Gamma_{2,1}(0))$, the sc-derivative at the level-$1$ point over $0$. The map $w \mapsto T(r_2(\iota_1\Gamma_{2,1}(w)))$ has, by the chain rule for the sc$^1$ map $T \circ r_2$ (differentiable from level $1$ to level $0$) and the differentiable $\Gamma_{2,1}$, the derivative $n \mapsto \mathcal D n$ at $0$ within $A_2$. On $A_2$ this map equals $\Gamma_1 \circ \beta$ (Step 3 and $r_2 \circ \Gamma_2 = \Gamma_2$), and $\Gamma_1 \circ \beta$ has, at $0$, the derivative $n \mapsto D\beta\, n + D\sigma_1(v_0)D\beta\, n$ by the chain rule — here the $C^1$-property of $\sigma_1$ *at $v_0$*, within $V_1$, is used, together with $\beta(A_2) \subseteq V_1$ and $\beta(0) = v_0$. By uniqueness of the derivative within $A_2$ at $0$,
$$\mathcal D n = D\beta\, n + D\sigma_1(v_0)\,D\beta\, n \qquad (n \in K_2).$$

*Step 7: $D\beta(0)$ is injective.* Let $D\beta\, n = 0$; then $\mathcal D n = 0$. The composite $(T' \circ r_1) \circ (T \circ r_2)$ is sc-smooth on $U_{r_2}$ ($T \circ r_2$ maps $U_{r_2}$ into the overlap retract of $\chi_1$, hence into $U_{r_1}$), and it equals $r_2$ there: $r_2(u)$ lies in the overlap, $T(r_2(u))$ in the other overlap where $r_1$ is the identity, and $T' \circ T = \mathrm{id}$ on the overlap. By the chain rule for sc$^1$ maps between relatively open subsets of partial quadrants ⟦Chapter2/DegeneracyIndex · scDeriv_comp_apply⟧, $D((T' \circ r_1) \circ (T \circ r_2))(\Gamma_{2,1}(0))n = D(T' \circ r_1)(\cdot)\,\mathcal D n = 0$. Since the composite agrees with $r_2$ on the relatively open $U_{r_2}$, its derivative at the level-$1$ point is $Dr_2(\Gamma_{2,1}(0))$ ⟦Chapter3/LinearizationCharts · scDeriv_eq_of_eqOn_relOpen⟧. And $Dr_2(\Gamma_{2,1}(0))n = n$: the family $w \mapsto \iota_1\Gamma_{2,1}(w)$ consists of fixed points of $r_2$, so the derivative of $r_2$ fixes its tangent vectors at $0$, which are the vectors $\iota_1\iota_{1,2}\lambda_2(n) = n$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyGraph · scDeriv_apply_toBase_deriv_eq_of_fixed⟧ — the tangent space $K_2$ of the graph lies in the tangent space of the retract. So $n = 0$. Hence $D\beta(0)$ is injective and $\dim K_2 \le \dim K_1$.

*Step 8: surjectivity by counting dimensions.* $\beta$ is differentiable on $A_2$, and $\beta(A_2)$ is a nonempty relatively open subset of the convex cone $K_1 \cap C_1$ (Step 3; it contains $\beta(0) = v_0$), which has nonempty interior in $K_1$ by good position; so $\beta(A_2)$ has nonempty interior ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · interior_nonempty_of_relOpen_convex⟧, and Sard's lemma in fixed dimension gives $\dim K_1 \le \dim K_2$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · finrank_le_of_interior_image_nonempty⟧. So $\dim K_1 = \dim K_2$, the injective $D\beta(0)$ is bijective, and $L = D\beta(0) : K_2 \to K_1$ is a linear isomorphism. This is the first conclusion of the lemma, and also the reason why the dimension of a set with two such descriptions cannot jump.

*Step 9: the inverse, and the regularity of $\sigma_1$.* Fix $m$ and $k$ and take $j = m + k + 2$. By Step 5, $\beta$ is $C^j$, hence $C^{\max(k, 1)}$, on $A_j$, $\beta(A_j) \subseteq B_s$, and its derivative within $A_j$ at $0$ is again $L$ (the derivatives within $A_j$ and within $A_2$ agree at $0$, both sets being relatively open near $0$ in the same cone). Now apply the local inverse theorem above with $D_1 = K_2 \cap C_2$, $D_2 = K_1 \cap C_1$ (convex with interior), $A = A_j$, the continuous inverse $\alpha$ on $\beta(A_j)$ and $w_0 = 0$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · exists_contDiffOn_inverse⟧: there is a relatively open $B' \subseteq \beta(A_j)$ in $K_1 \cap C_1$ containing $v_0$ on which $\alpha$ is $C^{\max(k,1)}$, with $\alpha(B') \subseteq A_j$. Next the level-$m$ map
$$\Psi(w) = (T \circ r_2)_m\bigl(\iota_{m, k+2}\,\Gamma_{2,j}(w)\bigr) - \lambda_m(\beta(w)) \in E_{1,m}, \qquad w \in A_j,$$
where $\iota_{m,k+2} : E_{2,m+k+2} \to E_{2,m}$ and $\lambda_m$ is the lift of $K_1$ to level $m$. It is $C^k$ on $A_j$: the sc$^{k+2}$ map $T \circ r_2$ is $C^{k+2}$ from level $m + k + 2$ to level $m$ (Proposition 1.2.3 (preprint 1.18) ⟦Chapter1/Levels · IsSck.contDiffOn_levels⟧), $\Gamma_{2,j}$ is $C^j$ into level $j = m + k + 2$, and $\lambda_m \circ \beta$ is $C^j$. Including into level $0$, $\iota_m\Psi(w) = T(r_2(\Gamma_2(w))) - \beta(w) = \Gamma_1(\beta(w)) - \beta(w) = \sigma_1(\beta(w))$. Hence for $v \in B'$, $v = \beta(\alpha(v))$ and $\sigma_1(v) = \iota_m\Psi(\alpha(v))$: $\sigma_1$ takes values in $E_{1,m}$ on $B'$, its level-$m$ map is $\Psi \circ \alpha$ ⟦Chapter1/ScSmooth · levelMap_eq_of_toBase_eq⟧, which is $C^k$ on $B'$ as a composite of $C^k$ maps, and $B' \subseteq B_s \subseteq V_1$. This is the text's identity $\delta \circ (\pi\varphi\psi^{-1}(1 + \tau)) = (1 - \pi)\varphi\psi^{-1}(1 + \tau)$, written on level $m$ through the lifts.

*Comparison with the text.* Four things in the formal argument are not in the sketch of preprint p. 84. (a) The isomorphism claim needs $\sigma_1$ differentiable at $v_0$, which for an arbitrary set with the tangent germ property fails; here $\sigma_1$ is assumed $C^1$ on all of $V_1$, and §III.36 arranges this by shrinking $V_1$ to the neighbourhood of $0$ on which the germ property makes $\delta$ of class $C^1$. (b) Only the *injectivity* of the linearization comes from the chain rule, and it uses that the tangent space $K_2$ of the second graph lies in the tangent space of the overlap retract (Step 7). The symmetric argument for surjectivity would differentiate $T' \circ \Gamma_1 = \Gamma_2 \circ \alpha$ at $v_0$; but $T' \circ r_1$ is differentiable only from level $1$ to level $0$, so this would need $\sigma_1$ differentiable *into level $1$* at $v_0$, which is known near the centre of $\chi_1$ (Theorem 3.5.11 (1) (preprint Corollary 3.47)) and not at $v_0$. Surjectivity therefore comes from the dimension count by Sard's lemma (Step 8), which at the same time shows that the dimension is locally constant. (c) The regularity of $\beta^{-1} = \alpha$ is not the quadrant inverse function theorem 3.72 (§III.16): that theorem asks for $d_C(x) = d_C(\beta(x))$ (formally: interior points to interior points), which here would be a statement about the intrinsic degeneracy index of $S$ — not available. It is Lemma 3.8.21 (preprint 3.77) and the bootstrapping of Proposition 3.8.17 (preprint 3.73) under the weak data, with the relative openness of $\beta(U)$ supplied by the continuity of the inverse $\alpha$. (d) The neighbourhood $B'$ depends on $(m, k)$ through the lift $\Gamma_{2,m+k+2}$; this is harmless, because sc-smoothness in the set sense is a local property of the level maps (§III.36).

### III.36 Regular data by re-centring, and Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) with locally constant dimension

This section turns the re-centring lemma of §III.35 into condition (a) of `IsRegular` (§III.24) and adapts Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) to data whose dimension varies from point to point. The tool is the shrinking of the data of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25): the chart is restricted to an open subset of its domain and the parameter set $V$ to a smaller relatively open set, with the same $N$, $Y$ and $\delta$. The data at a point $x$ are shrunk into the neighbourhood of $0$ where $\delta$ is $C^1$; then, at every point of the shrunk graph, the data at that point (which a set with the tangent germ property has everywhere) provide the second graph description that the re-centring lemma needs.

*Open subsets of tame retracts* (told in §III.27). The restriction of a tame retraction $r : U \to U$ to $U \cap r^{-1}(W)$, $W$ open (Proposition 2.3.1 (1) (preprint 2.10)) ⟦Chapter2/Retracts · ScRetraction.restrict⟧, is tame: the degeneracy-index condition of Definition 2.5.2 (preprint 2.34) is inherited pointwise, and at a smooth point $x$ of $O \cap W$ the sc-complement of $T_xO$ of the original retraction serves, because the derivative of the restricted retraction at $x$ is $Dr(x)$ — the two maps agree on a relatively open neighbourhood ⟦Chapter3/LinearizationCharts · scDeriv_eq_of_eqOn_relOpen⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · ScRetraction.restrict_isTame⟧. So $O \cap W$ is a tame retract ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · IsTameRetract.inter_isOpen⟧, and the model $\varphi(s)$ of a chart restricted to an open $s$ of its domain is tame when the model of $\varphi$ is, since $\varphi(s) = O \cap W$ for some open $W$ ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MChart.exists_isOpen_image_eq⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MChart.isTameRetract_image⟧. The text uses this silently whenever it shrinks a chart of a tame M-polyfold.

**Shrinking the data** ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink⟧. Let $D = (\varphi, N, Y, V, \delta)$ be data of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25) for $M$ at $x$ of dimension $n$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart⟧, $s$ an open subset of the domain of $\varphi$ containing $x$, $V' \subseteq V$ relatively open in $N \cap C$ containing $0$, and $M'$ a set with $\varphi(M' \cap s) = \{v + \delta(v) \mid v \in V'\}$. Then the chart $\varphi$ restricted to $s$ ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MChart.restrict⟧ — a chart of the structure ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MPolyfold.IsChart.restrict⟧ with a tame model by the paragraph above — together with the same $N$, $Y$, $\delta$ and the parameter set $V'$, are data of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25) for $M'$ at $x$ of dimension $n$. Every field is inherited: good position, the closed good complement, $\delta(0) = 0$, $D\delta(0) = 0$ and the germ property of $\delta$ concern $N$, $Y$, $\delta$ and the cone $N \cap C$ only, which are unchanged, and continuity on $V'$ follows from continuity on $V$. The set $M'$ may differ from $M$; this is how §III.37 passes between the solution set in a neighbourhood and the global solution set. The shrunk data have the source $s$, the model $\varphi(s)$, the same chart values, the same $N$, $\delta$ and projection $\pi$, and the parameter set $V'$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_φ_source⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_φ_model_O⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_φ_apply⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_N⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_V⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_δ⟧ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_π⟧. They are regular ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.IsRegular⟧ as soon as $\delta$ is sc-smooth on $V'$ in the set sense and the original data satisfy condition (b), $\pi(O) \subseteq C$: condition (b) passes to the smaller retract $\varphi(s) \subseteq O$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_isRegular⟧.

**The shrinking data** ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.exists_shrinkData⟧. For an open $W \ni x$ in $X$ and an open $O'' \ni 0$ in $N$, let
$$s = \{y \in \mathrm{dom}\,\varphi \cap W \mid \pi\varphi(y) \in O''\}, \qquad V' = \{v \in V \cap O'' \mid \varphi^{-1}(v + \delta(v)) \in W\}.$$
Then $s$ is open (the preimage of $O''$ under the continuous $\pi \circ \varphi$ on the open $\mathrm{dom}\,\varphi \cap W$) and contains $x$ ($\pi\varphi(x) = \pi(0) = 0$); $V'$ is relatively open in $N \cap C$ because $v \mapsto \varphi^{-1}(v + \delta(v))$ is continuous on $V$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.Γ_continuousOn⟧, and contains $0$ ($\varphi^{-1}(0) = x \in W$); $s \subseteq W$, $V' \subseteq V \cap O''$; and $\varphi(M \cap s) = \{v + \delta(v) \mid v \in V'\}$. For the last identity: a point $y \in M \cap s$ has $\varphi(y) = \Gamma(v)$ with $v = \pi\varphi(y) \in V$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.exists_eq_Γ⟧, $v \in O''$ because $y \in s$, and $\varphi^{-1}(\Gamma(v)) = y \in W$; conversely for $v \in V'$ the point $\varphi^{-1}(\Gamma(v))$ lies in $M$ and in the domain ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.symm_Γ_mem⟧, in $W$ by definition of $V'$, and $\pi\varphi$ of it is $v \in O''$ since $\pi\Gamma = \mathrm{id}$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.π_Γ⟧.

**The lifts from the germ property.** Definition 3.2.1 (ii) (preprint 3.25), in the reading of §III.24 ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · IsScInftyGermAt⟧, gives for every $m$, $k$ a relatively open $V_{m,k} \ni 0$ on which $\delta$ takes values in $E_m$ and is $C^k$ into $E_m$. With $m = k$: for every $k \ge 1$ the level map of $\delta$ on $V_{k,k} \cap V$ is a $C^k$-lift $\delta_k$ of $\delta$ into $E_k$ — the hypothesis of the re-centring lemma on the centred side ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.exists_lift⟧. With $(m, k) = (0, 1)$: $\delta$ is $C^1$ on a relatively open neighbourhood of $0$ in $N \cap C$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · IsScInftyGermAt.contDiffOn_one⟧. And $D\delta(0) = 0$ within $V$, since the data give it within $N \cap C$ and $V$ agrees with $N \cap C$ near $0$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.fderivWithin_V_zero⟧.

**Condition (a) of `IsRegular` by re-centring** ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.exists_isRegular_of_locally⟧. Let $X$ be an M-polyfold (Hausdorff, paracompact), $M \subseteq X$, $D$ data of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25) for $M$ at $x$ of dimension $n$ satisfying condition (b), $\pi(O) \subseteq C$, and $U \ni x$ open such that every point of $M \cap U$ has data of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25) for $M$ of *some* dimension. Then there are data $D'$ for $M$ at $x$ of dimension $n$ which are regular (`IsRegular`: (a) and (b)), whose chart domain lies in $U$, and such that every datum of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25) for $M$ at a point of $M$ in the chart domain of $D'$ has dimension $n$. (The proof constructs $D'$ as the shrinking of $D$, with the same $N$, $Y$, $\delta$; the statement records only the regularity, the domain and the dimension.)

*Step 1: shrinking into the $C^1$ neighbourhood.* By the germ property, $\delta$ is $C^1$ on $V_1 = O_1 \cap (N \cap C)$ with $O_1$ open and $0 \in O_1$. The shrinking data for $W = U$ and $O'' = O_1$ give $s \subseteq U$ and $V' \subseteq V \cap O_1$, so $V' \subseteq V_1$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.V_subset⟧, with $\varphi(M \cap s) = \Gamma(V')$; let $D_1$ be the shrunk data for $M$ itself. Then $\delta$ is $C^1$ on all of $D_1$'s parameter set $V'$: this is hypothesis (a) of the re-centring lemma on the side of $D_1$, and the reason for the shrinking.

*Step 2: the re-centring at every point.* Let $y \in M \cap s$ and let $D''$ be any data of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25) for $M$ at $y$, of dimension $n'$. Put $v_0 = \pi\varphi(y)$; then $v_0 \in V'$ and $\varphi(y) = \Gamma(v_0)$. The re-centring lemma applies with $\chi_1 = $ the chart of $D_1$ (graph of $\delta$ over $V'$ on its whole domain $s$, $\delta$ of class $C^1$ on $V'$) and $\chi_2 = $ the chart of $D''$, centred at $y$ (graph of $\delta''$ over $V''$ on its whole domain, $\delta''(0) = 0$, $D\delta''(0) = 0$ within $V''$, the lifts from the germ property of $D''$); all the remaining hypotheses — smooth finite-dimensional $N$, closed good complement, good position, relatively open parameter sets — are fields of the two data ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · recentre_contDiffOn_levels⟧. It gives $n = \dim N = \dim N'' = n'$, and for every $m$, $k$ a relatively open $B' \subseteq V'$ in $N \cap C$ containing $v_0$ on which $\delta$ takes values in $E_m$ and is $C^k$ into $E_m$.

*Step 3: $\delta$ is sc-smooth on $V'$ in the set sense.* $V'$ is a relatively open subset of the partial quadrant $N \cap C$ of $N$ with the constant sc-structure ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.V_isRelOpenInQuadrant⟧, all of whose levels are $V'$. By Proposition 1.2.4 (preprint 1.19) in the form "maps every level into the corresponding level, and the level maps are $C^\infty$" ⟦Chapter1/Levels · IsScSmooth.of_contDiffOn_levels⟧, it suffices that for every $m$: (i) $\delta(V') \subseteq E_m$, and (ii) the level map $\delta : V' \to E_m$ is $C^k$ for every $k$. For $v \in V'$ the point $y = \varphi^{-1}(\Gamma(v))$ lies in $M \cap s \subseteq M \cap U$, so by hypothesis there are data at $y$ of some dimension, and Step 2 applies with $v_0 = \pi\varphi(y) = v$. For (i), the set $B'$ for $(m, 0)$ contains $v$, and $\delta$ maps it into $E_m$. For (ii), fix $k$; around every $v \in V'$ the set $B' = O_B \cap (N \cap C)$ for $(m, k)$ contains $v$, and the level map is $C^k$ on $O_B \cap V' \subseteq B'$; a map that is $C^k$ near every point of a set is $C^k$ on the set ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/Calculus/ContDiff/Defs · contDiffOn_of_locally_contDiffOn⟧. Here the dependence of $B'$ on $(m, k)$ (§III.35, Step 9) does no harm: the property is checked one $(m, k)$ at a time, and locally.

*Step 4: conclusion.* With (a) from Step 3 and (b) inherited from $D$, the shrunk data $D_1$ are regular ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_isRegular⟧; their chart domain is $s \subseteq U$; and by Step 2 every datum at a point of $M \cap s$ has dimension $n$.

*What the statement gives for an arbitrary subset.* The hypotheses of the theorem are about $M$ and $D$ only; no solution set, section or Fredholm property enters. If $M$ has the $n$-dimensional tangent germ property ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.HasTangentGermProperty⟧, every point of $M$ has data of dimension $n$, so the hypothesis "every point of $M \cap U$ has data of some dimension" holds with $U = X$. Hence, read off the statement (this is not a separate Lean theorem): *for any $M$ with the tangent germ property, any data at $x$ that satisfy condition (b) can be shrunk to regular data at $x$, of the same dimension.* Condition (a) of `IsRegular` is therefore not an independent hypothesis of Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27): if at every point of $M$ some $n$-dimensional data satisfy (b), the regular data that Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) asks for exist at every point ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · theorem_3_2_3_of_isRegular⟧. (The inclusion $M \subseteq X_\infty$ of Definition 3.2.1 (1) (preprint 3.25) is not used.) The counterexample to the text's step "$\delta$ is an sc$^\infty$-germ near every $v_0$" (§III.24) is not contradicted: there the data are regular only after shrinking $V$ away from the point with a vertical tangent, which is what Step 1 does. What remains a genuine restriction is condition (b), which fails for some good complements (§III.24) and is obtained for solution sets by the choice of the complement (§III.29, §III.34).

*The dimension is locally constant.* The last clause of the theorem says more than the text: around every point where regular data exist, all data of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25) at nearby points of $M$ have the same dimension. The dimension of a set with the tangent germ property at varying dimensions is therefore locally constant along $M$ — but not necessarily globally constant, and Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) as formalized fixes $n$ (§III.24). For the solution set of an sc-Fredholm section, whose dimension $\dim\ker f'(x)$ can change between components, the fixed-dimension theorem would need $S$ split first; instead Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) is restated for locally constant dimension.

**Manifolds with corners of locally constant dimension** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.IsManifoldWithCornersLoc⟧. An M-polyfold structure is that of a manifold with boundary with corners of locally constant dimension if it has an equivalent atlas all of whose charts have models $(O, C, E)$ with $O$ relatively open in the partial quadrant $C$ and $E_0$ finite-dimensional — the definition of §III.24 ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.IsManifoldWithCorners⟧ without the requirement that every chart have dimension $n$. A manifold with corners of dimension $n$ is one of locally constant dimension ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.IsManifoldWithCorners.isManifoldWithCornersLoc⟧.

**Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) with locally constant dimension** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · theorem_3_2_3_loc_of_isRegular⟧. Let $X$ be an M-polyfold, $M \subseteq X$ nonempty, and suppose every $x \in M$ has regular data of Definition 3.2.1 (2) (preprint 3.25) for $M$ of some dimension $n(x)$. Then $M$ is a sub-M-polyfold of $X$; its induced structure (Proposition 2.3.19 (preprint 2.21)) is tame and that of a manifold with boundary with corners of locally constant dimension; and for every $x \in M$, all regular data $d$ at $x$ of any dimension and every base point $a_0 \in M$ (the value of the chart inverse off its domain), the chart $y \mapsto \pi\varphi(y)$ of $d$ is a chart of the induced structure whose domain contains $x$. The proof is that of Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) (§III.24) with the dimension quantified pointwise; every step there is either pointwise or does not use $n$.

*Sub-M-polyfold* ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_loc_isSubMPolyfold⟧. Definition 2.3.18 (preprint 2.20) is pointwise: at $a \in M$ take regular data $d$ (of whatever dimension); the open set $U_0 = \varphi^{-1}(\Sigma \cap O)$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.U₀⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.isOpen_U₀⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.mem_U₀⟧ and the sc-smooth retraction $s = \varphi^{-1} \circ t \circ \varphi$ onto $M \cap U_0$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.s⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.s_isScSmoothOn⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.s_s⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.image_s⟧ are those of §III.24, exactly as in the fixed-dimension proof ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · theorem_3_2_3_isSubMPolyfold⟧.

*The charts and the atlas.* Let the charts be all maps $\pi\varphi$ of all regular data at all points of $M$, of all dimensions, with all base points ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.tangentChartsLoc⟧ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.chart⟧. Each is a chart of the induced structure ⟦Chapter2/SubMPolyfold · subPolyfold⟧: it is compatible with every chart $\varphi'|_{W \cap M}$ of the induced atlas, by the lemma of §III.24, whose proof never compares dimensions ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.chart_compatible_subChart⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_loc_isChart⟧. Any two of them are compatible, by the locality of compatibility: both are compatible with every chart of the induced atlas, whose domains cover $M$ ⟦Chapter2/SubMPolyfold · MPolyfold.subAtlas⟧ ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MChart.compatible_of_locally⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.tangentChartsLoc_compatible⟧. The argument never mentions the dimension of either chart. They form an atlas: every $y \in M$ has regular data, and the chart of these data, with the base point a fixed element of $M$ (here $M \ne \emptyset$ is used), contains $y$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.mem_chart_source⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.germAtlasLoc⟧. This atlas is equivalent to the induced atlas of Proposition 2.3.19 (preprint 2.21), since each of its charts is a chart of the induced structure ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MAtlas.equiv_iff⟧ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_loc_atlas_equiv⟧.

*Tame, and a manifold with corners.* The model of each chart $\pi\varphi$ is $(V, N \cap C, N)$ with the identity retraction, a tame retract ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.model_isTameRetract⟧; so the induced structure is tame, witnessed by the equivalent atlas just built ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_loc_isTame⟧. Each model is relatively open in the partial quadrant $N \cap C$ of the finite-dimensional $N$ ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.chart_isCorner⟧; so the induced structure is that of a manifold with boundary with corners of locally constant dimension ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · MPolyfold.theorem_3_2_3_loc_isManifoldWithCornersLoc⟧. The assembled statement collects these, with the chart property of every $\pi\varphi$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · theorem_3_2_3_loc_of_isRegular⟧.

As for Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27), the hypotheses are the regularity conditions at every point; by the observation above, condition (a) there can be replaced by condition (b) plus shrinking. Theorem 3.6.11 (preprint 3.57) (§III.37) applies this version to the solution set, where regular data exist at every zero by §§III.34–III.36 and the dimension $\dim\ker f'(x)$ is locally constant by Step 2 of the theorem above; the fixed-dimension statement for each part $S_n$ of the solution set is obtained from Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) itself.

### III.37 The implicit function theorems assembled: Theorems 3.6.8, 3.1.26, 3.1.22 (preprint 3.54, 3.23, 3.19), Corollary 3.1.27 (preprint 3.24), Theorems 3.6.10, 3.6.11 (preprint 3.56, 3.57)

The text derives all its implicit function theorems from Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54): (1) good position at every zero near $x$, (2) the sub-M-polyfold structure of the solution set near $x$, through the tangent germ property and Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27). In the formalization the pieces are: the data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) at the centre, with condition (b) (§III.34); good position at the nearby zeros, under an added hypothesis (§III.33); the re-centring, which turns data at all nearby points into regular data and shows that the dimension is locally constant (§§III.35–III.36); and Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27), with fixed dimension (§III.24) or with locally constant dimension ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheorems · theorem_3_2_3_loc_of_isRegular⟧ (§III.36). This section assembles them. Throughout, $f$ is a section of a strong bundle $P : Y \to X$ over an M-polyfold, "the charts have tame base retractions" means that the base retraction of every strong bundle chart compatible with the atlas is tame (the formal reading of "tame strong bundle", §III.23), $S = \{x \in X : f(x) = 0\}$ is the global solution set ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFT · solutionSet⟧, and good position is Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53) in the chart form ⟦Chapter3/GoodPositionGermG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGerm⟧ (§III.21).

**The data for the global solution set** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheoremsG · theorem_3_6_8_tangentGermChart_solutionSet⟧. *If the charts have tame base retractions, $f(x_0) = 0$ and $(f, x_0)$ is in good position, then $S$ has at $x_0$ data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) of some dimension $d$ with $\pi(O) \subseteq C$.* §III.34 gives data $D$ for $S_{V_x} = \{y \in V_x : f(y) = 0\}$. Restrict its chart to the open set $s = \text{source} \cap V_x \ni x_0$, keeping $N$, $Y$, $V$, $\delta$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink⟧ ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MChart.restrict⟧: the graph identity holds for the global $S$ because $S \cap s = S_{V_x} \cap \text{source}$. The restricted model $\varphi(s)$ is a tame retract (an open subset of a tame retract, §III.36), and condition (b) passes to it, since $\varphi(s) \subseteq O$ ⟦Chapter2/MPolyfold · MChart.image_subset_model⟧ and the projection is unchanged ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_π⟧.

**Theorem 3.6.8 (2) (preprint 3.54), from the nearby good position** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheoremsG · theorem_3_6_8_two⟧. *Let the charts have tame base retractions, $f(x_0) = 0$, $(f, x_0)$ in good position, and let every zero of $f$ in an open $V_{x_0} \ni x_0$ be in good position. Then there is a dimension $d$ and regular data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) of dimension $d$ for $S$ at $x_0$ (condition (a): $\delta$ sc-smooth on $V$ in the set sense; condition (b): $\pi(O) \subseteq C$) ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.IsRegular⟧, on a chart whose source lies in $V_{x_0}$; and every datum of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) for $S$ at a point of $S$ in that source has dimension $d$.* Only that $f$ is a section is assumed; the regularity of $f$ enters through the good position at $x_0$ and at the nearby zeros, which carry the sc-Fredholm germ data. *Proof.* Take the data $D$ at $x_0$ above. Every $y \in S \cap V_{x_0}$ is a zero in good position, so the same theorem at $y$ gives data of Definition 3.2.1 (preprint 3.25) for $S$ at $y$, of some dimension. These are the hypotheses of the re-centring theorem of §III.36 ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.exists_isRegular_of_locally⟧ for $D$ (which satisfies (b)) and the open set $V_{x_0}$: it shrinks $D$ to regular data $D'$ with source in $V_{x_0}$, of the same dimension $d$, along which every datum has dimension $d$. This is the text's "proceeding as above we can construct a map $\sigma$" at every zero, made into a proof: the regularity condition (a), an added hypothesis of Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) for general sets (§III.24), is a theorem for solution sets (items 150–151).

**Theorem 3.6.8 (1) and (2) (preprint 3.54)** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheoremsG · theorem_3_6_8⟧. *Let $f$ be an sc-Fredholm section, the charts with tame base retractions, $f(x_0) = 0$, and let $(f, x_0)$ be in good position **with a filler that satisfies condition (3) of Definition 3.1.4 (preprint 3.4) at the zeros of the filler near $0$** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGermFilled⟧. Then there is an open $V_x \ni x_0$ such that (1) $(f, y)$ is in good position at every zero $y \in V_x$, and (2) the conclusions of Theorem 3.6.8 (2) (preprint 3.54) above hold with $V_{x_0} = V_x$.* (1) is §III.33 ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · theorem_3_6_8_one⟧; (2) is the previous theorem, fed with (1), with the strengthened good position weakened to good position ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · StrongBundle.IsGoodPositionGermFilled.isGoodPositionGerm⟧ and the section property of the sc-Fredholm section. The filler condition was an **added hypothesis** when this form was proved: Definition 3.1.4 (preprint 3.4) imposes condition (3) at $0$ only, and the surjectivity of $f'(y)$ at the nearby zeros is equivalent to it there (§III.33; items 100, 105–106); the kernel half of (1) needs no added hypothesis (§III.32). Since the second addendum the condition is a consequence of good position (§III.45), and Theorem 3.6.8 (preprint 3.54) is proved with the text's hypotheses (§III.43). Part (3) of the text's proof, the graph description, is §III.29 ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTG · theorem_3_6_8_graph_proj⟧, and part (4), the kernel at the nearby zeros in the chart at $x_0$, is §III.32 ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbySurjG · theorem_3_6_8_nearby_kernel⟧. The text's neighbourhood $V$ is the $V_x$ of (3), shrunk in (2) to the chart source of the re-centred data.

**Theorem 3.1.26 (preprint 3.23), boundary case, from the nearby good position** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheoremsG · theorem_3_1_26_of_nearby⟧. *Let $f$ be an sc-Fredholm section, the charts with tame base retractions, $f(x) = 0$, $(f, x)$ in good position ($f'(x)$ onto with kernel in good position, Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53)), and every zero in an open $V_{x_0} \ni x$ in good position. Then there are an open $U$ with $x \in U \subseteq V_{x_0}$ and a dimension $d$ such that every zero in $U$ is a smooth point, $S(f, U) = \{y \in U : f(y) = 0\}$ is a sub-M-polyfold of $X$, and its induced M-polyfold structure (Proposition 2.3.19 (preprint 2.21)) ⟦Chapter2/SubMPolyfold · subPolyfold⟧ is tame and equivalent to that of a $d$-dimensional manifold with boundary with corners* ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · MPolyfold.IsManifoldWithCorners⟧. *Proof.* Theorem 3.6.8 (2) (preprint 3.54) at $x$ gives regular data $D$ of dimension $d$ with source $U \subseteq V_{x_0}$ and the local constancy of the dimension; let $S' = S(f, U)$. At every $y \in S'$, Theorem 3.6.8 (2) (preprint 3.54) at $y$, with the open neighbourhood $U \cap V_{x_0}$ in place of $V_{x_0}$, gives regular data $D_y$ for $S$ with source in $U \cap V_{x_0}$, of a dimension $d_y$; since $y \in S \cap U$, the local constancy along $D$ gives $d_y = d$. Because the source of $D_y$ lies in $U$, $S' \cap \text{source} = S \cap \text{source}$, so $D_y$ restricted to its own source is a datum for $S'$ ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink⟧, still regular ⟦Chapter3/SolutionGermRecentre · MPolyfold.TangentGermChart.shrink_isRegular⟧. So every point of $S'$ has regular data of dimension $d$, $S' \ni x$ is nonempty, and Theorem 3.2.3 (preprint 3.27) with fixed dimension (§III.24) ⟦Chapter3/TangentGerm · theorem_3_2_3_of_isRegular⟧ gives the sub-M-polyfold with its tame induced structure equivalent to a $d$-dimensional manifold with boundary with corners. The zeros are smooth points because an sc-Fredholm section is regularizing ⟦Chapter3/TameGermsG · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmSection.mem_smoothSet_of_isZeroAt⟧. The text's "a uniquely determined structure ... the same as the one defined in Proposition 2.21" is the induced structure itself, with the equivalent atlas of the charts $\pi \circ \varphi$; uniqueness is not stated separately.

**Theorem 3.1.26 (preprint 3.23)** ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheoremsG · theorem_3_1_26⟧. *The same under the added filler hypothesis at $x$ instead of the nearby good position, with in addition: every zero in $U$ is in good position.* Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54) (§III.33) supplies the open $V_x$ of nearby good position, and the previous theorem applies with $V_{x_0} = V_x$.

**Theorem 3.1.22 (preprint 3.19), interior case.** The first clause, "$f'(x_0)$ is sc-Fredholm", is Proposition 3.6.5 (preprint 3.52) in the chart at $x_0$ ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheoremsG · theorem_3_1_22_linearization⟧ ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermLinearizationG · proposition_3_6_5_preprint⟧, applied to the sc-Fredholm germ contained in the good position: $Dg(0)$, for a derivative family $Dg$ of the filling germ (§III.21), and $Tf(x_0) = Dg(0)|_{T_0O}$ are Fredholm on level $0$ with the same index. The statement inherits the index clause of that proposition, "$= n - N$ for some natural numbers $n$, $N$", which carries no information; the index as the $n - N$ of the conjugating basic germ is §III.30 ⟦Chapter3/FredholmGermIndexG · proposition_3_6_5_index⟧. *The theorem* ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTTheoremsG · theorem_3_1_22⟧: *let $d_X \equiv 0$ ⟦Chapter2/DegeneracyIndex · MPolyfold.IsWithoutBoundary⟧, $f$ an sc-Fredholm section, the charts with tame base retractions, $f(x_0) = 0$, and $(f, x_0)$ in good position with the filler condition at the nearby zeros. Then there are an open $U \ni x_0$ and $d$ such that every zero in $U$ is in good position (in particular $f'(y)$ is onto) and a smooth point, and $S(f, U)$ is a sub-M-polyfold whose induced structure is equivalent to a $d$-dimensional manifold with boundary with corners and has degeneracy index $0$ everywhere.* *Proof.* Theorem 3.1.26 (preprint 3.23) gives everything but the last clause; for that, Lemma 2.4.6 (preprint 2.26), $d_S(y) \le d_X(y)$ ⟦Chapter2/SubMPolyfold · lemma_2_4_6⟧, and $d_X = 0$. So the interior case is the boundary case plus Lemma 2.4.6 (preprint 2.26); $d_X \equiv 0$ is used for nothing else. *Two readings and two omissions* (item 154), all resolved in the second addendum. (a) The text's hypothesis is "$f'(x_0)$ surjective"; good position adds condition (2) of Definition 3.6.6 (preprint 3.53), which the text calls an empty condition when $d_X(x) = 0$. Formally condition (2) is stated in the chart for the chart's partial quadrant, so this form takes good position as in the boundary case; that the condition is void at $d_X = 0$ in a tame chart, and Theorem 3.1.22 (preprint 3.19) with "$f'(x_0)$ surjective", are §III.45. The filler condition at the nearby zeros was the added hypothesis of Theorem 3.6.8 (1) (preprint 3.54); the form with the text's hypotheses is §III.43. (b) "The dimension agrees with the Fredholm index": this statement quantifies $d$ existentially; the proof takes $d = \dim\ker D(h + t)(0)$ (§III.34), and $\operatorname{ind} D(h + t)(0) = \dim P - \dim Q = \operatorname{ind} f'(x_0)$ holds for the conjugation data of the proof (§§III.18, III.30); the graph description with the conjugation exposed, and $\dim\ker = \operatorname{ind}$ for the surjective $D(h + t)(0)$, give the dimension as the index in §III.44.
